Clinical Psychology, Chapter 1

Chapter 1 Clinical Psychology: The Profession, Its History, Its Models and Its Ethics

In this chapter
  1. 1.1 What Clinical Psychology Is: define clinical psychology (APA Division 12, the RCI), tell it apart from psychiatry, counselling psychology, psychiatric social work and psychiatric nursing, and list what clinical psychologists do and where.
  2. 1.2 From Demons to Asylums: trace the early views of mental illness, from trephining and demonology through Hippocrates and Galen, the Middle Ages and the witch hunts, to the first asylums.
  3. 1.3 Reform, Moral Treatment and Modern Psychiatry: explain the reforms of Pinel, Tuke, Dix and Beers, moral treatment and the mental hygiene movement, and Kraepelin's classification.
  4. 1.4 The Rise of Clinical Psychology in the West: trace the profession from Witmer's clinic through the testing era, the World Wars and the Veterans Administration to the Boulder, Vail and clinical science training models.
  5. 1.5 Clinical Psychology in India: trace the profession in India from Girindrasekhar Bose to the RCI, explain how a clinical psychologist trains and registers today, and describe what working in India demands.
  6. 1.6 What Counts as Abnormal: compare the statistical, social-norm, maladaptiveness, personal-distress and ideal-mental-health criteria and the four Ds, and explain why no single criterion works.
  7. 1.7 Models of Psychopathology and Treatment: compare the supernatural, biological, psychodynamic, behavioural, cognitive, humanistic-existential, sociocultural and family-systems models, and explain the biopsychosocial and diathesis-stress models.
  8. 1.8 Ethics I: Principles, Competence, Consent and Confidentiality: state the principles behind professional ethics, and explain competence, informed consent, confidentiality, privilege and the duty to warn and protect.
  9. 1.9 Ethics II: Boundaries, Records, Assessment and New Settings: explain dual relationships and boundaries, record keeping, the ethics of testing, research and teletherapy, supervision, the RCI code, and how to reason through an ethical dilemma.
  10. 1.10 Mental Health Law and Policy in India: describe India's mental health laws from the Lunacy Act 1912 to the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, the disability and rehabilitation laws, and the national programmes, policy and survey.

Weightage. 52 of the 422 questions mapped in this book (12.3%) test this chapter, 30 of them from real past papers. 3 of its 10 sections are High yield.

In March 1896 a schoolteacher in Philadelphia brought one of her pupils to the psychology laboratory of the University of Pennsylvania. Her name was Margaret T. Maguire. She taught in one of the city's grammar schools and was also attending psychology courses at the university. The boy was fourteen years old, and the man she brought him to later hid his identity under the name "Charles Gilman". Charles sat in a class whose normal age was eleven. He had failed to win promotion three times and had, in the words of his examiner, "irretrievably lost three years in eight, more than one-third of his school life". His teachers described him as serious and industrious, and he was plainly not dull. Yet he could not spell, and he could barely read. In the language of the schools he was a "chronic bad speller", and nobody knew what to do with him. Miss Maguire had learned that psychology was the science of the mind, and she was, her examiner wrote, "imbued with the idea that a psychologist should be able, through examination, to ascertain the causes" of such a defect and to say how it might be overcome.

The psychologist was Lightner Witmer, twenty-eight years old, who had taken his doctorate under Wilhelm Wundt at Leipzig in 1892 and now ran the Pennsylvania laboratory. Witmer saw that psychology had never attempted what the teacher was asking. There was no textbook, no test and no method for finding out why one particular child could not learn, so he had to invent his approach as he went. He examined the boy himself. Charles's intelligence was average or better, his speech was good and his memory for sounds was good; he could recognise shapes, colours and single letters. What he could not do was hold a written word in his mind as a whole. Then Witmer noticed something odd. When Charles fixed his eyes steadily on a finger, the finger appeared double; letters doubled under his pen as he traced a word, and on the printed page as he read. Witmer sent him to an eye specialist, Dr Posey, who found that each eye on its own saw practically normally. The trouble was that the boy "lacked the power to direct the two eyes co-ordinately upon the same point in space". Glasses corrected the muscular weakness as far as was then possible.

Treatment took years. From the autumn of 1896 Charles came regularly to the laboratory for lessons in reading and spelling, and between these visits Miss Maguire taught him almost every day; once his vision had been steadied, the lessons concentrated on recognising whole words at sight. Progress was slow. He finished grammar school in June 1899, at seventeen, "having taken eleven years to accomplish the work of eight", and a few years later he was reading Dumas and Balzac and sent Witmer a well-phrased letter that "contained no mistakes in spelling". (He did not live long. He developed tuberculosis of the muscles and joints and died young, shortly before Witmer published his case.) Meanwhile, in December 1896, Witmer had stood before the American Psychological Association (APA) and outlined what he called "a scheme of practical work in psychology": the study of mental development in schoolchildren, a psychological clinic working alongside a hospital-style school for children whose difficulties held back their schooling, practical training for teachers, physicians and social workers, and the preparation of students for a new profession that he called the "psychological expert". The plan found little support among colleagues who thought of psychology as a laboratory science. But the clinic at Pennsylvania kept running, and it was the first psychological clinic in the world.

Eleven years later, on 15 March 1907, Witmer launched a journal of his own, The Psychological Clinic. Its opening article, by Witmer himself, was titled "Clinical psychology", and it is generally cited as the first appearance of the term in print. He explained that he had borrowed the word "clinical" from medicine "because it is the best term I can find to indicate the character of the method", and he added that "the term 'clinical' implies a method, and not a locality". In the very next issue he wrote up the case of Charles Gilman. Look at what he had done with one boy. He accepted a referral, examined a single individual with the methods of science, formed a hypothesis about the cause, called in a medical colleague, designed a programme of treatment, delivered it through a teacher, and followed the boy up for years. More than a century later, that is still the work of a clinical psychologist. Remember Charles Gilman, the chronic bad speller; you will return to him at the end of the chapter.

1.1 What Clinical Psychology IsMedium yield · 2 Qs

Tell a relative that you are going to study clinical psychology and you will probably be asked one of three questions. Will you become a doctor? Will you be able to give medicines? Is it the same thing as counselling? The answers are no, no, and not quite. A clinical psychologist is not a medical doctor and, in India, does not prescribe drugs; yet she works beside doctors in hospitals, diagnoses mental disorders, treats them, and is trusted by law to do so. She often counsels, but she is trained for problems far more severe than those most counsellors see. To explain what she is, this section first defines the field, then sets out who in India is legally allowed to use the title, then places her in the team of professions that care for people with mental illness, and finally describes what she actually does all week and where she does it.

Defining the field

Start with the man who named it. Lightner Witmer (1867 to 1956) founded the first psychological clinic at the University of Pennsylvania in 1896 and introduced the term clinical psychology in print in 1907, in the article that opened his journal The Psychological Clinic. For Witmer the essential idea was a way of working, not a place. "The term 'clinical'", he wrote, "implies a method, and not a locality". The word itself comes from the Greek for a bed, and in medicine "clinical" meant learning at the patient's bedside rather than from books. Witmer kept that spirit. The clinical psychologist, he said, "is interested primarily in the individual child", and the clinical method is needed wherever the state of an individual mind is found out by observation and experiment and then changed by some form of treatment. His clients were children, but he insisted that the method applied equally to adults, and to the gifted and the ordinary as much as to the struggling.

Modern definitions keep Witmer's two ingredients, science and the individual case, and widen everything else. The definition quoted in most textbooks comes from the Society of Clinical Psychology, which is Division 12 of the APA. According to it, the field of clinical psychology "integrates science, theory, and practice to understand, predict, and alleviate maladjustment, disability, and discomfort as well as to promote human adaptation, adjustment, and personal development", and it "focuses on the intellectual, emotional, biological, psychological, social, and behavioral aspects of human functioning across the life span, in varying cultures, and at all socioeconomic levels."

Read that definition slowly, because each phrase rules something in. First, it integrates science, theory and practice: clinical psychology is not a set of techniques but an applied science, and its practitioners are expected to use, evaluate and produce research. Second, its three verbs, understand, predict and alleviate, describe three jobs: explaining why a person has a problem, forecasting its course (Will this boy fall further behind? Is this patient likely to harm herself?), and relieving it. Third, its targets are broad. "Maladjustment, disability and discomfort" cover everything from a diagnosable disorder to a learning difficulty to plain unhappiness, and the clause about promoting "adaptation, adjustment and personal development" means that the field is not limited to illness. Finally, the closing phrase sets its breadth: all of human functioning, from the intellectual to the biological to the social, at every age, in every culture and at every income level. (In March 2025 the Society published an updated statement defining and describing the field; the wording quoted above is the one that textbooks and examiners use.) The APA's own description of clinical psychology as a specialty says the same thing in a phrase worth remembering: it is "a specialty in breadth", providing "continuing and comprehensive mental and behavioral health care" rather than help with one narrow kind of problem.

In India the profession is regulated by the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI), and its training guidelines describe the field in the same spirit. Clinical psychologists, the RCI writes, "apply knowledge and methods from all substantive fields of biopsychosocial sciences for promotion and maintenance of mental health of individuals", using techniques from several branches of psychology in the "promotion of mental health, and in prevention, diagnosis, treatment and rehabilitation of mental and physical disorders" where "psychological factors play a major role". Notice two things in the RCI's wording. It includes physical disorders, because psychological factors matter in heart disease, cancer and chronic pain (Chapter 9). And it includes rehabilitation, which reflects the fact that in India the profession is regulated by a council for disability and rehabilitation. The RCI states the aim of training just as plainly: to prepare a clinical psychologist "to function as an independent clinician, competent to deliver mental, physical and rehabilitative healthcare services".

One more distinction saves confusion. Strictly speaking, psychopathology is the scientific study of psychological disorders, and abnormal psychology is the name of the academic subject that studies them: what they are, what causes them and how they develop. Clinical psychology is a profession. It draws on abnormal psychology, but also on learning, personality, development, social psychology, physiology and research methods, and applies all of them to the assessment and treatment of real people. An experimental psychologist may spend a career studying the causes of depression without ever assessing or treating a depressed person; a clinical psychologist does both. That is why the subjects of this book's sister volumes (General Psychology, Assessment, Psychiatric Disorders and the rest) are all part of a clinical psychologist's training.

Remember

A working definition you can say aloud in an interview: clinical psychology is the branch of psychology that applies psychological science to the assessment, understanding, treatment and prevention of mental disorders, disability and distress, and to the promotion of well-being, in individuals, families and groups across the lifespan. Its two defining features, since Witmer, are the scientific method and the individual case.

Exam trap

"Who founded the first psychological clinic?", "Who coined the term clinical psychology?" and "Who first used the term clinical psychology in print?" all have the same answer: Lightner Witmer (clinic 1896; term in print 1907, in the first issue of The Psychological Clinic). Some accounts say he first used the phrase in his December 1896 address, but the printed source that histories cite is the 1907 article, so the answer to "first in print" is 1907. The distractors are chosen because each is a real "first". Wilhelm Wundt founded the first psychology laboratory (Leipzig, 1879) and supervised Witmer's doctorate; G. Stanley Hall founded the APA in 1892 and was its first president; James McKeen Cattell, Witmer's earlier teacher, coined the term "mental test" in 1890. Freud founded psychoanalysis, Rogers client-centred therapy, Skinner operant behaviourism and Erikson the psychosocial stages; none of them named clinical psychology.

Who may be called a clinical psychologist in India

In most countries the title "psychologist" is protected by law, and you cannot use it until a state body has checked your training and entered your name on a register. In the United States this is done by state licensing boards (Section 1.4). In India the title that the law protects is clinical psychologist, and the body that protects it is the Rehabilitation Council of India, a statutory body under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. The RCI was set up as a registered society in 1986 and became a statutory body under the Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1992. It approves training programmes, sets their syllabus, and keeps the Central Rehabilitation Register (CRR), the official list of registered rehabilitation professionals; clinical psychologists are one category on that register. Under the RCI Act, a person who is not on the register may not practise as a clinical psychologist, and doing so is an offence. Section 1.5 tells the history of the RCI and the rules of registration in full; for now the point is simple. In India "clinical psychologist" is a legal title, not a description anybody may adopt.

What qualifies a person to be registered? For several decades the standard route was a master's degree in psychology followed by a two-year, full-time M.Phil. in Clinical Psychology with supervised clinical training at an RCI-approved centre. After the National Education Policy 2020, the University Grants Commission discontinued M.Phil. degrees, and the RCI replaced the M.Phil. with a two-year, RCI-regulated M.A. in Clinical Psychology, effective from the academic session 2025 to 2026. Its minimum entry requirement is a four-year B.A. or B.Sc. in psychology with at least 55 percent marks (50 percent for SC and ST candidates); graduates of three-year programmes must first complete a bridge course or the first year of a master's in psychology. The RCI's 2025 guidelines note that clinical psychology training is now offered at more than seventy recognised centres across the country. Section 1.5 compares the old and new routes and the shorter professional diploma.

The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 writes this professional identity into mental health law. Section 2(1)(g) defines a "clinical psychologist" as a person who has a recognised qualification in clinical psychology from an institution approved and recognised by the RCI, or who has a postgraduate degree in psychology, clinical psychology or applied psychology together with an M.Phil. in clinical psychology or in medical and social psychology, obtained after a two-year full-time course that includes supervised clinical training. The Act also requires clinical psychologists, psychiatric social workers and mental health nurses to be registered with the State Mental Health Authority to work as mental health professionals under it (section 55), and the Central Mental Health Authority keeps a national register of them (section 43). Section 1.10 places these provisions within the Act as a whole.

Exam trap

The RCI dates come as a pair: a registered society in 1986, a statutory body under the RCI Act, 1992. Options of 1987 (the Mental Health Act), 2016 (the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act) and 2017 (the Mental Healthcare Act) are there to catch you. The RCI comes under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, not the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare and not the Ministry of Education, because it was created to regulate disability and rehabilitation professionals. And the Act that makes RCI registration compulsory for rehabilitation professionals, clinical psychologists among them, is the RCI Act, 1992, not either mental health act.

The mental health team

Clinical psychology is one of four professions that between them have traditionally provided specialist mental health care. Barlow and Durand's textbook names them as clinical and counselling psychologists, psychiatrists, psychiatric social workers and psychiatric nurses (adding marriage and family therapists and mental health counsellors, who in the United States train for a shorter time), and in India the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 uses almost the same list when it defines a mental health professional: a psychiatrist; a clinical psychologist, psychiatric social worker or mental health nurse registered with the State Authority; or a professional with a postgraduate degree in the psychiatric specialty of Ayurveda, Homoeopathy, Unani or Siddha. Each profession has its own training and its own centre of gravity, summarised in Table 1.1, and the differences are a favourite of examiners and interviewers alike.

Psychiatry. A psychiatrist is a medical doctor. In India the route is the MBBS degree followed by postgraduate training in psychiatry (the MD, the DNB or, formerly, the two-year Diploma in Psychological Medicine), and the psychiatrist is registered with a medical council. The Mental Healthcare Act defines a psychiatrist as a medical practitioner with a recognised postgraduate degree or diploma in psychiatry. Because psychiatrists are physicians, they can do several things that a clinical psychologist cannot: prescribe medicines, order and interpret blood tests and brain scans, give electroconvulsive therapy, and take medical responsibility for admission to hospital. Psychiatrists also diagnose and many practise psychotherapy, so the two professions overlap a great deal. The real difference lies in training. The psychiatrist spends years learning medicine and then specialises; the clinical psychologist spends years learning psychology, including research methods, psychological testing and the full range of psychological therapies, and then specialises. Neither training is a lesser version of the other. A useful one-line contrast is that the psychiatrist brings the body to the team and the psychologist brings the science of behaviour, though a good member of either profession knows a lot about both.

Two further titles are often confused with these. A psychoanalyst is anyone, psychiatrist, psychologist or otherwise, who has completed training at a psychoanalytic institute (Chapter 3). A psychotherapist is anyone who practises psychotherapy; the word describes what a person does rather than a qualification. And in most of the world, India included, psychologists do not prescribe medicines. A small number of American states have, since 2002, allowed specially trained psychologists to prescribe, after a long and bitter debate within the profession that Section 1.4 describes.

Psychiatric social work. A psychiatric social worker (PSW) is a social worker who has specialised in mental health. The Mental Healthcare Act defines a PSW as a person with a postgraduate degree in social work and an M.Phil. in Psychiatric Social Work, obtained after a two-year full-time course with supervised clinical training; institutes such as the National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS) in Bengaluru and the Central Institute of Psychiatry (CIP) in Ranchi have long run this training alongside their clinical psychology programmes. Where the psychologist looks mainly at the person, the PSW looks mainly at the person's world. She takes the detailed social and family history, assesses how the family is coping and how much burden it carries, works with relatives, visits the home, connects the patient with welfare schemes, disability benefits, legal aid, housing and jobs, plans discharge from hospital, and leads much of the rehabilitation work in the community. Social work has three classic methods, and you should know their names: social casework (helping one individual or family with their problems in living), social group work (using a small group and its relationships to help its members grow and change) and community organisation (helping a whole community identify and meet its own needs). In psychiatric settings all three are used, from casework with one family to self-help groups for caregivers to awareness work in a village.

Exam trap

Papers for clinical and psychiatric social work programmes sometimes ask about the principles of social group work set out by Harleigh B. Trecker in Social Group Work: Principles and Practices. The ten usually listed are planned group formation, specific objectives, a purposeful worker-group relationship, continuous individualisation, guided group interaction, democratic group self-determination, flexible functional organisation, progressive programme experiences, resource utilisation, and evaluation. A distractor such as "the principle of focusing on change and possibilities" sounds plausible but is not one of them.

Psychiatric nursing. A psychiatric nurse, called a mental health nurse in the Mental Healthcare Act, is a nurse with a diploma or degree in general nursing or in psychiatric nursing recognised by the Nursing Council of India and registered with the State nursing council; many go on to a postgraduate degree in psychiatric (mental health) nursing. Psychiatric nurses are the members of the team who are with the patient most of the time, especially in hospital. They observe and record behaviour, sleep and mood around the clock, give medicines and watch for side effects, prepare patients for and care for them after electroconvulsive therapy, manage the ward as a therapeutic environment, de-escalate aggression, teach patients and families about the illness and its treatment, and increasingly follow patients up at home and in community programmes.

Table 1.1 The core mental health professions in India.
ProfessionTypical training in IndiaRegistered withPrescribes?Distinctive contribution
Clinical psychologistPsychology degree, then two-year RCI-approved M.Phil. (now M.A.) in Clinical PsychologyRCI (Central Rehabilitation Register); also the State Mental Health AuthorityNoPsychological assessment and testing, psychotherapy, research, rehabilitation
PsychiatristMBBS, then MD, DNB or DPM in psychiatryMedical councilYesMedical diagnosis, medication, ECT, physical investigation, admission
Psychiatric social workerMaster's in social work, then M.Phil. in Psychiatric Social WorkState Mental Health Authority (under the 2017 Act)NoFamily and social assessment, casework, welfare and community links, rehabilitation
Psychiatric (mental health) nurseNursing diploma or degree, often with psychiatric nursingState nursing councilNoRound-the-clock care, observation, medication, ward milieu, psychoeducation
Counsellor or counselling psychologistDegree or diploma in psychology or counselling (varies widely)No statutory register for general counsellorsNoAdjustment, career, educational and relationship problems; growth and prevention

Other professionals often complete the team: occupational therapists, who help patients rebuild the skills of daily living and work; special educators, who teach children with intellectual and learning disabilities; counsellors; and, increasingly, peer support workers who have recovered from mental illness themselves. When these people work together on the same patients, sharing information and planning jointly, they form a multidisciplinary team. Its central meeting is the case conference, at which each member presents what she has found and the team agrees on a single plan. Figure 1.1 shows the arrangement.

Push a case through the team and the division of labour becomes concrete. Prakash, a 24-year-old engineering student, is brought by his parents to the psychiatry outpatient department of a medical college. For two months he has stopped attending classes, sleeps little, and is convinced that his neighbours can hear his thoughts. The psychiatrist takes the history, examines him, orders blood tests and a urine drug screen to rule out a medical or substance-induced cause, makes a provisional diagnosis of a psychotic disorder, starts an antipsychotic medicine and decides that a short admission is needed. On the ward the psychiatric nurse watches his sleep, eating and behaviour, gives his medicines, notices on the third day the restlessness that is a common side effect, and reports it so that the dose can be adjusted. The psychiatric social worker meets the parents. She learns that the family first took Prakash to a temple healer, that the father blames the son's "laziness" and shouts at him, that money is short because the mother has stopped work to watch over him, and that the college may cancel his registration; she starts work on all four. The clinical psychologist carries out a structured psychological assessment, rates the severity of his symptoms so that change can be measured, later assesses his attention and memory, builds a formulation of how his difficulties developed and what keeps them going, and, once he is calmer, begins psychoeducation and cognitive behavioural work on his beliefs and on early warning signs of relapse, with the PSW running sessions for the family. At the weekly case conference the four accounts are put together into one plan. The therapies themselves appear later in this book (cognitive behavioural therapy for psychosis in Section 5.6, family work and expressed emotion in Section 7.2, rehabilitation in Section 9.8), and the idea that biological, psychological and social causes must all be addressed is the biopsychosocial model of Section 1.7.

Exam trap

Keep the four roles apart by their one defining feature. Only the psychiatrist is a medical doctor who can prescribe; the clinical psychologist's distinctive tools are psychological assessment, the psychological therapies and research; the psychiatric social worker works mainly with the family and the social world (social history, casework, rehabilitation, welfare); the psychiatric nurse provides continuous care and observation. "Both psychiatrists and clinical psychologists diagnose and both do psychotherapy" is true; "clinical psychologists in India may prescribe after RCI registration" is false.

Clinical psychology and counselling psychology

The closest neighbour of clinical psychology is counselling psychology, the branch of applied psychology that helps people with the problems of everyday living and development: adjustment to new circumstances, educational and career decisions, relationship difficulties, stress, and personal growth. Its professional home in the United States is Division 17 of the APA, the Society of Counseling Psychology. The two fields share most of their methods, and in practice they overlap heavily, so the differences between them are differences of degree. As Barlow and Durand put it, counselling psychologists "tend to study and treat adjustment and vocational issues encountered by relatively healthy individuals", whereas clinical psychologists "usually concentrate on more severe psychological disorders".

The difference makes more sense once you know that the two fields have different family trees. Clinical psychology grew out of Witmer's clinic, the mental testing movement, the psychiatric hospital and, after the Second World War, the American Veterans Administration (Section 1.4). Its centre of gravity has always been psychopathology: diagnosing it, measuring it and treating it, often in people with severe and chronic disorders. Counselling psychology grew out of the vocational guidance movement started by Frank Parsons in Boston in 1908 and out of the counselling revolution led by Carl Rogers in the 1940s (Section 6.10 tells that story). Its centre of gravity is the basically healthy person facing a difficult stage of life, and its emphasis falls on strengths, development, prevention, education and work. Clinical psychologists are found more often in hospitals and psychiatric clinics; counselling psychologists more often in university counselling centres, schools, career services and community agencies. Table 1.2 sets the traditional contrasts side by side.

Table 1.2 Clinical and counselling psychology: the traditional contrasts (all of them differences of emphasis, not absolute rules).
FeatureClinical psychologyCounselling psychology
APA divisionDivision 12, Society of Clinical PsychologyDivision 17, Society of Counseling Psychology
Historical rootsWitmer's clinic (1896), mental testing, psychiatric hospitals, the Veterans AdministrationVocational guidance (Parsons, 1908), Rogers's counselling, student counselling
Typical clientPerson with a mental disorder, often severe or chronicRelatively healthy person facing a problem of living
Typical problemsPsychosis, severe depression, anxiety disorders, personality disorders, neuropsychological and developmental disordersAdjustment, career choice, study, relationships, stress, life transitions
EmphasisPsychopathology, diagnosis, assessment, treatmentStrengths, development, prevention, education and work
Typical settingsHospitals, psychiatric clinics, rehabilitation centres, private practiceColleges, schools, career centres, community agencies, private practice
Status in IndiaRegulated title: RCI registration requiredNo statutory register for counsellors

Do not overstate the contrasts. Many counselling psychologists treat diagnosable disorders, and many clinical psychologists spend their days helping students with exam anxiety or couples who argue. Over the past half-century the two fields have moved steadily closer in training, methods and settings. In India the practical difference is largely legal: a clinical psychologist must hold an RCI-recognised qualification and be on the Central Rehabilitation Register, whereas a counsellor working in a school or a college has no comparable statutory register. Two other neighbours are tested in the same breath. Health psychology (APA Division 38) applies psychology to physical health and illness, and behavioural medicine is the interdisciplinary field that integrates behavioural and biomedical knowledge; Section 9.1 explains how they differ. And the difference between counselling and psychotherapy, as activities rather than professions, is taught in Section 6.10.

Exam trap

Match the division numbers: Division 12 is clinical psychology, Division 17 is counselling psychology, Division 38 is health psychology. When an option says that counselling psychology deals with "normal-range" problems, development and career while clinical psychology deals with more severe psychopathology, that is the expected answer; when an option says the two use entirely different methods, or that counselling psychologists never see people with disorders, it is wrong.

What clinical psychologists do

Ask a group of clinical psychologists what they did last week and you will get a surprisingly long list. The RCI lists the functions that a newly trained clinical psychologist must be able to perform: to diagnose and assess mental health problems; to understand child, adolescent, adult and old-age problems within a psychological framework and treat them; to apply psychological principles in rehabilitating people with mental health problems and disabilities; to work with the psychological side of physical diseases; to work with communities to promote health and well-being; to do research; to teach and train; to take on administrative and supervisory responsibilities; and to give expert testimony in a court of law. Those functions fall into the activities described below and summarised in Table 1.3.

Assessment. In psychological assessment the psychologist gathers and integrates information about a person in order to answer a clinical question: Why is this child failing at school? How severe is this woman's depression? Has this man's head injury damaged his memory? Is this person fit to stand trial? The psychologist uses interviews, observation, the mental status examination (Chapter 2) and standardised psychological tests of intelligence, personality, neuropsychological functioning and symptoms (taught in the Assessment book). Assessment was the first job of clinical psychologists, and testing is the one skill that has belonged to them more than to any other profession.

Diagnosis and formulation. A diagnosis places a person's problems in a category of a classification system such as the ICD-11 or the DSM-5-TR. It is a label that summarises, communicates and guides the choice of treatment, but it does not explain why this particular person has this particular problem now. That is the work of a formulation, an individual hypothesis about the causes, triggers and maintaining factors of a person's difficulties. Clinical psychologists do both, and the difference between them is taught in Section 2.9.

Psychotherapy and other interventions. By psychotherapy we mean the treatment of psychological problems by psychological means, chiefly a structured professional relationship in which talking, learning and new experience bring about change. Most of this book (Chapters 3 to 8) is about its varieties: psychodynamic, behavioural, cognitive, humanistic and family and group therapies. But a clinical psychologist's interventions go beyond the therapy hour: cognitive retraining after brain injury, behaviour programmes for children with intellectual disability, psychoeducation for families, skills training for people with schizophrenia, and stress management for patients with heart disease.

Research. The definition you met earlier makes science part of the job, and clinical psychology is unusual among health professions in training its practitioners to do research. Even a clinician who never publishes is expected to act as what Barlow and Durand call a scientist-practitioner in two smaller senses: as a consumer of research, who uses the treatments that the evidence supports, and as an evaluator, who measures whether her own work is helping her own clients. Some go further and become creators of research. How this ideal became the official model of training, at the Boulder conference of 1949, is told in Section 1.4.

Teaching, training and supervision. Clinical psychologists teach psychology students and also medical, nursing and social work students; they run workshops for teachers, police and health workers; and they supervise trainees, whose clinical work they oversee and for which they share responsibility (Section 8.12).

Consultation. In consultation the psychologist helps another professional or an organisation do its own work better, rather than treating the client directly: advising a teacher on how to manage a disruptive pupil, helping a cancer ward handle a patient who refuses treatment, or helping an NGO design a programme. In general hospitals this takes the form of consultation-liaison work, in which a psychologist is called to the medical and surgical wards to assess and help patients whose physical illness has psychological causes or consequences. Gerald Caplan's types of mental health consultation are taught in Section 9.6.

Administration, prevention and expert work. Senior psychologists head departments and services, plan programmes and manage budgets and staff. Many work in prevention and community mental health: screening children in schools, running awareness campaigns, and training lay counsellors (Section 9.6). And some appear in court as an expert witness, a professional whose specialised knowledge allows her to give an opinion that the court needs, for example on a person's mental capacity, a child's best interests in a custody dispute or an accused person's mental state (Chapter 10).

Table 1.3 The main activities of a clinical psychologist, with an example of each from one week in a medical college department.
ActivityWhat it involvesAn example from the week
AssessmentInterview, observation, mental status examination, standardised testsTesting Aditi, aged 11, whose school reports that she cannot read at her grade level
Diagnosis and formulationClassifying the problem; explaining how it arose and what maintains itConcluding that Hema's chest pain is panic, and why it keeps returning
Psychotherapy and interventionIndividual, family and group therapy; rehabilitation; psychoeducationCognitive behavioural therapy for Hema; family sessions for Prakash
ResearchUsing, evaluating and producing evidenceRating Prakash's symptoms each week to see whether treatment is working
Teaching and supervisionTraining students and overseeing trainees' casesA lecture to MBBS students; supervising two clinical psychology trainees
ConsultationHelping other professionals and agencies with their workAdvising the cardiology team on patients with repeated normal tests
AdministrationRunning the department and its servicesPreparing the department's monthly case statistics
Prevention and community workScreening, awareness, training lay workersA talk on examination stress at a government school
Expert workReports and testimony for courts and authoritiesA report on Aditi's learning difficulty for her disability certificate

Follow the week in Table 1.3 and you see how the activities blend into one another. On Monday, Aditi, an 11-year-old girl, is brought by her mother because the school says she cannot read at her grade level, although she answers well in class and is good at arithmetic. Like Witmer with Charles Gilman, the psychologist's first question is whether the cause lies in something simple, so she asks about Aditi's vision and hearing and arranges for them to be checked, before going on to tests of intelligence, reading, spelling and writing. If the results point to a specific learning disability, the department will prepare the report needed for her disability certificate (the certification procedure is described in Section 9.9) and meet her teachers to plan help in class. On Tuesday the cardiology department refers Hema, a 42-year-old schoolteacher, who has been to the emergency room three times with chest pain, breathlessness and a terror that she is dying, and whose heart tests have each time been normal. The psychologist's assessment shows that these are panic attacks, and she begins a brief course of cognitive behavioural therapy (Section 5.6); she also suggests to the cardiologists a simple way of spotting such patients early, which is consultation. Wednesday is the family session for Prakash, and Thursday is for teaching and supervision. On Friday the department meets to review its outcome data, because it has been rating every patient's symptoms at the start and end of treatment. On Saturday morning one psychologist gives a talk on examination stress at a government school.

How is the time divided? The best-known evidence comes from surveys of the members of APA Division 12, repeated since 1960 and in recent decades conducted by John C. Norcross and his colleagues. They show psychotherapy taking the largest single share of the working week, with assessment next, and research, writing, teaching, supervision and administration sharing the rest. Over the decades the share of time spent on psychological testing has fallen, projective testing most of all, and most clinical psychologists now combine several roles, many of them holding more than one position at once. No equivalent national survey of Indian clinical psychologists is as well known, but in Indian hospitals assessment, psychotherapy, teaching and supervision of trainees usually dominate the week.

Exam trap

A diagnosis classifies the problem using a system such as ICD-11 or DSM-5-TR; a formulation explains it, as an individual hypothesis about causes, triggers and maintaining factors (Section 2.9). In surveys of American clinical psychologists the single biggest share of time goes to psychotherapy, not testing, and the share given to assessment has been falling. And remember Witmer's point that "clinical" refers to a method, not a place: a clinical psychologist working in a school, a court or a village is still doing clinical psychology.

Where clinical psychologists work

Witmer's clinic served schoolchildren, and he wrote that "the school room, the juvenile court, and the streets are a larger laboratory of psychology". His successors have spread into all three, and much further. In the United States the largest single group of clinical psychologists works in private practice, and most of the rest work in hospitals and medical centres, universities and medical schools, community mental health centres, and government services such as those for military veterans.

In India the profession grew up inside the large mental health institutes, and they remain its training grounds: NIMHANS in Bengaluru, the Central Institute of Psychiatry in Ranchi, the Ranchi Institute of Neuro-Psychiatry and Allied Sciences (RINPAS), the Institute of Human Behaviour and Allied Sciences (IHBAS) in Delhi, and the Lokopriya Gopinath Bordoloi Regional Institute of Mental Health in Tezpur, among others. The RCI notes that training has been shifting from these traditional mental hospitals to universities, medical colleges and NGOs, and its guidelines expect trainees to work alongside psychiatry, neurology, neurosurgery, paediatrics, cardiology and oncology. The main settings today are:

  • Mental hospitals, institutes and departments of psychiatry or clinical psychology in medical colleges, where clinical psychologists assess and treat outpatients and inpatients, teach, and supervise trainees.
  • General hospitals, in consultation-liaison work with paediatrics, neurology, oncology, cardiology, pain clinics and palliative care (Chapter 9).
  • Private practice, alone or in group clinics, which has grown fast in India's cities, along with online practice.
  • Schools, colleges and universities, in student counselling centres, special schools, and assessment of learning and developmental difficulties.
  • Courts, prisons, juvenile justice and child protection settings, in assessments for courts and in the rehabilitation of offenders (Chapter 10).
  • Rehabilitation centres and de-addiction centres, for people with intellectual disability, brain injury, severe mental illness and substance use problems (Sections 9.8 and 9.9).
  • Non-governmental organisations and community programmes, including the district mental health programmes of the national mental health programme (Section 1.10) and projects that train lay counsellors (Section 9.6).
  • Industry, defence and other organisations, in employee assistance and well-being programmes, and in military and paramilitary settings.
  • Telehealth, from private online therapy to national helplines such as Tele-MANAS (Section 1.10); the ethics of working at a distance are covered in Section 1.9.

As the settings multiplied, clinical psychology grew specialties of its own, each with its own skills and often its own professional body. The specialty of clinical neuropsychology (APA Division 40) assesses and rehabilitates people with brain injury and disease. Practitioners of forensic psychology apply psychology to the law and the courts. Clinical child and adolescent psychology works with the young, and geropsychology with older adults. Health psychology, community psychology and rehabilitation psychology complete the family. Figure 1.2 shows these neighbours and where in this series each is taught.

Clinical link

Interview panels for clinical psychology programmes very often open with "Why clinical psychology and not psychiatry or counselling?" A strong answer uses the ideas of this section rather than a personal story alone: that clinical psychology is an applied science combining assessment, psychological treatment and research; that it works across the whole range of severity, including severe mental illness, and across the lifespan; that it is a member of the multidisciplinary team with skills (testing, formulation, the psychological therapies, outcome measurement) that the other professions do not provide in the same depth; and that in India it is a regulated profession with an RCI register, which means accountability to clients. Then say which setting or population draws you, and why.

Scan Your Brain
