1.2 From Demons to AsylumsLower yield · not asked yet

Why should a clinical psychologist in a hospital in Bengaluru or Delhi care what a Greek physician thought two and a half thousand years ago, or what happened in a London madhouse in 1814? There are two good reasons. The first is that the question "what causes madness?" has only ever received three kinds of answer, and they keep coming back. One answer looks outside the natural world, to gods, demons, spirits, stars or sin: the supernatural tradition. A second looks to the body, to humours, brains, germs and genes: the biological tradition. A third looks to the mind and to the person's life, to passions, habits, families, learning and society: the psychological tradition. Each answer brings its own treatment, and the history of mental health care is largely the story of these three answers rising, falling and rising again. Section 1.7 turns them into the formal models of psychopathology that you will use in practice; this section and the next tell the story behind them.

The second reason is closer to home. In India all three traditions are alive in the same week of the same patient's life. A young man who begins to hear voices may first be taken to a temple or a dargah, then to a local healer, and only months later to a psychiatrist and a psychologist. To work with him and his family you need to understand where each of those explanations came from, and why each has been believed. Figure 1.3 maps the whole journey that this section and Section 1.3 will travel, from holes drilled in Stone Age skulls to Emil Kraepelin's classification of the psychoses.

Holes in the skull: trephining and the supernatural tradition

Archaeologists digging up Stone Age burial grounds in Europe, South America and Asia keep finding skulls with neat holes in them. The holes are not wounds. They are round or square openings, a few centimetres across, cut or scraped through the bone with stone tools. More than 1,500 such skulls from the Neolithic period have been found, between 5 and 10 percent of all skulls from that era; at one burial site in France dated to about 6500 BCE, 40 of the 120 skulls had been opened. India has its own example: a child's skull from the Harappan site of Lothal in Gujarat, dated to about 2200 BCE, shows the same operation. Most remarkable of all, the edges of many of the holes are smooth and rounded, which means that new bone grew after the operation. The patients survived, often for years.

This operation is called trephining (also trepanation): cutting a hole in a living person's skull. It is probably the oldest surgical procedure for which we have physical evidence, and it is the usual starting point of any history of abnormal psychology. Why was it done? Here you must be careful, because nobody left a written record. In the nineteenth century the French surgeon and anthropologist Paul Broca (1824 to 1880), who studied trephined skulls from Peru and from Neolithic France, suggested a ritual purpose, and the textbook explanation that grew from this is that a hole was opened to let out an evil spirit that was causing strange behaviour, seizures or severe headaches. That is a reasonable guess, but it is only a guess. Many trephined skulls also show fractures, so in some cases the surgeon was probably lifting out bone fragments or releasing blood after a blow to the head. The Inca of Peru, who trephined far more often than Neolithic Europeans, used it for head wounds and for epilepsy. The honest conclusion is that trephining may have been, among other things, the first treatment of mental disorder, and that its purpose has been inferred, not recorded.

What is certain is that for most of recorded history, in most cultures, behaviour that could not otherwise be explained was blamed on beings from outside the natural world. In the Persian empire of about 900 to 600 BCE all illness, physical and mental, was considered the work of the devil. The belief that abnormal behaviour is caused by possession by evil spirits, demons or gods is called demonology, and the treatment that follows from it is exorcism: a ritual to drive the spirit out of the body. Exorcism took many forms. A priest or shaman might pray over the sufferer, chant, make loud noises, burn foul-smelling substances, give bitter potions, starve or beat the body to make it an unpleasant home for the spirit, or bargain with the spirit to leave. Notice the logic. If the cause is a spirit, the treatment must act on the spirit, and the person's own suffering is beside the point.

Demonology is not a museum piece. Many Indian families still speak of bhoot-pret (ghosts and spirits), the evil eye, black magic or the anger of a deity when a relative becomes disturbed, and healers at shrines across the country still perform rituals to free the possessed. Section 1.7 examines the supernatural model alongside the scientific ones, and possession states as clinical phenomena belong to the Disorders book. For now, remember the pattern: an explanation from outside nature, a treatment aimed at the outside agent, and a sufferer who is acted upon rather than cared for.

Exam trap

Trephining is prehistoric (Stone Age), not Greek or medieval, and its purpose (releasing an evil spirit) is an inference from skulls, not a recorded fact. Keep the two supernatural terms apart: demonology is the belief that spirits cause abnormal behaviour; exorcism is the treatment. When a question about the diathesis-stress model offers "supernatural influences" as an option, it is the one option that belongs to no modern scientific model at all.

Hippocrates and the natural view: Greece and Rome

The first great break with the supernatural came in classical Greece. Hippocrates (about 460 to 377 BCE), whom we still call the father of Western medicine, and his followers left a collection of writings, the Hippocratic Corpus, composed between about 450 and 350 BCE. Its most famous essay for our purposes, On the Sacred Disease, is about epilepsy, which the Greeks called sacred because they thought the gods caused it. The Hippocratic author will have none of this. Epilepsy, he says, is no more divine than any other disease; it has a natural cause, and people call it sacred only because they do not understand it. The same essay places the mind firmly in the head: it is from the brain, and from the brain only, that our pleasures, joys, laughter and jests arise, as well as our sorrows, pains, griefs and tears.

This is the naturalistic view of mental disorder: abnormal behaviour has natural causes in the body and the environment, and it can be studied and treated like any other illness. Hippocrates put it to work. He sorted mental disorders into three broad groups: mania (excited, overactive states), melancholia (sadness and despondency) and phrenitis (brain fever, an acute confusional state with fever). He thought that disorders could come from brain disease, head injury and heredity, which are remarkably modern ideas, and he also recognised that family stress could contribute; on occasion he removed patients from their families. His treatments were gentle by the standards of what came later: rest, a regulated diet, exercise, abstinence from alcohol and sex, and, when the body seemed overloaded, bleeding or induced vomiting.

The theory behind those treatments is the most influential idea in the history of medicine before the germ theory. The humoral theory held that health depends on a balance of four bodily fluids, or humours: blood, yellow bile, black bile and phlegm. Each humour was linked with two of the four basic qualities of Greek physics (hot, cold, moist and dry) and with an organ that produced it. Disease, physical or mental, came from an excess or a lack of one humour. Too much black bile, for example, flooded the brain and caused melancholia; the word itself is Greek for "black bile" (melaina chole), and our "melancholy" carries it still. Because each humour was tied to a quality, the doctor could restore balance by changing the patient's surroundings (heat, cold, dryness, moisture) or by draining the excess directly, through bleeding, often with leeches, and through purging and vomiting. Figure 1.4 and Table 1.4 lay the scheme out. You might call humoral theory the first chemical-imbalance theory of mental illness, and you would be right to notice how familiar that phrase still sounds.

Table 1.4 The four humours and what was believed about them.
HumourMade inQualitiesTemperament (Galen)An excess was blamed for
BloodHeartHot and moistSanguine: ruddy, cheerful, optimisticInsomnia, delirium, overexcitement
Yellow bile (choler)LiverHot and dryCholeric: hot-temperedIrritability, rage, mania
Black bileSpleenCold and dryMelancholic: sad, broodingMelancholia (depression)
PhlegmBrainCold and moistPhlegmatic: sluggish, apathetic, calmDullness, lethargy

One more Hippocratic idea has had a very long and unhappy life. Egyptian physicians had noticed that some women developed paralysis, blindness, fits or pains for which no bodily cause could be found, and they had a theory: the womb, empty and restless, wandered about the body, producing a symptom wherever it lodged. The Greeks took the idea over, and the word hysteria comes from hystera, the Greek for uterus. The term is traditionally credited to Hippocrates, although historians point out that the Hippocratic texts speak of diseases of the womb rather than of one condition called hysteria. The prescribed cures followed from the theory: marriage and pregnancy, or fumigating the vagina with sweet smells to lure the womb back into place. Anatomy eventually showed that the womb cannot wander, but the habit of calling dramatic women "hysterical" survived into the 1970s. Today these presentations are classified among the somatic symptom and conversion disorders, which occur in both sexes (the Disorders book teaches them; Section 3.1 tells how hysteria led Freud to psychoanalysis).

Two Greek philosophers belong in this story too. Plato (about 427 to 347 BCE) distinguished two kinds of madness: a divine madness, the inspiration of prophets, poets and lovers, and a madness that comes from disease. He believed that maladaptive behaviour arose when the passions and appetites overpowered reason, often because of a bad upbringing or a bad social environment, and that the cure was re-education through rational discussion, so that reason could rule again. That is a recognisable ancestor of modern talking therapy. He also argued, in the Laws, that the mentally ill should be cared for at home by their relatives rather than punished. His pupil Aristotle (384 to 322 BCE) also stressed the social environment and early learning, and accepted the humoral theory; a work attributed to him, the Problems, famously asks why men who are outstanding in philosophy, politics, poetry or the arts so often seem to be melancholic. Meanwhile the temples of Asclepius, the Greek god of healing, received the chronically ill, including the mentally disturbed, and offered them rest, sleep, massage, soothing music and a pleasant setting: a hint of the humane institutions that would not appear again on any scale for two thousand years.

The Greek physicians' ideas passed to Rome and were made into a system by Galen (about 129 to 216 CE; some textbooks give his death as 198), a Greek physician who practised in Rome and served as doctor to emperors. Galen accepted the brain as the seat of the mind, studied the anatomy of the nervous system by dissecting animals, and expanded the humours into a theory of personality: the four temperaments, sanguine, choleric, melancholic and phlegmatic, each produced by a predominance of one humour (Table 1.4). His authority was so great that the Hippocratic-Galenic system dominated Western and Islamic medicine for well over a thousand years, into the nineteenth century. As late as 1621 the English scholar Robert Burton, in his vast Anatomy of Melancholy, was still recommending purges and induced vomiting for depression. (The temperaments as a theory of personality belong to the Personality book.)

Exam trap

Hippocrates: naturalistic view, the brain as the seat of the mind, epilepsy not sacred, the four humours, and the classification into mania, melancholia and phrenitis. Galen: systematised the humours and gave us the four temperaments. Do not give the temperaments to Hippocrates or the humours to Galen if both names are offered. The word hysteria (wandering womb) is Greek, credited to Hippocrates, with the idea borrowed from Egypt; it is not Charcot's, Freud's or Janet's. In a question on la belle indifférence, Hippocrates is a distractor: that phrase is linked with Pierre Janet (Section 1.3 and Chapter 3).

Hospitals of the Islamic world, and India's own tradition

When the Roman empire in the West collapsed, Greek medicine was preserved and developed in the Islamic world. The great cities of the Abbasid caliphate built hospitals called bimaristans (from a Persian word for a house of the sick). The first general hospital in Baghdad was built under the caliph Harun al-Rashid around 805 CE; a hospital at al-Fustat in Cairo, founded in 872, is described as the first to offer treatment for mental disorders; and the huge al-Mansuri hospital in Cairo, completed in 1284, treated mental patients and used music as therapy. The charitable endowments that funded these hospitals often laid down that no one, including the mentally ill, was to be turned away, and wards were divided for different illnesses. For several centuries the insane in Baghdad, Cairo and Damascus were treated more humanely than their counterparts in Europe.

The towering physician of this tradition was Avicenna (Ibn Sina, about 980 to 1037), a Persian born near Bukhara, whose Canon of Medicine was a standard medical textbook in Europe as well as the Islamic world for some five hundred years. One story about him shows a strikingly psychological touch. A Persian prince, traditionally identified as the Buyid ruler Majd al-Dawla, fell into melancholia, came to believe that he was a cow, begged to be slaughtered and refused to eat. Avicenna sent word that the butcher was coming. He arrived with a knife, ran his hands over the prince as a butcher examines an animal, and announced that this cow was far too thin to be killed; it would have to be fattened first. The prince began to eat, and as his strength returned, so the story goes, his delusion faded. Whatever the literal truth of the tale, it shows a physician entering the patient's belief rather than fighting it, an idea that therapists still use.

India's classical medicine developed its own naturalistic account of madness, independently of Greece but with a striking family resemblance. The texts of Ayurveda, the traditional Indian system of medicine, describe health as a balance of three doshas: vata, pitta and kapha, a three-part cousin of the four humours. The Charaka Samhita, compiled more than two thousand years ago, discusses unmada, the Ayurvedic term for insanity or psychosis, which it describes as a perversion of the mind, intellect, conscience, behaviour and conduct. Charaka lists five types of unmada: one caused by each of the three doshas (vataja, pittaja, kaphaja), one caused by the combined disturbance of all three (sannipatika, regarded as incurable), and an exogenous type, agantuja, attributed to causes outside the body, among which the classical texts include possessing spirits. Other classical texts, such as the Sushruta Samhita, give slightly different lists. Notice the mixture: most types of unmada are natural and bodily, but the supernatural is given a place of its own. Ayurveda even has a branch for it: bhuta vidya, one of its eight classical branches, deals with disorders attributed to spirits and is often loosely translated as "Ayurvedic psychiatry".

Charaka also described three kinds of therapy, and they make a neat bridge to modern practice. Daivavyapashraya is spiritual therapy (prayers, mantras, rituals and offerings); yuktivyapashraya is rational therapy (diet, herbal medicines and purificatory procedures); and sattvavajaya, literally "conquest of the mind", restrains the mind from harmful objects and is often described as the Ayurvedic form of psychotherapy. A caution belongs here. Indian scholars warn that unmada cannot simply be equated with schizophrenia or any other modern category, because the concepts grew from a different culture and a different theory of the body; treat the correspondences as family likenesses, not translations.

Indian context

Healing at religious shrines is still one of the commonest first routes to care in India. Hindu temples such as the Balaji temple at Mehandipur in Rajasthan, and Sufi dargahs such as the one at Erwadi in Tamil Nadu, have for generations received families bringing a disturbed relative for prayer, ritual and a stay at the shrine. The tradition has a dark side: on 6 August 2001 a fire swept through a privately run "home" near the Erwadi dargah and killed 28 mentally ill residents who had been kept in chains and could not escape. The Supreme Court then took up the issue of its own accord, and the National Human Rights Commission directed every state to certify that no mentally ill person was kept in chains. A clinical psychologist working in India should neither sneer at faith healing nor romanticise it: families often value the shrine's meaning and community, while the person may also need medical treatment and protection of their rights.

Key study: Healing at a Tamil Nadu temple (Raguram and colleagues, 2002)

Does a stay at a healing temple help people with severe mental illness, and if so, why? R. Raguram and his colleagues (Venkateswaran, Ramakrishna and Weiss) studied the temple of Muthuswamy in Tamil Nadu, where families bring mentally ill relatives to stay. Between June and August 2000 they assessed all 31 people who came: 23 with paranoid schizophrenia, 6 with delusional disorder and 2 with bipolar disorder. Each was rated on a standard psychiatric rating scale on the first day of the stay and again on the day of leaving. Scores fell by nearly 20 percent. The authors, writing in the British Medical Journal in 2002, attributed the improvement not to any specific ritual but to the supportive, non-threatening environment of the temple, and suggested that such places might have a role in community mental health care. The criticism is the obvious one: there was no comparison group, the follow-up ended when people left, and natural fluctuation in symptoms could explain part of the change. The study is valuable because it took a traditional practice seriously enough to measure it.

Exam trap

Unmada is the Ayurvedic word for insanity; Charaka gives five types (three single-dosha types, the combined sannipatika, and the exogenous agantuja); if an option gives a different number, check whether it is quoting another text, since the classical authors' lists differ. Bhuta vidya is the branch of Ayurveda dealing with spirit-caused disorders. Sattvavajaya is the Ayurvedic "psychotherapy". In the Islamic world, the hospitals are bimaristans and the great physician is Avicenna (Ibn Sina), author of the Canon of Medicine.

The Middle Ages: two currents, and the dancing manias

It is often said that the European Middle Ages simply swapped Greek naturalism for demons. The truth is more mixed, and historians still argue about the balance. Some, writing in the first half of the twentieth century, painted the period as one of almost unbroken demonology; others think supernatural explanations had much less influence than that. What is clear is that both currents ran side by side, sometimes in the treatment of the same patient.

The clearest example is a king. In the summer of 1392 Charles VI of France was riding with his army towards Brittany, under great strain. When an attendant dropped a lance with a loud clatter, the king thought he was under attack and turned on his own men, killing several knights before he could be overpowered from behind. Over the following years he would at times hide in a corner believing that he was made of glass, roam the corridors howling like a wolf, forget who he was, or fly into a rage at the sight of his own coat of arms. Watch how his court treated him. First came a 92-year-old physician who prescribed exactly what a follower of Hippocrates would have prescribed: removal to a country residence with clean air, rest, relaxation and recreation, and freedom from the burdens of government. The king improved. Then the physician died, the madness returned, and the court turned to the supernatural: a charlatan who claimed to own a book that God had given to Adam, rituals and potions, and in the end a hunt for the sorcerers who were supposed to have bewitched him. Two Augustinian friars even proposed cutting incisions in the king's head; the council refused. Nothing worked. The story, told by the historian Barbara Tuchman, captures the age: natural and supernatural explanations alternating, each tried when the other failed.

Natural explanations had serious medieval defenders. In the fourteenth century Nicholas Oresme, a bishop, philosopher and adviser to the French king, argued that much bizarre behaviour came from the disease of melancholy rather than from demons, and pointed out that most of the evidence for sorcery came from people who had been tortured and who, understandably, would confess to anything. Depression and anxiety were widely recognised as illnesses and treated with rest, sleep, baths, ointments, potions and a cheerful environment, although the Church often saw despair and lethargy as the sin of acedia, or sloth. In many villages the mentally disturbed, like the physically disabled, were looked after in turn by neighbours, moving from house to house.

One medieval community made such care its life's work. At Geel (also spelt Gheel), in what is now Belgium, pilgrims came to the shrine of Saint Dymphna, a legendary seventh-century princess believed to protect the insane. From the thirteenth century the pilgrims who were not cured were taken in as boarders by local families, and the town has been practising family foster care of the mentally ill ever since. In 1938 there were 3,736 boarders living with Geel families; close to 500 still do. Geel is often cited as the oldest example of what we now call community care.

Clinical link

Geel's model, ordinary families sharing their homes and daily lives with people who have long-term mental illness, anticipates two modern findings: that people with severe mental illness usually do better living in the community than in large institutions, and that social roles (helping in the shop, eating at the family table) are themselves therapeutic. Psychosocial rehabilitation, taught in Section 9.8, is in part a rediscovery of what Geel's families had been doing for seven centuries.

The strangest phenomena of the late Middle Ages were outbreaks of collective abnormal behaviour. On 24 June 1374 crowds in Aachen began to dance wildly in the streets, and the outbreak spread to Cologne, Flanders and Strasbourg. In July 1518 a woman in Strasbourg, named in most chronicles as Frau Troffea, began dancing in the street without music, and within weeks somewhere between 50 and 400 people had joined her. The city council at first hired musicians and strong men to keep the dancers going, on the theory that they would dance the affliction out of themselves; when that failed it banned public dancing and sent the dancers to a shrine of Saint Vitus. These episodes, which occurred mainly in mainland Europe between the fourteenth and seventeenth centuries, are called dancing mania, and also St Vitus' dance or St John's dance, because sufferers were thought to have been cursed by one of those saints. (The physician Paracelsus coined the word "choreomania" for them.) In southern Italy, especially around Taranto in Apulia, a related condition called tarantism was blamed on the bite of the tarantula spider; the victims were believed to need frenzied dancing to music to avoid death, and the lively folk dance called the tarantella is said to have grown out of this cure. Tarantism was first described in the eleventh century and was most common in the sixteenth and seventeenth.

What caused the dancing manias? People at the time blamed possession, divine punishment and overheated blood. Later suggestions include insect bites, poisoning by ergot (a fungus on rye that contains a chemical related to LSD, although poisoned people could hardly dance for days), and secret revivals of banned religious rites. The explanation most historians now favour is what we call mass hysteria, or in modern terms mass psychogenic illness: the rapid spread of symptoms with no physical cause through a group of people under stress. The historian John Waller has argued that the Strasbourg dancers had been worn down by famine, disease and fear, and that a shared belief in Saint Vitus' curse gave their distress its form. The phenomenon has not disappeared. In one modern American case, 17 students and 4 teachers were rushed to hospital with dizziness, headache, nausea and hyperventilation after a 14-year-old girl reported a strange smell from a classroom vent and collapsed; inspectors found no cause, doctors found no physical abnormality, and everyone recovered quickly. Fear, it seems, is contagious, an effect psychologists call emotion contagion, and frightened people are highly suggestible.

One more medieval label is worth knowing. The term lycanthropy (from the Greek for "wolf-man") means the belief that one has become, or can turn into, a wolf or another animal, with behaviour to match: howling, growling, crawling. Medieval writers often explained it by demons or curses, but as early as the fourth century the physician Oribasius had blamed an excess of black bile, and in 1563 Johann Weyer, whom you will meet in a moment, said the same. Charles VI's howling in the corridors and Avicenna's prince who believed he was a cow are both examples. Clinical lycanthropy is still occasionally reported in psychiatric case series as a delusion within psychotic or mood disorders.

Exam trap

Tarantism belongs to southern Italy (Taranto, Apulia; the tarantula; the tarantella). St Vitus' dance is the German and Low Countries form of dancing mania (Aachen 1374, Strasbourg 1518); note that "St Vitus' dance" is also an old medical name for Sydenham's chorea, a movement disorder of childhood, so read the stem carefully. Lycanthropy is the belief that one has turned into a wolf or other animal. The modern name for the dancing manias is mass psychogenic illness (mass hysteria). Geel (Belgium) is family foster care, not an asylum.

The witch hunts, and the first voices of protest

The darkest chapter came at the end of the Middle Ages and lasted into the age of science. In 1484 Pope Innocent VIII issued a bull, Summis desiderantes affectibus, that authorised the Dominican inquisitor Heinrich Kramer to investigate witchcraft in Germany. Kramer's answer was a book. The Malleus Maleficarum ("The Hammer of Witches") was first printed at Speyer in 1486 (the approbation printed with it is dated 1487, which is why some sources give that year). It is traditionally attributed to Kramer and his fellow Dominican Jacob Sprenger, and examiners expect both names, but historians doubt that Sprenger contributed much: he was first credited as co-author in 1519, twenty-four years after his death in 1495.

The Malleus had three parts. The first argued that witchcraft was real, since the Devil was real. The second described what witches could do and how they recruited, and the remedies against them. The third was a step-by-step legal manual for prosecuting witches, including how to interrogate and torture them. It taught that women were especially prone to witchcraft because they were, it claimed, weaker in faith and more carnal than men. Theologians at Cologne condemned it, and the Inquisition itself rejected Kramer's procedures, but secular courts across Europe used it, and it went through 28 editions between 1486 and 1600. The most intense witch prosecutions came between about 1560 and 1630, and they did not end in Europe until around 1780. They crossed the Atlantic too: the Salem witch trials in Massachusetts took place in 1692. Tens of thousands of people were executed, most of them women.

Were the accused witches mentally ill? The older histories said yes: the behaviour of disturbed people was taken as proof of a pact with the Devil, and many of the condemned were, on this view, psychotic or depressed. Later historians have questioned this. When they went back to the trial records, they found that most of the accused were not obviously mentally ill at all; they were more often poor, elderly, unpopular or quarrelsome women caught up in village conflicts, and their "confessions" were extracted by torture. The safest statement is that some people with mental illness were caught up in the witch hunts, and that demonology made it easy to treat strange behaviour as evidence of guilt, but that the witch hunts were not mainly a persecution of the mentally ill.

Against this background a few brave voices spoke for the natural view. The most important was the Dutch physician Johann Weyer (1515 to 1588), a pupil of the occultist Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa and later court physician to the Duke of Jülich-Cleves. In 1563 he published De Praestigiis Daemonum ("On the Illusions of the Demons"), which attacked the Malleus directly. Weyer argued that real witchcraft was practically impossible, and that a woman who confessed to it was most likely suffering from a mental disturbance, chiefly melancholy, and needed a physician rather than an executioner. He was attacked by the political philosopher Jean Bodin and by King James VI of Scotland, who wrote his own book on demonology, but he is often called the first psychiatrist for insisting that the mind could be sick in the same way as the body. In England Reginald Scot made a similar case in The Discoverie of Witchcraft (1584).

Another sixteenth-century figure offered a different natural explanation. The Swiss physician Paracelsus (1493 to 1541) rejected possession by the devil but suggested that the movements of the moon and stars, through their pull on the body's fluids, affected psychological functioning. Textbooks such as Barlow and Durand's link his theory with the word lunatic, from the Latin luna, the moon, although the word itself is older: the Late Latin lunaticus, "moonstruck", was in use centuries before Paracelsus, and it came into English around 1300. What Paracelsus did was give the ancient idea a new, quasi-scientific form. The belief survives in casual talk ("it must be the full moon") and in astrology, though no serious evidence has ever supported it.

Exam trap

The Malleus Maleficarum (1486; some sources 1487) is by Heinrich Kramer and Jacob Sprenger, two Dominicans, and it was a manual for hunting witches. Johann Weyer (De Praestigiis Daemonum, 1563) was the physician who argued against it, saying that accused witches were mentally ill. Do not swap them. The papal bull that preceded the Malleus is 1484 (Innocent VIII). Paracelsus is the moon theory of madness, which textbooks connect with the word "lunatic" (the word itself is older than he is); he also coined "choreomania".

The first asylums

An asylum originally meant a sanctuary, a place of refuge. The first institutions set aside for the mentally ill in Europe were founded in that spirit, and many of them became their opposite: places of confinement where people were hidden, restrained and sometimes displayed.

The founding story of one of the earliest is told in Valencia, in Spain. On 24 February 1409 a friar, Joan Gilabert Jofré (1364 to 1417), was walking to the cathedral to preach when he saw two young men brutally attacking a madman in the street. He rescued the man, took him to his convent, and then preached a sermon calling for a charitable house for the insane and other outcasts. Eleven citizens of Valencia, led by a merchant named Llorenç Salom, agreed to pay for it, and in 1410 a papal bull approved the Hospital of the Holy Innocents, known locally as the Hospital of the Mad. It is often claimed to be the first psychiatric institution in Europe. Similar hospitals soon followed in other Spanish cities, and the Spanish carried the idea to the New World: in Mexico City Bernardino Álvarez (1514 to 1584) obtained a licence in 1566 to build the Hospital of San Hipólito, regarded as the first hospital for the mentally ill in the Americas (sources give founding dates from 1566 to 1577, the later dates probably marking the completion of its buildings).

The most famous early asylum is in London. The Priory of St Mary of Bethlehem was founded in 1247 as a religious house, collecting alms and sheltering the poor. By 1403, the earliest firm record, it held six men described as mente capti, "deprived of their minds", and by about 1460 it had become a specialist institution for the insane. Londoners shortened its name to Bethlem, and then to Bedlam, which by the seventeenth century had become an ordinary English word for uproar and chaos. For a century and a half Bedlam was also a tourist attraction. Visitors came to stare at the inmates and were expected to leave a donation; in 1610 a nobleman paid ten shillings for a tour, and from the 1720s to 1770 the donations brought in some three hundred pounds a year. Only in 1770 did the hospital end free public visiting and require a ticket signed by a governor.

The image of the old asylum that reformers would later use most effectively also comes from Bethlem. In 1814 a Quaker reformer, Edward Wakefield, visited the hospital and found an American marine, James (often called William) Norris, who had been held since 1800 and restrained for more than ten years in an iron harness, chained so that he could barely move. Wakefield publicised the case, an engraving of Norris in his harness circulated widely, and his reports fed into the House of Commons Select Committee on Madhouses of 1815. Bethlem itself survived: it moved to Moorfields in 1676, to St George's Fields in Southwark in 1815, and to Beckenham on the edge of London in 1930, where the Bethlem Royal Hospital still treats patients today.

In seventeenth-century France confinement became state policy. In 1656 Louis XIV founded the Hôpital Général in Paris, which gathered beggars, vagrants, the old and sick, prostitutes, petty criminals and the insane into vast institutions, among them the Bicêtre for men and the Salpêtrière for women. The French philosopher Michel Foucault (1926 to 1984), in his history of madness published in 1961, called this the great confinement, and argued that the insane were locked up less to be cured than to be removed from a society that prized reason, order and work. You do not have to accept all of Foucault's argument to see his point: for most of their early history asylums served society's wish to be rid of disturbing people at least as much as they served the patients. In Vienna the emperor Joseph II added a circular five-storey "Fools' Tower" (the Narrenturm) to the city's new general hospital in 1784. In colonial America the Pennsylvania Hospital in Philadelphia, founded in 1751 by Benjamin Franklin and the physician Thomas Bond, admitted mentally ill patients and kept them in basement cells, and in 1773 Williamsburg, Virginia, opened the first public hospital in the American colonies devoted solely to the insane.

India's first asylums were colonial imports. The first lunatic asylum in India is usually dated to Bombay in 1745, followed by Calcutta in 1784, and more were built in the decades that followed. They were small, they were built for Europeans first (soldiers and sailors of the East India Company who had broken down), and Indian patients were admitted later and in worse conditions. Their growth was organised by the Indian Lunatic Asylums Act of 1858, and Section 1.10 traces the law from there; the European Mental Hospital at Ranchi, the ancestor of today's Central Institute of Psychiatry, appears in Section 1.5. Table 1.5 lists the early institutions in one place.

Table 1.5 Early institutions for the mentally ill.
