What was life like inside? In most eighteenth-century asylums patients were chained to walls or beds, often in darkness and cold, sometimes naked, and they were fed poorly. Treatment, where there was any, came from the humoral tradition: bleeding, purging, induced vomiting and blistering. Other "cures" relied on fright or shock. Patients were plunged without warning into ice-cold water, spun in rotating chairs until they lost consciousness, or, in a remedy some institutions actually built, lowered over a pit of snakes in the hope of scaring them back to their senses. Occasionally these shocks seemed to work for a while, which was enough to keep them in use. The underlying attitude was that the insane had lost their reason and with it their humanity; they were thought to be insensitive to cold, hunger and pain, and to need taming like animals.

That was the world the reformers of the late eighteenth century walked into. They did not invent kindness to the mad; the Asclepian temples, the bimaristans, Geel and many families had practised it long before. What they did was to turn kindness into a deliberate method of treatment, write it down, test it against results and spread it across two continents. That is the subject of Section 1.3.

Exam trap

Bedlam is a corruption of Bethlem, the Priory (later Hospital) of St Mary of Bethlehem in London, founded 1247; it is not in Bethlehem, and it is not the York Retreat. The "first mental hospital in Europe" usually means Valencia (1409); the first in the Americas is San Hipólito, Mexico City. The "great confinement" of 1656 is Foucault's phrase. India's first asylum is usually dated to Bombay, 1745.

1.3 Reform, Moral Treatment and Modern PsychiatryLower yield · not asked yet

Within a single decade at the end of the eighteenth century, in three cities a long way apart, people looked at chained, filthy, frightened patients and decided that they were not animals to be tamed but human beings who were ill and could get better. The decade was the 1790s; the cities were Florence, Paris and York. Their reform, which came to be called moral treatment, spread across Europe and America, achieved results that would impress us even today, and then collapsed under the weight of its own success. Out of the wreckage, in the second half of the nineteenth century, came a new biological psychiatry that found the cause of one major mental illness and gave the world its first modern classification of the psychoses. This section tells that story, and it ends where clinical psychology begins.

Chiarugi, Pinel and Pussin: unchaining in Florence and Paris

The first of the reformers is the one examiners forget. Vincenzo Chiarugi (1759 to 1820), a young physician in Florence, directed the hospital of Santa Dorotea from 1785 to 1788 and banned chains as a means of restraint there. In 1788 he became physician-director of the Bonifacio hospital, and in 1789 he was the chief author of its new humanitarian regulations: every patient was to have a detailed case history, men and women were to be separated, and inflicting physical pain was forbidden. Restraint was still used, but in gentler forms. Between 1793 and 1794 he published a three-volume treatise, On Insanity and Its Classification. Chiarugi did all this a few years before the French reformers, but Florence was a quiet backwater compared with revolutionary Paris, he left no pupils to spread his name, and history gave the credit to a Frenchman.

In August 1793, in the middle of the Terror, Philippe Pinel (1745 to 1826), a 48-year-old physician from the south of France with a scholarly interest in mental illness, was appointed physician of the infirmaries at the Bicêtre, the vast hospice for men on the southern edge of Paris. The insane were kept in one section of it, and that section was run by its superintendent, Jean-Baptiste Pussin (1746 to 1811). Pussin was no doctor. He was a former tanner who had come to the Bicêtre in 1771 as a patient, to be treated for scrofula (tuberculosis of the lymph glands), had stayed on as a member of staff, and in 1784 had been put in charge of the mental ward. With his wife Marguerite Pussin, who worked beside him from 1786, he had already been running the ward on new lines: firm but kind management, no beatings, attention to each patient, and as little restraint as possible.

Pinel watched, listened and learned. He talked to the patients for hours and took careful notes on their histories and on what seemed to help them. In 1795 he moved to become chief physician at the Salpêtrière, the even larger hospice for women, a post he kept for the rest of his life. Back at the Bicêtre, in 1797, Pussin took the decisive step: he removed the iron chains from the patients and permanently banned them as a means of restraint (straitjackets remained in use). When Pussin joined Pinel at the Salpêtrière in 1802, the chains came off there too. In his great book of 1801, the Medico-Philosophical Treatise on Mental Alienation, or Mania (translated into English as A Treatise on Insanity in 1806), Pinel acknowledged his debt to both Pussins, and in the second edition of 1809 he recorded that the ban on chains had been Pussin's initiative.

Remember

The famous picture of Pinel striking the chains from the madmen of the Bicêtre in 1793 is largely a legend. It was spread after his death, above all by his son Scipion Pinel, and fixed in the public mind by paintings such as Tony Robert-Fleury's Pinel Freeing the Insane from Their Chains (1876). The record shows a partnership: Pinel the physician who observed, wrote and gave the reform its authority, and Pussin the lay superintendent who actually took off the chains, at the Bicêtre in 1797. For exams, "Pinel, Bicêtre, 1793" remains the expected answer; for interviews, knowing about Pussin shows that you have read beyond the textbook.

What did Pinel actually believe? First, that the insane were sick people, not sinners, criminals or beasts, and that many of them could recover if treated humanely. Second, that the causes of their illness were often psychological and social, such as grief, disappointment, fear, overwork and family trouble, as well as physical. Third, that the physician's job was to observe each patient closely, record the history and course of the illness, and use a calm, ordered, respectful environment as treatment. He called this traitement moral, and in French "moral" meant emotional or psychological, not ethical. In a nosology (a system of classifying diseases) of 1798 Pinel grouped mental disorders into melancholia, mania, dementia and idiocy; the treatise of 1801 refined this into five forms, adding a striking new one, manie sans délire, "mania without delusion", in which a person behaves violently or impulsively while their reasoning remains intact. It is one of the ancestors of the later ideas of moral insanity and psychopathy (the personality disorders are taught in the Disorders book). Pinel is widely called the father of modern psychiatry.

Pinel's favourite pupil carried the reform across France. Jean-Étienne Dominique Esquirol (1772 to 1840) toured the country's asylums at his own expense in 1810, 1814 and 1817 and showed that the reforms of Paris had not reached the provinces. He was the main architect of the French law of 1838, which required every department of France to provide an asylum for its needy insane, and he summed up his faith in the institution in a famous phrase: a lunatic hospital is an instrument of cure. He gave the word "hallucination" its modern clinical meaning in 1817. Meanwhile, in Germany, the physician Johann Christian Reil (1759 to 1813) had coined the word psychiatry in 1808, from the Greek for "healing of the soul". A doctor who specialised in the insane was then usually called an alienist, from the French aliéné, a person estranged from his own mind; the name survived into the early twentieth century.

Exam trap

Pinel is the expected answer for "unchained the insane", "Bicêtre 1793", "moral treatment in France" and "father of modern psychiatry" (a title some books also give Kraepelin; let the rest of the stem decide: humane reform points to Pinel, classification to Kraepelin). The chains actually came off under Pussin in 1797; Chiarugi in Florence had banned chains even earlier (1785 to 1789). Pinel's Bicêtre was for men, the Salpêtrière for women (and later Charcot's hospital). Reil coined "psychiatry" in 1808; Esquirol drafted the French asylum law of 1838.

Tuke and the York Retreat

England's reform began with one death. In 1790 a Quaker widow named Hannah Mills was admitted to the York Asylum, a public institution in the north of England. Her Quaker friends asked to visit her and were refused, and a few weeks later she was dead. Those who later got inside the asylum found patients kept in conditions they described as worse than those of animals. Among the shocked Quakers was William Tuke (1732 to 1822), a prosperous tea and coffee merchant with no medical training. He proposed that the Society of Friends build its own institution, where its members could be cared for in a way that matched their beliefs. Quakers held that every human being carries an "inner light" that no illness can put out; if that is true, a madman is still a person, and must be treated as one.

The York Retreat opened in 1796 in the countryside outside the city. It was planned for about 30 people and began with just three. There were no chains or manacles, and physical punishment was banned. Residents wore their own clothes, walked in the gardens and courtyards, worked on the farm, did handicrafts, read and wrote, and shared a quiet, family-like life with a religious rhythm of prayer. The staff tried to restore each person's self-esteem and self-control through kindness, respect, regular occupation and gentle encouragement. Restraint was not entirely abolished (straitjackets were still used as a last resort, especially at first), but it was the exception rather than the rule. At first only Quakers were admitted; the Retreat gradually opened to everyone, and it still operates today.

The Retreat became famous through a book written by William's grandson, Samuel Tuke. His Description of the Retreat near York (1813) persuaded physicians across Britain and America to try the method, and it popularised the English term moral treatment, borrowed from the French traitement moral of Pinel and Pussin. Quakers in America founded similar retreats, among them the Friends Asylum near Philadelphia and the Hartford Retreat in Connecticut. In England the reform went one step further in the 1830s, when Robert Gardiner Hill at the Lincoln Asylum and then John Conolly (1794 to 1866) at the huge Hanwell Asylum near London, from 1839, abolished mechanical restraint altogether. This policy of non-restraint showed that even a large public asylum could be run without chains, straps or straitjackets, provided it had enough attentive staff.

Exam trap

William Tuke: English, Quaker, a merchant not a doctor, founded the York Retreat in 1796, triggered by the death of Hannah Mills (1790) at the York Asylum. The Retreat and the Asylum are different places, and they are on opposite sides of the story. Samuel Tuke, the grandson, wrote the Description of the Retreat (1813). Conolly (Hanwell, 1839) is the name for non-restraint.

Moral treatment: what it was and what it achieved

Put the French and English reforms together and you have a coherent method. Moral treatment was a psychosocial approach to mental disorder, influential in Europe and America in the first half of the nineteenth century, which aimed to restore patients to reason by treating them as normally as possible, in small institutions, within a setting that encouraged and rewarded ordinary social behaviour. Its main principles can be listed:

  • The patient is a person who is ill, not a beast or a sinner, and can recover; the staff's attitude should be kind, respectful and optimistic.
  • Chains, beatings and, as far as possible, all restraint and seclusion are abolished.
  • The institution is small, so that the superintendent and staff know every patient individually; experience suggested that it worked best with 200 patients or fewer.
  • Each day follows a regular routine of work (farming, gardening, sewing, carpentry), recreation, exercise, reading and religious observance, so that the patient's healthy habits are exercised and his disturbed ones crowded out.
  • Appropriate behaviour is noticed and rewarded with praise and privileges, so that relationships with staff become a means of treatment.
  • The physician observes each patient closely and keeps a record of the history and course of the illness.

Read that list again with Chapter 4 in mind and you will see positive reinforcement and a structured environment; read it with Chapter 7 in mind and you will see the therapeutic community. Moral treatment was a psychological treatment more than a century before psychology existed as a profession.

In the United States the transition was gradual, and its most famous early figure was a mixture of the old and the new. Benjamin Rush (1746 to 1813), a Philadelphia physician and one of the signers of the Declaration of Independence in 1776, joined the staff of the Pennsylvania Hospital in 1783 and in 1792 led a successful campaign for a separate, more humane ward for the insane. He noticed that patients who worked, cutting wood or washing, tended to recover, while those excused from work languished, and he insisted that the mentally ill were sick people deserving care. His Medical Inquiries and Observations upon the Diseases of the Mind (1812) was one of the first American books on psychiatry. Yet Rush also believed that madness came from disordered circulation of the blood in the brain, and he treated it with heavy bleeding, purging with mercury compounds, a "tranquilliser chair" that strapped the patient down with his head enclosed in a box, and a "gyrator", a board on which patients were strapped and spun to stimulate their circulation. The American Psychiatric Association recognised him in 1965 as the father of American psychiatry, and his profile is on its seal.

True moral treatment arrived in the United States in the 1810s and 1820s, through the Quaker retreats and new hospitals such as the McLean Asylum near Boston and the Worcester State Hospital in Massachusetts. The results were remarkable. In 1833 Horace Mann, chairman of the trustees at Worcester, reported on 32 patients who had been given up as incurable; treated with moral therapy, they recovered and were released to their families. Of 100 patients who had been violently assaultive before treatment, no more than 12 were still violent a year later, and of 40 who had routinely torn off any new clothes they were given, only 8 still did so. Figures like these, assembled by the historian J. Sanbourne Bockoven in 1963, would be impressive today. In 1844 thirteen American asylum superintendents founded the Association of Medical Superintendents of American Institutions for the Insane; in 1921 it took the name it has today, the American Psychiatric Association, which is why that association's seal carries the date 1844.

Clinical link

Lalitha is 46. She developed schizophrenia at 24 and has spent the last fourteen years on the long-stay ward of a state mental hospital. Her hallucinations are now mild, but she no longer remembers how to cook, catch a bus, handle money or start a conversation, and she sits most of the day in the same chair. Much of her disability comes not from her illness but from years of institutional life, the pattern Russell Barton called institutional neurosis (Section 9.6). The rehabilitation team that now works with her (a clinical psychologist, an occupational therapist, a psychiatric social worker and nurses) is using ideas that Tuke would have recognised: a regular daily routine, real work with real responsibility, praise for small steps, respect, and a move towards a small, homelike group residence. Moral treatment returned in the twentieth century as the therapeutic community (Section 7.11) and psychosocial rehabilitation (Section 9.8).

Exam trap

In "moral treatment" (or moral therapy) the word moral means psychological or emotional, not religious or ethical. Benjamin Rush is the "father of American psychiatry" (author of the first major American book on mental diseases, 1812), but he bled, purged and spun his patients and was only partly a moral therapist; do not confuse him with Lightner Witmer, the founder of clinical psychology (Section 1.4). The American Psychiatric Association began in 1844 as the association of asylum superintendents.

Dorothea Dix, the state hospitals and the decline of moral treatment

On Sunday 28 March 1841 a retired Boston schoolteacher named Dorothea Lynde Dix (1802 to 1887) went to the jail at East Cambridge, near Boston, to teach a Sunday school class for women prisoners. Among the prisoners she found mentally ill people, locked up alongside criminals in an unheated room in the middle of a New England winter. When she asked why they had no heat, she was told, so the story goes, that the insane did not feel the cold. Dix pressed the authorities until heat was provided, and then she set out to discover whether East Cambridge was unusual. She spent the better part of two years visiting the jails and almshouses of Massachusetts, taking notes.

In 1843 she presented her findings to the state legislature in a document known as the Memorial to the Legislature of Massachusetts. Its most quoted sentence is still worth reading aloud: "I proceed, Gentlemen, briefly to call your attention to the present state of Insane Persons confined within this Commonwealth, in cages, closets, cellars, stalls, pens! Chained, naked, beaten with rods, and lashed into obedience!" The legislature voted to expand the state hospital at Worcester. Dix then did the same thing in state after state, and over the next forty years she is credited with founding or enlarging 32 mental hospitals in the United States, including the state hospitals at Trenton in New Jersey and Harrisburg in Pennsylvania. In 1854 she persuaded the United States Congress to pass a bill setting aside more than 12 million acres of federal land to fund care for the indigent insane, but President Franklin Pierce vetoed it, arguing that welfare was the business of the states. In the same year she investigated the asylums of Scotland, and reform laws followed there in 1857. During the American Civil War she served as Superintendent of Army Nurses for the Union. She spent her last years living in the Trenton hospital she had helped to found, and died there in 1887.

Here lies one of history's sharpest ironies. Dix's crusade was a triumph of humanitarian reform, and it helped to kill moral treatment. She fought to make sure that everyone who needed care received it, including the poor, the homeless and those held in jails. The new and enlarged state hospitals filled up, and kept filling. Moral treatment depended on small numbers and individual attention; the state hospitals soon held 1,000 or 2,000 patients and more, with nowhere near enough staff. After the Civil War waves of poor immigrants arrived in America, and many of the patients now came from groups that the native-born staff and public regarded, with open prejudice, as less deserving of costly care. The first generation of enthusiastic reformers died, and their successors were more administrators than healers. Even the famous cure rates turned out to have been inflated, partly by counting the same patient's repeated admissions and discharges as separate cures; later studies by the asylum doctor Pliny Earle and others showed that the claims had been overblown. By the last decades of the nineteenth century the American mental hospital had become a place of custodial care: patients were housed, fed and controlled, but hardly treated. Figure 1.5 traces the chain of causes.

The final blow came from medicine itself. In the middle of the nineteenth century many leading physicians came to believe that insanity was always a disease of the brain, and, since the brain disease could not be found or cured, that most insanity was incurable. In America the champion of this view was John P. Gray (1825 to 1886; spelt "Grey" in some textbooks), from 1854 the superintendent of the Utica State Hospital in New York, the largest in the country, and editor of the American Journal of Insanity, the forerunner of today's American Journal of Psychiatry. Gray held that the causes of insanity were always physical and that the mentally ill should be treated as physically ill, with rest, diet, warmth and good ventilation (he even invented a rotary fan to air his huge hospital). His hospitals were clean and humane, but the belief in incurable brain disease made active psychological treatment seem pointless, and attention shifted from treating patients to diagnosing them, studying their brains after death and settling legal questions about their responsibility. The asylums went on growing for another century; in the United States they peaked in 1955 at about 559,000 patients. How they were finally emptied, by new drugs and the policy of deinstitutionalisation, and at what cost, is told in Section 9.6.

Exam trap

Dorothea Dix: American schoolteacher; East Cambridge jail (1841); Memorial to the Massachusetts legislature (1843); founded or enlarged 32 state hospitals; campaigned for the poor and those in jails and almshouses. Some textbooks (Barlow and Durand among them) say her work "became known as the mental hygiene movement". Most sources, and most Indian question papers, give the mental hygiene movement to Clifford Beers (1908 to 1909). If both names appear as options for "founder of the mental hygiene movement", choose Beers; Dix is the answer for asylum reform and the state hospitals.

Clifford Beers and the mental hygiene movement

The next great reformer had been a patient himself. Clifford Whittingham Beers (1876 to 1943) was born in New Haven, Connecticut, and graduated from Yale in 1897. In 1900, after a suicide attempt, he was admitted to a private mental institution in a state of severe depression with paranoid ideas, and was later diagnosed with manic-depressive illness. Over the next three years he passed through private hospitals and a state institution, and in all of them he experienced and watched rough handling, coercive restraint, neglect and sheer ignorance on the part of attendants and doctors alike. When he recovered, he resolved to do for the mentally ill what Harriet Beecher Stowe's Uncle Tom's Cabin had done for American slaves: to make the public feel what they suffered.

His autobiography, A Mind That Found Itself, was published in 1908. It described his breakdown, his delusions and his treatment in vivid, unsparing detail, and it became a bestseller. The philosopher and psychologist William James (1842 to 1910), the most famous American psychologist of the day, read the manuscript, praised it and helped launch it; a letter from him was printed in later editions. The Swiss-born psychiatrist Adolf Meyer (1866 to 1950), the founder of the psychobiological approach to psychiatry and soon to be the first director of the Henry Phipps Psychiatric Clinic at Johns Hopkins (opened in 1913), suggested the name for Beers's new organisation. In 1908 Beers founded the Connecticut Society for Mental Hygiene, and in 1909 he organised the National Committee for Mental Hygiene, which he served as secretary until 1939. In 1930 he helped organise the First International Congress on Mental Hygiene in Washington. The National Committee later became the National Mental Health Association, known today as Mental Health America. Beers spent his last years as a patient at Butler Hospital in Providence, Rhode Island, where he died in 1943.

The term mental hygiene was older than Beers. The American physician William Sweetser had used it in the mid-nineteenth century, and the psychiatrist Isaac Ray defined it as "the art of preserving the mind against all incidents and influences calculated to deteriorate its qualities". What Beers created was a movement. The mental hygiene movement was an early twentieth-century campaign, led by former patients, psychiatrists, psychologists, social workers and philanthropists, to improve the care of the mentally ill, to treat mental illness early, and above all to prevent it. Its programme included better conditions and trained staff in hospitals, outpatient clinics so that people could be treated before they needed admission, public education to reduce fear and stigma, research, and attention to children, on the theory that mental illness could be prevented by sound upbringing and early help. That last idea led directly to the child guidance clinics of the 1920s, which became one of the first homes of clinical psychologists (Section 1.4). The movement had a darker side as well: in the climate of its time, parts of it supported eugenics and the sterilisation of people judged "unfit", a reminder that a reform movement can carry the prejudices of its age. After the Second World War the phrase "mental hygiene" gradually gave way to "mental health", and the movement's emphasis on prevention passed into community mental health (Section 9.6).

Between Chiarugi's regulations of 1789 and Beers's committee of 1909, then, the reform of mental health care passed through four countries and many hands. Table 1.6 gathers the reformers in one place; it is worth learning as a set, because examiners like to swap their names, places and dates.

Table 1.6 The reformers at a glance.
ReformerWhereWhenRemember for
Vincenzo ChiarugiFlorence (Santa Dorotea, Bonifacio)1785 to 1789Banned chains; humane regulations, before Pinel
Philippe PinelParis (Bicêtre, then Salpêtrière)1793; treatise 1801Traitement moral; "father of modern psychiatry"
Jean-Baptiste and Marguerite PussinParis (Bicêtre)Chains removed 1797Lay superintendents who actually unchained patients
William TukeYork, England1796Quaker York Retreat; Samuel Tuke's Description (1813)
John ConollyHanwell, near LondonFrom 1839Non-restraint in a large public asylum
Benjamin RushPhiladelphia1783 to 1813; book 1812"Father of American psychiatry"; still bled and purged
Dorothea DixUnited States, Scotland1841 to 1880sMemorial (1843); 32 state hospitals
Clifford BeersUnited States1908 to 1909A Mind That Found Itself; mental hygiene movement
Exam trap

A Mind That Found Itself: Clifford Beers, 1908, a former patient's autobiography. National Committee for Mental Hygiene: 1909 (the Connecticut Society came first, 1908). Adolf Meyer suggested the name "mental hygiene"; William James endorsed the book. Do not mix these years with Witmer's (clinic 1896, journal and the term "clinical psychology" 1907, Section 1.4). Beers was a layman and former patient, not a psychiatrist or psychologist.

The biological breakthrough: general paresis

While the reformers were changing how patients were treated, the biological tradition was slowly rebuilding itself on a scientific footing. Its programme was stated most clearly by the German psychiatrist Wilhelm Griesinger (1817 to 1868), whose textbook The Pathology and Therapy of Mental Diseases (1845) argued that mental illness must be understood as disease of the brain. His position is usually summed up in the slogan that mental diseases are brain diseases; in the first issue of the journal he founded in Berlin, in 1868, he wrote that patients with so-called mental illnesses are really individuals with illnesses of the nerves and brain. This is the somatogenic perspective: the view that abnormal psychological functioning has physical causes. (Interestingly, Griesinger was progressive about care: he favoured short hospital stays and keeping patients connected to their families and communities.) The perspective needed proof, and the proof came from a disease that filled the asylums.

Nineteenth-century asylum doctors had noticed a group of patients who were unlike the rest. They often began with grandiose delusions (believing themselves to be enormously rich, or kings, or God) or with persecutory ideas, and then deteriorated steadily: their speech slurred, their limbs weakened into paralysis, their intellect decayed, and they died, usually within about five years. In 1822 the young French physician Antoine Laurent Jessé Bayle (1799 to 1858) described this condition as a distinct disease (some textbooks date its recognition to 1825), and it came to be called general paresis, or general paralysis of the insane. It was common: by 1877 one New York asylum reported that it accounted for more than 12 percent of male admissions. The question was what caused it. Many doctors suspected syphilis, because so many paretic patients had a history of it, but many others denied any history of infection, and the gap of ten or twenty years between infection and madness made the connection hard to prove.

Key study: The inoculation experiment (reported by Krafft-Ebing, 1897)

The Viennese psychiatrist Richard von Krafft-Ebing (1840 to 1902) reported to an international medical congress in Moscow in 1897 an experiment designed to settle the question. It rested on a simple fact: a person who has had syphilis once cannot catch it again. Nine men with typical general paresis, in whom neither a careful history nor a specialist examination had found any sign of past syphilis, were inoculated with material from the sores of three patients with active syphilis. If they had never been infected, they should have developed the signs of new syphilis. They were watched for 180 days, the longest known incubation period, and none of them did. The conclusion was that they must already have been infected, and that syphilis was the cause of their paresis. The experiment is usually credited to Krafft-Ebing in textbooks, although his report said the inoculations had been done by a "highly reliable colleague" who wished, for the time being, to remain unnamed. It is also an experiment that no ethics committee would allow today, since the patients could not consent and the procedure risked serious harm (Section 1.8 explains the rules that now forbid it).

The laboratory soon confirmed the conclusion. In 1905 Fritz Schaudinn and Erich Hoffmann identified the organism that causes syphilis, the spirochaete now called Treponema pallidum; in 1906 August von Wassermann introduced a blood test for the infection; and in 1913 Hideyo Noguchi (1876 to 1928) and J. W. Moore demonstrated the spirochaete in the brains of patients who had died of general paresis. A major form of madness had been traced, for the first time, to a specific physical cause. A treatment followed. The Austrian psychiatrist Julius Wagner-Jauregg (1857 to 1940) had noticed that paretic patients sometimes improved after a high fever, and in 1917 he deliberately infected them with malaria; the fever destroyed much of the heat-sensitive spirochaete, and the malaria could then be treated with quinine. For this fever therapy he received the Nobel Prize in 1927, the first ever awarded to a psychiatrist. After the Second World War penicillin made both malaria treatment and general paresis itself rare.

The lesson that psychiatry drew from general paresis was enormous, and partly mistaken. If one form of insanity was caused by a germ in the brain, perhaps all the others would turn out to have equally specific bodily causes, waiting only for better microscopes. That hope drove a century of biological research and produced real discoveries, but no other major mental disorder has turned out to have a single cause of this kind. Today's view, as Section 1.7 explains, is that most mental disorders arise from many biological, psychological and social causes acting together.

Exam trap

General paresis = late brain syphilis (Treponema pallidum). The sequence: described by Bayle (1822); inoculation experiment reported by Krafft-Ebing (1897); organism found in paretic brains by Noguchi and Moore (1913); malaria fever therapy by Wagner-Jauregg (1917; Nobel Prize 1927). It is the classic example of the somatogenic perspective. Griesinger (1845): mental diseases are brain diseases. Kraepelin did not discover the cause of paresis.

Emil Kraepelin and the classification of mental disorders

The somatogenic perspective had one more giant, and he changed psychiatry not by finding a cause but by sorting patients. Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926) studied medicine at Leipzig, where he also worked in the laboratory of Wilhelm Wundt (1832 to 1920), the founder of experimental psychology; there he measured how alcohol, tea and other drugs changed simple mental processes such as reaction times, work that makes him a forerunner of psychopharmacology. He went on to hold chairs of psychiatry at Dorpat, Heidelberg and Munich. His Compendium of Psychiatry (1883) grew, edition by edition, into the most influential psychiatric textbook ever written; by the eighth edition (1909 to 1915) it filled four volumes, and a ninth appeared after his death, in 1927.

Kraepelin's insight was methodological. The asylum doctors of his day classified patients by their symptoms at the moment of examination, and the same symptoms (excitement, delusions, despair) turned up in patients whose futures were completely different. Kraepelin, working with large numbers of patients in Heidelberg, recorded each one's symptoms on cards and then followed the cards over years to see what became of the person. He argued that a true disease should be defined by its whole pattern: its symptoms, its age of onset, its course over time and its outcome, and that each such disease probably had its own cause, much as general paresis did. Classification of this kind, the sorting of diseases into categories, is called nosology.

Applying this method to the major psychoses, Kraepelin reached a famous division. Several earlier writers had described pieces of the picture. The French psychiatrist Bénédict-Augustin Morel (1809 to 1873) had used the phrase démence précoce ("early dementia") in passing in 1852 for young people whose minds seemed to decay; the German psychiatrists Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899) and Ewald Hecker (1843 to 1909) had described hebephrenia (Hecker, 1871) and catatonia (Kahlbaum, 1874). In the fourth edition of his textbook (1893) Kraepelin took up the term dementia praecox, and in the sixth edition (1899) he made it the name of a single disease that united the hebephrenic, catatonic and paranoid forms. Against it he set a second great group, manic-depressive insanity, which gathered together mania, melancholia and the circular and periodic psychoses. The difference between them, he argued, lay above all in course and outcome. Table 1.7 sets out the contrast.

Table 1.7 Kraepelin's division of the major psychoses (sixth edition, 1899).
FeatureDementia praecoxManic-depressive insanity
Core disturbanceIntellect and will: thinking, attention, volitionMood: elation or depression
Typical onsetAdolescence or early adulthood (hence "praecox", early)Any age, often in episodes
CourseContinuous, progressive deteriorationEpisodic, with symptom-free intervals
OutcomePoor; recovery thought to be rareGood between episodes; often full recovery
Forms includedHebephrenic, catatonic, paranoidMania, melancholia, circular and periodic forms
Modern descendantSchizophrenia (Bleuler's term)Bipolar and recurrent depressive disorders

The name dementia praecox did not last. The Swiss psychiatrist Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939), director of the Burghölzli hospital in Zurich, saw that many of these patients did not deteriorate steadily, that some improved, and that the illness did not always begin early. In 1908 he proposed a new name, schizophrenia, from the Greek for "split mind", meaning a splitting of the normally unified functions of thinking, feeling and acting (not a "split personality"), and in 1911 he published his monograph Dementia Praecox, or the Group of Schizophrenias. The symptoms Bleuler considered fundamental, and the later first-rank symptoms of Kurt Schneider, are taught in the Disorders book.

Kraepelin's legacy is double. On the positive side, his insistence on careful description, long-term follow-up and the separation of distinct disorders is the foundation of every modern classification. When the American Psychiatric Association published DSM-III in 1980, with explicit criteria for each disorder, its architects were often called "neo-Kraepelinians". He was also one of the founders of comparative psychiatry: in 1904 he travelled to Java to see whether the same disorders appeared in a very different culture. On the negative side, his prognosis for dementia praecox was too gloomy, and long-term studies have since shown that the course of schizophrenia is far more varied and hopeful than he believed (Section 9.8). He was a strong supporter of eugenics and racial hygiene, and his purely categorical approach, in which a person either has a disease or does not, has been challenged by dimensional views of psychopathology (Section 2.10). Like Gray before him, he was much more interested in diagnosis than in treatment.

Exam trap

Dementia praecox is the historical name for schizophrenia, not for Alzheimer's disease, bipolar disorder or frontotemporal dementia; Kraepelin defined it as a disease (1893, unified 1899), Morel had used démence précoce loosely (1852), and Bleuler renamed it schizophrenia (1908; book 1911). Kraepelin's basis for separating it from manic-depressive insanity was course and outcome. Kraepelin is the classic biological, categorical classifier: he is a distractor, not the answer, for the biopsychosocial model (George Engel, 1977, Section 1.7). First-rank symptoms are Kurt Schneider's; the double bind is Bateson's (Chapter 7).

The psychological tradition returns, and the road to the present

Against the somatogenic perspective stood the psychogenic perspective: the view that the chief causes of abnormal functioning are often psychological. It too had a nineteenth-century history, and it began, improbably, with a showman. In the 1770s the Viennese physician Franz Anton Mesmer (1734 to 1815) claimed that an invisible fluid, which he called animal magnetism, flowed through all living bodies and that its blockage caused illness. In Paris, from 1778, he treated fashionable patients seated around a tub of "magnetised" water, holding iron rods, while he passed his hands over them, and many of them fell into fits and trances and declared themselves cured. In 1784 a royal commission that included the American scientist and diplomat Benjamin Franklin investigated and concluded that there was no magnetic fluid: the effects came from imagination. The commission was right about the fluid, and it missed the point, which was that imagination could cure. Mesmer is often called the father of hypnosis, and the word itself came later, from the Scottish surgeon James Braid in 1843.

Hypnosis returned to respectability in France in the 1880s through a famous dispute. At the Salpêtrière, the same hospital where Pinel had worked, the great neurologist Jean-Martin Charcot (1825 to 1893) studied hysteria and held that only people with a diseased nervous system could be deeply hypnotised. In the provincial town of Nancy, the physician Hippolyte Bernheim, building on the country doctor Ambroise-Auguste Liébeault, argued that hypnosis was simply heightened suggestibility, a normal capacity, and that hysterical symptoms could be produced and removed by suggestion; the Nancy school's view largely won. Pierre Janet (1859 to 1947), who worked under Charcot, explained hysteria by dissociation, a splitting off of parts of consciousness, and is the name most often attached to la belle indifférence, the curious calm of some patients towards their dramatic symptoms. In Vienna the physician Josef Breuer (1842 to 1925), treating the patient known as Anna O. between 1880 and 1882, found that her symptoms eased when, under hypnosis, she talked about the emotional experiences connected with them. His younger colleague Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), who had studied with Charcot in 1885 and 1886, worked with Breuer on Studies on Hysteria (1895) and went on to create psychoanalysis. That story, from mesmerism to free association, is told in full in Section 3.1. The second great psychological school of the twentieth century, behaviourism, which explained abnormal behaviour as learned, is the subject of Chapter 4.

The biological tradition did not stand still either. In the 1930s, with the asylums overflowing and no effective treatment for psychosis, psychiatrists tried a series of bold and often dangerous physical treatments: insulin coma therapy (Manfred Sakel), convulsions induced by drugs (Ladislas Meduna, 1934), electroconvulsive therapy (Ugo Cerletti and Lucio Bini in Rome, 1938) and prefrontal leucotomy, a crude form of brain surgery (António Egas Moniz, 1935, rewarded with a Nobel Prize in 1949). Most of these were abandoned; electroconvulsive therapy survives in a much safer, modified form, and its legal regulation in India is set out in Section 1.10. Then, around 1950, came the first effective drugs: lithium for mania (John Cade, 1949) and chlorpromazine for psychosis (introduced in France in 1952). Together with the new community psychiatry, these drugs made possible the emptying of the great asylums that Dix had helped to build (Section 9.6).

Where, in all this, is clinical psychology? Not yet born, but almost. By the 1890s psychology had become an experimental science in the laboratories of Wundt and his students; psychiatrists like Kraepelin were importing its methods; the mental hygiene movement was about to demand people who could assess and help troubled children; and the psychological tradition was about to produce psychoanalysis. In March 1896, in Philadelphia, a psychologist named Lightner Witmer saw his first case, and Section 1.4 takes up the story from there.

The fastest way to see what this long history means for a patient is to push one patient through it. Imagine Govind, a 24-year-old son of a handloom weaver in a village near Mysuru. Over three weeks he stops sleeping, hears voices accusing him of shameful acts, becomes convinced that his neighbours are poisoning his food, and stops working. Table 1.8 asks what would have been made of Govind, and done to him, in each era of this chapter.

Table 1.8 Worked example: one patient, Govind, in different eras.
Era and settingHow Govind's illness is explainedWhat is done for (or to) him
Stone AgeAn evil spirit has entered him (inferred)Perhaps trephining or an exorcism rite
Hippocratic GreeceAn imbalance of humours affecting the brainRest, diet, abstinence; bleeding or purging
Classical AyurvedaUnmada: a dosha disturbance, or agantuja if blamed on spiritsPurification, herbal medicines, rituals, sattvavajaya
Late medieval EuropePossession or sorcery; perhaps melancholyExorcism; at worst suspicion of witchcraft
Bethlem, 1790sHe has lost his reason and is dangerousChains, bleeding, purging, cold plunges, display to visitors
York Retreat, 1800sA person whose mind is disordered but can recoverKindness, routine, farm work, conversation, no chains
Crowded state hospital, 1880sBrain disease, probably incurableCustodial care for years
Kraepelin's clinic, 1900Dementia praecox (if he deteriorates over years)Careful diagnosis; poor prognosis given
After 1906Rule out syphilis firstA blood test for general paresis
Today in IndiaBiological, psychological and social causes togetherAssessment and formulation (Chapter 2), medication, psychological therapy, family work, rights under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (Section 1.10)

Two lessons come out of the table. First, explanation drives treatment: every era did to Govind what its theory of his illness said it should, which is why a clinician's model of a problem matters so much (Section 1.7). Second, the old explanations do not disappear. In today's Mysuru, Govind's family may well take him to a temple or to a healer before they bring him to a clinic, and the clinician who understands why will work with the family far better than one who does not.

Remember

Three traditions run through the whole history. Supernatural: trephining, demonology, exorcism, the witch hunts. Biological (somatogenic): Hippocrates and the humours, Galen, Griesinger, general paresis, Kraepelin, the somatic treatments and modern drugs. Psychological (psychogenic): Plato's re-education, the Asclepian temples, moral treatment (Pinel, Pussin, Tuke), the mental hygiene movement, Mesmer, Charcot, Janet, Breuer and Freud. Humane reform (Chiarugi, Pinel, Tuke, Dix, Beers) cuts across them: it is about how patients are treated, whatever is believed to cause their illness.

Exam trap

Mesmer: animal magnetism (1770s), "father of hypnosis", investigated by a commission including Benjamin Franklin (1784); the word hypnosis is Braid's (1843). Charcot (Salpêtrière) thought hypnotisability was a sign of hysteria; Bernheim (Nancy) said it was normal suggestibility. Janet: dissociation and la belle indifférence. Breuer: Anna O. and the cathartic method. The somatogenic perspective says causes are physical; the psychogenic perspective says they are psychological.
