1.4 The Rise of Clinical Psychology in the WestMedium yield · 4 Qs

Sections 1.2 and 1.3 followed the care of the mentally ill from the exorcist to the asylum and through the great reforms. Look back over that story and you will notice something odd: psychologists are almost nowhere in it. The reformers were physicians, philanthropists and former patients; the classifiers were psychiatrists. In 1890 a psychologist was a laboratory scientist who measured reaction times and sensory thresholds, and a psychologist who wanted to help a troubled person had no role to play, no training to get and no title to use. Sixty years later there was a profession with its own doctoral training, its own internships, a government paying for its students and an agreed model of what a clinical psychologist should be. How did a laboratory science become a helping profession? The answer comes in five stages: a clinic for schoolchildren, an era of mental testing, a long struggle to organise, two world wars, and a series of conferences that argued over how clinical psychologists should be trained. Figure 1.6 lays the milestones out in order; keep it in view as you read.

Witmer's clinic and the birth of a name

You met Lightner Witmer (1867 to 1956) at the start of this chapter, in March 1896, turning a teacher's question about one boy who could not spell, Charles Gilman, into the first case of a new kind of work. Why was it Witmer who took the case, and not one of the many other psychologists in American universities? Part of the answer is his training. Born in Philadelphia, he had studied psychology at the University of Pennsylvania as an assistant to James McKeen Cattell (1860 to 1944), the American psychologist most interested in measuring differences between individuals. When Cattell left for Columbia University, Witmer went to Leipzig, where he earned his doctorate in 1892 under Wilhelm Wundt (1832 to 1920), the founder of the first psychological laboratory (1879), and then returned to direct the laboratory at Pennsylvania. He was, in other words, a thoroughly trained experimentalist. The other part of the answer is temperament. Witmer believed that a science of the mind should be judged by whether it could help a particular person, and he was willing to try.

The clinic he opened at the University of Pennsylvania in 1896 was the first of its kind anywhere, and its way of working became a template for the profession. Four features stand out. First, the unit of study was the individual case, not the group average of the laboratory. Second, work began with careful assessment: the child was observed, examined and set tasks, and other specialists, such as a physician or an eye doctor, were brought in where a problem might be physical. Third, assessment led to treatment, which for Witmer meant a programme of remedial teaching designed for that one child and followed up to see whether it worked. Fourth, the clinic was tied to the school and the family: teachers and parents referred the children, and they carried out the plan. Most of Witmer's cases were children with what we would now call learning disabilities, intellectual disability, speech problems and behaviour problems. A psychological clinic, in Witmer's sense, was therefore a place where the methods of psychology were used to study one person and to change the course of that person's development.

In December 1896 Witmer described his plan for this practical work to the annual meeting of the American Psychological Association. His audience showed little enthusiasm. Psychology was working hard to be accepted as an experimental science, and applying it to schoolchildren seemed premature. Witmer pressed on. In 1907 he founded the first journal of the new field, The Psychological Clinic, and its opening article, titled "Clinical psychology", put the name of the field into print. He borrowed the word "clinical" from medicine, where it had long meant teaching and studying at the patient's bedside (the Greek klinē means a bed), but he insisted that the new field was not a branch of medicine. The methods of clinical psychology, he wrote, are "necessarily invoked wherever the status of an individual mind is determined by observation and experiment, and pedagogical treatment applied to effect a change". Read it slowly and you will find two halves: assessment ("determined by observation and experiment") and intervention ("treatment applied to effect a change"). More than a century later, those are still the two halves of the job.

In the same year Witmer added a hospital school in which children could live while they were treated, and by 1914 about two dozen psychological clinics had opened in American universities. Yet the profession that grew up under his name did not stay inside his clinic. Witmer's clinic belonged to education. The clinical psychology of the mid-twentieth century would belong to hospitals, child guidance centres and the armed forces, and its first great tool was not remedial teaching but the mental test.

Exam trap

The first psychological clinic (University of Pennsylvania, 1896), the first journal of the field (The Psychological Clinic, 1907) and the first appearance of the term "clinical psychology" in print (1907) all belong to Lightner Witmer. The distractors are real people with different firsts: Wilhelm Wundt founded the first psychological laboratory (Leipzig, 1879) and supervised Witmer's doctorate; G. Stanley Hall founded the American Psychological Association (1892) and brought Freud to Clark University in 1909; James McKeen Cattell coined "mental test" (1890) and was Witmer's teacher; Sigmund Freud founded psychoanalysis; Carl Rogers founded client-centred therapy; B. F. Skinner is operant conditioning; Erik Erikson is the psychosocial stages. Do not swap 1896 (the clinic) with 1907 (the journal and the term in print).

The testing era

If you had met a clinical psychologist in 1930, you would almost certainly have met a tester. To see why, go back to London in 1884. At the International Health Exhibition, Francis Galton (1822 to 1911), Charles Darwin's cousin, ran an anthropometric laboratory in which, for a small fee, visitors had their reaction time, grip strength, keenness of sight and hearing and other qualities measured. Galton believed that intelligence showed itself in sharp senses and quick reactions, so that measuring these was measuring ability; he is often called the father of mental testing. Cattell, who had worked with Galton in England, carried the programme to America, and in an 1890 paper in the journal Mind, "Mental tests and measurements", he coined the term mental test.

The programme failed its first real trial. In 1901 Clark Wissler (1870 to 1947), one of Cattell's students at Columbia, compared students' scores on Cattell's tests with their college grades and found almost no relationship; the tests did not even agree well with one another. Sensory sharpness was not intelligence. The method that worked came from France. In 1904 the French government asked Alfred Binet (1857 to 1911) to find a way of identifying children who could not profit from ordinary schooling and needed special classes. With Théodore Simon (1872 to 1961) he published in 1905 a scale of thirty tasks of increasing difficulty that sampled judgement, comprehension and reasoning rather than reaction time, and in the 1908 revision the tasks were grouped by the age at which typical children could pass them, which gave each child a mental age. Notice the purpose. The first successful intelligence test was built to find children who needed help, exactly the children Witmer was seeing in Philadelphia.

The scale crossed the Atlantic quickly. Henry Herbert Goddard (1866 to 1957) translated it in 1908 for use at the Vineland Training School in New Jersey, a residential school for children then called "feeble-minded", and later used it, with grave errors of judgement, to argue that feeble-mindedness was inherited. Lewis Madison Terman (1877 to 1956) of Stanford University standardised an American revision in 1916, the Stanford-Binet, and popularised the intelligence quotient (IQ), an idea William Stern (1871 to 1938) had proposed in 1912. Then war turned testing into a mass enterprise. When the United States entered the First World War in 1917, Robert Mearns Yerkes (1876 to 1956), that year's president of the American Psychological Association, led the committee that built two group tests for army recruits: the Army Alpha, a written test for recruits who could read English, and the Army Beta, a pictorial and performance test for those who were illiterate or did not know English. About 1.75 million men were tested. For the same army Robert Sessions Woodworth (1869 to 1962) built the Personal Data Sheet, a list of yes-or-no questions about symptoms intended to screen out men likely to break down under fire; it is usually counted as the first personality questionnaire.

The war gave psychology public prestige and a practical identity, but it also showed what tests can do in careless hands: the army results were later quoted in American arguments for restricting immigration from southern and eastern Europe, as if the scores measured inborn ability rather than schooling and familiarity with American life. Between the wars the clinician's toolkit filled up: the Swiss psychiatrist Hermann Rorschach's inkblot test (1921), Christiana Morgan and Henry Murray's Thematic Apperception Test (1935), the Wechsler-Bellevue Intelligence Scale of David Wechsler (1896 to 1981) in 1939, and Starke Hathaway and J. Charnley McKinley's Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (MMPI) in 1943. The tests themselves are taught in the Assessment book. What matters here is the role they created. In the hospitals and clinics of the 1920s and 1930s the clinical psychologist was a psychometrician who tested patients at the request of a psychiatrist, while diagnosis and psychotherapy belonged to medicine. Changing that division of labour would take another war.

Exam trap

Galton (anthropometric laboratory, 1884) is the father of mental testing; Cattell coined the term "mental test" (1890); Binet and Simon (1905) built the first practical intelligence test, to pick out children who needed special education; Stern proposed the intelligence quotient (1912) and Terman popularised it in the Stanford-Binet (1916). Army Alpha was the verbal, written test for literate recruits; Army Beta was the non-verbal, pictorial test for illiterate and non-English-speaking recruits (Yerkes, 1917). Do not reverse Alpha and Beta, and do not date Wechsler's first scale before 1939.

Child guidance and the first professional organisations

A second root of the profession grew from the courts. In 1909 the physician William Healy (1869 to 1963) opened the Juvenile Psychopathic Institute in Chicago to study young offenders sent by the juvenile court, one child at a time, on the assumption that delinquency had psychological causes that could be found and treated. In the 1920s a charitable foundation, the Commonwealth Fund, paid for demonstration clinics in American cities, and the child guidance movement spread a new way of organising care. Each clinic had a team of three: a psychiatrist who treated the child, a psychologist who tested the child, and a psychiatric social worker who worked with the parents. That team is the direct ancestor of the multidisciplinary team described in Section 1.1, and it gave psychologists a regular place in clinical work, even if the place was the testing room.

Meanwhile psychologists were arguing about who they were. The American Psychological Association (APA) had been founded in July 1892 at Clark University by Granville Stanley Hall (1844 to 1924), its first president, as a society for the scientific study of the mind, and it had little interest in practice. In 1917 a small group of applied psychologists, among them J. E. Wallace Wallin (1876 to 1969) and Leta Stetter Hollingworth (1886 to 1939), broke away to form the American Association of Clinical Psychologists (AACP). The split was short-lived: in 1919 the AACP rejoined the APA as its Clinical Section. An APA scheme of the 1920s to certify "consulting psychologists" attracted few applicants and was abandoned in 1927. In 1937 the applied psychologists left again, this time with consulting, educational and industrial colleagues, to form the American Association for Applied Psychology (AAAP).

The two organisations reunited in 1945, when the APA reorganised itself into divisions and added the advancement of psychology "as a profession" to its aims. Clinical psychology became Division 12 of the APA (today the Society of Clinical Psychology), the body whose definition of the field you met in Section 1.1. Counselling psychology is Division 17, and health psychology, created much later (1978), is Division 38. In 1947 the American Board of Examiners in Professional Psychology was set up to award a diploma to experienced practitioners, an early form of specialist certification. By the end of the Second World War, then, clinical psychology had a name, a set of tools, a place in the team and a home in the APA. What it still lacked was a standard training and the money to pay for it.

Exam trap

Learn the sequence as one chain: 1892 APA founded (Hall); 1917 AACP breaks away; 1919 AACP rejoins as the APA Clinical Section; 1937 AAAP; 1945 reunion and Division 12 (clinical). If an item offers both 1919 and 1945 for the APA's clinical body, read it carefully: 1919 is the section, 1945 the division. Division 12 is clinical, 17 counselling, 38 health psychology.

War, the Veterans Administration and the Shakow report

The Second World War did for psychotherapy what the First had done for testing. Psychologists in uniform screened recruits, but they also found themselves treating soldiers with combat stress, because there were far too few psychiatrists to go round. When the war ended the problem came home. The Veterans Administration (VA), the American government agency responsible for former servicemen, had tens of thousands of veterans with psychiatric disorders in its hospitals and many more in its outpatient clinics. In 1946 it decided to train clinical psychologists on a large scale, paying graduate students to work in VA hospitals during their training and offering them jobs afterwards, and it soon became the largest employer of clinical psychologists in the country. The same year Congress passed the National Mental Health Act, which created the National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH) in 1949, and the United States Public Health Service began giving grants for mental health training. For the first time there was steady government money for making clinical psychologists.

Two developments changed what those psychologists did. One was the arrival of a psychological psychotherapy. In Counseling and Psychotherapy (1942) the psychologist Carl Ransom Rogers (1902 to 1987) described a non-medical way of helping people talk through their problems (his client-centred therapy is taught in Chapter 6), and the war generation of clinicians took it up eagerly. The other was a plan for training. Money needs standards: the VA wanted to know which universities could train clinical psychologists properly. Rogers, as president of the APA, asked David Shakow (1901 to 1981), who as chief psychologist at Worcester State Hospital in Massachusetts had drafted a training plan as early as 1941, to chair a Committee on Training in Clinical Psychology. Its report of 1947, usually called the Shakow report, set out what a training programme should look like:

  • Clinical psychologists should be trained first as psychologists and then as clinicians, in university psychology departments, to the level of the doctorate (the PhD).
  • The training should take four years: a foundation in psychology and related sciences in the first year, the principles and practice of assessment and therapy in the second, a supervised clinical internship in the third, and a research dissertation in the fourth.
  • The finished clinical psychologist should be competent in three areas: diagnosis (assessment), therapy and research.

In the same period the APA began to evaluate and accredit doctoral programmes against standards of this kind, and the government agencies preferred to fund students in accredited ones. What the plan needed now was the agreement of the profession as a whole.

Exam trap

The Shakow report (1947) came first and the Boulder conference (1949) adopted it; do not give Boulder the date 1947. The VA began funding clinical training in 1946; the NIMH, created by the National Mental Health Act of 1946, opened in 1949. Shakow asked for competence in three areas: assessment (diagnosis), therapy and research.

The Boulder conference and the scientist-practitioner model

Key study: the Boulder conference (1949)

The question: what should a clinical psychologist be, and how should one be trained? The meeting: from 20 August to 3 September 1949, 73 people met at the University of Colorado in Boulder for the Boulder Conference on Graduate Education in Clinical Psychology, sponsored by the APA and paid for by the United States Public Health Service. They represented academic and applied psychology, medicine and education. The method: two weeks of debate, committee by committee, on every aspect of training from admission to internship. The result: the conference adopted the essentials of the Shakow report and gave the model a name, the scientist-practitioner model; its proceedings were published by Victor Raimy as Training in Clinical Psychology (1950). The conclusion: the clinical psychologist is to be both a scientist and a practitioner, earning a research PhD in a university department with a year of supervised internship. The criticism: from the start, doubters asked whether one person could be fully trained in both roles, and later surveys suggested that most graduates went into practice and did little research.

The Boulder conference gave the profession its founding charter. The scientist-practitioner model, also called the Boulder model, holds that a clinical psychologist should be trained as both a researcher and a practitioner, and that each role should feed the other. Practice raises questions (why is this client not improving?), research answers them (which treatment works, and for whom?), and the clinician then applies the answer and checks whether it worked. Training therefore combines a research doctorate with supervised clinical work and a full-year internship. A clinician does not have to run large experiments to count as a scientist-practitioner. David H. Barlow (born 1942), Steven C. Hayes (born 1948) and Rosemery O. Nelson, in The Scientist Practitioner (1984), described three ways of filling the role: as a consumer of research, who keeps up with the evidence and uses the best-supported assessments and treatments; as an evaluator, who measures whether her own assessments and treatments are working; and as a creator of research, who carries out studies in the clinic that add to what is known.

Clinical link

Faisal is a clinical psychologist in a district hospital in Karnataka. Before treating a young man with obsessive-compulsive disorder he reads the evidence and chooses exposure and response prevention (Section 4.3), because it has the strongest support: he is a consumer of research. He gives every client a standard symptom rating scale at the first, sixth and twelfth sessions and changes course when the scores do not fall: he is an evaluator. And with two colleagues he runs a small study comparing a group programme for caregivers with the usual single information session: he is a creator. None of this needs a laboratory. It is exactly what the Boulder conference meant.

The model has been criticised from the start. Critics doubted that one training could make students expert in two demanding activities, and pointed out that the combination stretched doctoral training to five years or more, including the internship. Surveys in the following decades found that most graduates of Boulder programmes went into practice and published little, which suggested that the "scientist" half was often more an ideal than a reality. And many students who wanted only to practise felt pushed through research training they would never use. Yet the model survived its critics. It remains the basis of most American clinical PhD programmes and, as Section 1.5 shows, it is also the ideal behind training in India.

Exam trap

The scientist-practitioner model was adopted at Boulder in 1949, but the blueprint was Shakow's, and the conference report was published by Raimy (1950). It leads to the PhD. The three roles described by Barlow, Hayes and Nelson (1984) are consumer, evaluator and creator of research; "administrator" and "teacher" are not among them.

Vail and the practitioner-scholar model

By the 1960s the strain was showing. Most students entered clinical programmes because they wanted to practise; many of their professors valued research above everything; and the public wanted more therapists than research-heavy programmes were producing. A 1965 conference in Chicago recommended that the profession experiment with programmes geared more directly to practice. In 1968 the University of Illinois, under Donald R. Peterson, opened the first programme leading to a new degree, the Doctor of Psychology (PsyD), a professional doctorate modelled on the medical degree rather than on the research PhD. In 1969 the California School of Professional Psychology was founded as the first freestanding professional school of psychology, independent of any university department, and others soon followed.

The Vail conference of 1973, formally the National Conference on Levels and Patterns of Professional Training in Psychology, held at Vail in Colorado, gave this movement its charter. It accepted that when the emphasis of training is on delivering clinical services, the appropriate degree is the PsyD, and it recognised professional schools as a legitimate home for that training. Its model came to be called the practitioner-scholar model, or the Vail model: the clinical psychologist is first a practitioner, trained intensively in assessment and therapy, and a scholar in the sense of being an informed, critical consumer of research rather than necessarily a producer of it. PsyD programmes typically demand more supervised clinical work and less research than Boulder-model PhD programmes, and their dissertations are often smaller, applied projects. Today about half of the clinical psychology doctoral students in the United States are in PsyD programmes.

The argument did not end at Vail. Critics of some professional schools pointed to large classes, high fees and student debt, thin research training and weaker success in obtaining accredited internships; defenders replied that the public needed well-trained practitioners and that the PhD route had never produced enough of them. Later meetings tried to steady the field: a national conference on graduate education in psychology at Salt Lake City, Utah, in 1987, and a 1990 conference at Gainesville, Florida, which reaffirmed the scientist-practitioner model for PhD training.

Exam trap

Boulder, 1949: scientist-practitioner model, PhD, research and practice in equal measure. Vail, 1973: practitioner-scholar model, PsyD, practice first. The first PsyD programme (Illinois, 1968) came before Vail, which endorsed what already existed. Salt Lake City (1987) and Gainesville (1990) were later conferences, and Adelphi is a university, not the site of any of them. "Boulder model" and "scientist-practitioner model" are the same thing; so are "Vail model" and "practitioner-scholar model".

The clinical science model

If Vail pulled training towards practice, the third model pulled it hard back towards science. In 1990, in his address as president of a section of Division 12, Richard M. McFall of Indiana University argued that clinical psychology had drifted away from its scientific base, and in 1991 he published the argument as "Manifesto for a science of clinical psychology". Its Cardinal Principle was blunt: scientific clinical psychology is the only legitimate and acceptable form of clinical psychology. From it McFall drew two corollaries. First, psychological services should not be offered to the public, except under strict experimental control, until the exact nature of the service has been described clearly, its claimed benefits stated explicitly and confirmed scientifically, and its possible harmful side effects ruled out. Second, the primary and overriding aim of doctoral training should be to produce the most competent clinical scientists possible.

This is the clinical science model: clinical psychology is an applied science, and its practitioners should be scientists whose practice is itself an application of research, rather than practitioners who also know some research. In 1995, after a 1994 meeting at Indiana University, research-intensive programmes formed the Academy of Psychological Clinical Science to promote the model, and in the late 2000s a separate accrediting body for such programmes, the Psychological Clinical Science Accreditation System, followed. The model's influence reaches well beyond these programmes: the movement towards empirically supported treatments and evidence-based practice, described in Section 8.8, is its close relative. Its critics argue that it undervalues clinical skill and the judgement needed for clients who look nothing like the participants of research trials. Table 1.9 sets the three models side by side.

Table 1.9 The three models of training in clinical psychology.
ModelOriginDegreePlace of researchTypical home
Scientist-practitioner (Boulder model)Shakow report 1947; Boulder conference 1949PhDAn equal partner of practice: the clinician consumes, evaluates and creates researchUniversity psychology departments
Practitioner-scholar (Vail model)Illinois PsyD 1968; Vail conference 1973PsyDPractice first; the clinician is an informed consumer of researchProfessional schools and some universities
Clinical scienceMcFall's manifesto 1991; Academy of Psychological Clinical Science 1995PhDResearch first; practice is applied scienceResearch-intensive universities

A worked example makes the differences concrete. Picture three Indian psychology graduates applying for doctoral training in the United States. The first wants to run treatment trials and one day lead a university research group; a clinical science programme, small, selective and centred on research, suits her best. The second wants to spend his career treating patients in a hospital, but also to study his own patients and publish; a Boulder-model PhD fits that plan. The third wants to open a private practice and expects to read research rather than produce it; a PsyD programme built on the Vail model would give him the most supervised clinical hours. Notice that all three would end up licensed as clinical psychologists. The models differ in emphasis, not in the profession they lead to.

Exam trap

Three models, three labels: scientist-practitioner (Boulder 1949), practitioner-scholar (Vail 1973, PsyD) and clinical science (McFall 1991; Academy of Psychological Clinical Science 1995). The clinical science model is not another name for the scientist-practitioner model: it gives research priority over practice, whereas Boulder gives them equal weight.

Licensing and the prescription privileges debate

A profession is defined as much by law as by training. In the United States psychologists are regulated by the individual states, and two kinds of law are distinguished. A certification law protects the title: only qualified people may call themselves psychologists, though others may still offer similar services under other names. A licensure law protects the practice itself: only licensed people may provide psychological services. Connecticut passed the first certification law for psychologists in 1945, and by 1977 every state had a law of one kind or the other. The usual requirements today are a doctorate from an accredited programme, a period of supervised experience (part of it after the doctorate), a pass in the national Examination for Professional Practice in Psychology, often a state examination on law and ethics, and continuing education to keep the licence.

The sharpest recent argument concerns prescription privileges, the right of suitably trained psychologists to prescribe psychiatric medication. The idea took practical form in the 1990s, when the United States Department of Defense ran a demonstration project that trained a small group of military psychologists to prescribe. Guam, an American territory, passed the first prescribing law in 1999, and New Mexico became the first state to allow prescribing psychologists in 2002, followed by Louisiana in 2004, Illinois in 2014, Iowa in 2016, Idaho in 2017, Colorado in 2023 and Utah in 2024, and bills have since been debated in other states. These laws usually require a licensed psychologist to complete a postdoctoral master's degree in clinical psychopharmacology, pass an examination and work for a period in collaboration with physicians. Supporters argue that psychiatrists are scarce, especially in rural areas, that nurse practitioners and others already prescribe with less training in mental health, and that one professional managing both medication and psychotherapy would serve clients better. Opponents, led by organised medicine but including many psychologists, argue that a few hundred hours of coursework cannot replace medical training in physiology, drug interactions and physical illness, and that the move would pull psychology away from its distinctive strength, which is psychological treatment.

Indian context

In India prescribing medicine is reserved for registered medical practitioners, and clinical psychologists do not prescribe. They work alongside psychiatrists in the multidisciplinary team (Section 1.1), and this division of labour, medication from the psychiatrist and psychological assessment and treatment from the psychologist, is part of the professional identity described in Section 1.5.

Exam trap

New Mexico (2002) was the first state to give psychologists prescription privileges and Louisiana (2004) the second; Guam (1999) was earlier but is a territory, not a state. Connecticut (1945) passed the first law regulating psychologists. Certification protects the title; licensure protects the practice.

1.5 Clinical Psychology in IndiaMedium yield · 4 Qs

The Western story was driven by schoolchildren, tests, war and government money. The Indian story has a different cast: a psychology department in Calcutta, a physician-psychoanalyst who wrote to Freud, two great mental hospitals at Ranchi and Bangalore, a small professional association, and finally an act of Parliament that decides who may practise as a clinical psychologist. If you intend to practise in India, the last part of the story is the one that will govern your working life, so this section ends with how a clinical psychologist trains and registers today and what the work demands. Figure 1.7 gives the milestones.

Beginnings in Calcutta: Sen Gupta and Bose

Modern psychology in India began at the University of Calcutta. In 1916 Narendra Nath Sen Gupta (1889 to 1944), who had earned a doctorate at Harvard and had studied there with the experimental psychologist Hugo Münsterberg, set up the Department of Experimental Psychology at Calcutta, the first independent psychology department in the country (some histories date it to 1915), with a laboratory on Wundt's model. Sen Gupta is often called the father of modern psychology in India. The department's most remarkable early student, however, was a physician.

Girindrasekhar Bose (1887 to 1953) qualified in medicine at Calcutta Medical College in 1910, but he became fascinated by the mind, took a master's degree in the new psychology department and stayed on to lecture there. In 1921 he was awarded a doctorate (a DSc) for a thesis titled "Concept of Repression", widely described as the first doctorate in psychology from an Indian university. In it he set out his theory of opposite wishes: every wish has an opposite wish paired with it in the mind, an active and a passive form (the wish to beat goes with the wish to be beaten, the wish to see with the wish to be seen), and repression keeps one member of the pair out of awareness while the other is expressed. The theory drew on Indian philosophy as well as on clinical observation. Bose sent his thesis to Sigmund Freud, which began a long correspondence; Freud was pleased that psychoanalysis had reached so far.

In 1922 Bose founded the Indian Psychoanalytic Society in Calcutta and was its president from then until his death in 1953. The society was soon accepted into the international psychoanalytic movement, and among its early members was Owen Berkeley-Hill of the Ranchi European Mental Hospital, whom you will meet next. Its journal, Samiksha, began in 1947, and in 1940 Bose opened a nursing home in Calcutta that later became the Lumbini Park Mental Hospital. Bose matters to clinical psychology because he showed, very early, that the psychological treatment of mental disorder could be practised and theorised in India, by Indians, in conversation with Indian culture rather than as a simple import.

Exam trap

Sen Gupta, 1916: the first psychology department (experimental psychology, University of Calcutta). Bose, 1922: the Indian Psychoanalytic Society. Bose's 1921 doctorate was on repression, and his theory is the theory of opposite wishes. Do not credit Bose with the first department or Sen Gupta with psychoanalysis. The journal Samiksha (1947) belongs to Bose's society.

Ranchi: from European asylum to the Central Institute of Psychiatry

On 17 May 1918 the British opened a mental hospital at Kanke, on the outskirts of Ranchi, called the Ranchi European Lunatic Asylum. As the name says, it was for Europeans only: until independence it admitted only patients of European or American parentage. In 1922 it came under a board of trustees drawn from several provinces and was renamed the European Mental Hospital. Its transformation was the work of Owen Berkeley-Hill (1879 to 1944), an officer of the Indian Medical Service and an enthusiast for psychoanalysis, who took charge in October 1919 and stayed for about fifteen years. In 1922 the hospital opened an occupational therapy department (the Institute counts it the first in the country) and built a separate block for psychological assessment and treatment; from 1924 psychoanalysis was being used to treat personality disorders, and habit-formation charts were in use. When he left, Berkeley-Hill wrote with pardonable pride that "the miserable bear-garden I had taken charge of in October 1919 had become the finest mental hospital in Asia, and a great deal finer than many mental hospitals in Europe."

After independence the hospital opened its doors to Indian patients and, after several changes of name and management, became the Central Institute of Psychiatry (CIP), run today by the Directorate General of Health Services under the Union Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. Its department of clinical psychology is the oldest independent one in India. The Institute's history dates it to 1948 (its department page says it officially started in 1949) and claims for it the first clinical psychology laboratory in the country. In 1962 the CIP began a postgraduate training course, the Diploma in Medical and Social Psychology (DM&SP), which later became the M.Phil in Medical and Social Psychology and then the M.Phil in Clinical Psychology; a PhD in clinical psychology followed in 1972. The CIP also trains psychiatric social workers and psychiatric nurses, so a trainee there learns her profession inside a working multidisciplinary team. (The Institute also records that the first draft of the bill that became the Mental Health Act 1987 was written at Ranchi in 1949; that Act is taught in Section 1.10.)

Exam trap

The hospital opened in 1918 as the Ranchi European Lunatic Asylum, was renamed the European Mental Hospital in 1922 and is today the Central Institute of Psychiatry, under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. Its clinical psychology department dates from 1948 (some accounts say 1949) and its Diploma in Medical and Social Psychology from 1962. Berkeley-Hill ran Ranchi; Bose worked in Calcutta.

Bangalore: from the All India Institute of Mental Health to NIMHANS

The second great centre grew from an asylum founded in Bangalore in 1847. In 1925 it was renamed the Mysore Government Mental Hospital, and the psychiatrist M. V. Govindaswamy helped to make it a place of innovation: insulin coma therapy arrived in 1935, and the country's first leucotomy was performed there in 1942. After independence, following the recommendations of a mental health advisory committee in 1945 and of the Bhore Committee in 1946, the Government of India sanctioned an All India Institute of Mental Health (AIIMH) beside the state hospital on 1 April 1954. The Union Health Minister, Rajkumari Amrit Kaur, inaugurated it on 6 August 1954, and Govindaswamy became its director.

From the start the AIIMH was a teaching institute. In 1955 it began its first postgraduate courses: the Diploma in Psychological Medicine for doctors and a Diploma in Medical Psychology for psychology graduates, the beginning of formal clinical psychology training in Bangalore. A Diploma in Psychiatric Social Work followed in the late 1960s. As at Ranchi, the psychology diploma later grew into a two-year M.Phil, and for decades the M.Phil in Clinical Psychology from Bangalore, Ranchi and the centres modelled on them was the standard qualification of a clinical psychologist in India. On 27 December 1974 the state mental hospital and the AIIMH were amalgamated to form the National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS), and its Community Psychiatry Unit was launched in the same year. NIMHANS was declared a deemed university in 1994 and an Institute of National Importance in 2012 (by a Gazette notification of 14 September 2012). It also carried out the National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016, whose findings are given in Section 1.10.

Exam trap

Three NIMHANS dates are regularly swapped: 1954, the All India Institute of Mental Health; 1974, NIMHANS formed (and its Community Psychiatry Unit started); 2012, Institute of National Importance (it had been a deemed university since 1994). Formal clinical psychology training in Bangalore began in 1955. None of these events happened in 1968.

A profession organises: the Indian Association of Clinical Psychologists

By the 1960s India had a small but growing number of clinical psychologists trained at Ranchi and Bangalore, working in mental hospitals and in a few general hospitals and universities. They faced the same problem that American clinicians had faced in 1917. They were not academic psychologists, whose associations were about teaching and research, and they were not doctors, whose Indian Psychiatric Society, founded in the late 1940s, spoke for psychiatrists. In 1968, after several rounds of discussion, they registered the Indian Association of Clinical Psychologists (IACP) as a society under the Societies Registration Act of 1860, and in 1974 the association began publishing the Indian Journal of Clinical Psychology. A professional association gives a profession a voice. It holds conferences, publishes a journal, sets expectations of conduct for its members and speaks for them in debates about training, posts and regulation. The IACP has done all of these for more than half a century.

Exam trap

"In 1968, which event contributed to the development of clinical psychology in India?" The answer is the formation of the IACP. The tempting options are real events of other years: the NIMHANS Community Psychiatry Unit began in 1974, and NIMHANS became an Institute of National Importance in 2012. The PsyD option is clever bait, because 1968 is the year of the first PsyD programme, at the University of Illinois in the United States (Section 1.4); India's recognised qualifications in clinical psychology are the M.Phil, the new master's and the RCI diploma, not a PsyD.

The Rehabilitation Council of India

Until the 1990s nothing in Indian law stopped anyone from setting up as a psychologist, a counsellor or even a clinical psychologist. Doctors had the Medical Council of India; psychologists had no council at all. The body that eventually filled the gap came from an unexpected direction, disability rehabilitation. Why there? Because the professionals who work with persons with disabilities (speech therapists, special teachers, rehabilitation engineers, vocational counsellors, and psychologists who assess and rehabilitate people with intellectual disability, brain injury and mental illness) needed common standards of training, and the government chose to regulate them together under one council.

The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI) was set up in 1986 as a registered society. A society's powers are limited, so in September 1992 Parliament passed the Rehabilitation Council of India Act, and in 1993 the RCI became a statutory body, that is, a body created by an act of Parliament and drawing its powers from it. In 2000 Parliament amended the Act to make it more broad-based, adding the monitoring of training and the promotion of research in rehabilitation and special education to the Council's work. The RCI functions under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, through its Department of Empowerment of Persons with Disabilities (Divyangjan). It is not under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, which runs the CIP, NIMHANS and the mental health programmes, nor under the Ministry of Education, which oversees universities through the University Grants Commission (UGC). Table 1.10 sorts out who regulates what.

Table 1.10 Who regulates what: the bodies an Indian clinical psychologist deals with.
Body or lawMinistryWhat it does for the profession
Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI Act 1992, amended 2000)Social Justice and Empowerment (Department of Empowerment of Persons with Disabilities)Recognises clinical psychology courses and institutions; keeps the Central Rehabilitation Register
Disability laws and the National Trust (Section 1.10)Social Justice and EmpowermentDisability rights, benefits and guardianship
CIP Ranchi, NIMHANS and other central institutesHealth and Family WelfareTraining, services and research in mental health
Mental Healthcare Act 2017, National Mental Health Programme, Tele-MANASHealth and Family WelfareMental health services, patients' rights, regulation of mental health establishments
University Grants CommissionEducationDegree regulations for universities, including the withdrawal of the M.Phil

What does the RCI actually do? Its stated objectives include regulating training policies and programmes in rehabilitation, standardising training courses, prescribing minimum standards of education and training for each category of professional, recognising the universities and institutions that run approved courses (and foreign qualifications on a reciprocal basis), promoting research, encouraging continuing education, and maintaining the Central Rehabilitation Register (CRR), the official register of qualified rehabilitation professionals and personnel. The Act names clinical psychologists among the rehabilitation professionals whose training and registration it governs, alongside audiologists and speech therapists, special teachers, rehabilitation engineers, vocational counsellors and others. (Section 1.1 explains who may call themselves a clinical psychologist; this is the machinery behind that rule.)

For a clinical psychologist the RCI means three things in practice. The course you take must be one the RCI recognises, run at an institution it has approved. On qualifying you must apply to be entered on the Central Rehabilitation Register, which gives you a registration number. And you must keep the registration alive by renewing it periodically, for which you earn points by attending approved Continuing Rehabilitation Education (CRE) programmes. Registration is not a formality. Under the RCI Act, practising as a rehabilitation professional without being on the Register is a punishable offence, so an unregistered person who sets up as a clinical psychologist is breaking the law. Section 1.10 gives the clause and its penalty, and shows how the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 builds RCI qualifications into its own legal definition of a clinical psychologist. Before you accept a referral from, take a job with or train under someone who calls himself a clinical psychologist, it is reasonable to ask for the CRR number, just as a patient may check a doctor's medical registration.

Exam trap

The RCI's dates come as a set: 1986, registered society; 1992, RCI Act; 1993, statutory body; 2000, amendment. It is under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, not Health and Family Welfare, Education, or Labour and Employment. The Act that requires clinical psychologists to be registered with the RCI is the RCI Act 1992, not the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (which uses RCI qualifications to define a clinical psychologist but does not create the Register), not the Persons with Disabilities Act 1995, and not the 1987 law, which was the Mental Health Act (there is no "Mental Healthcare Act 1987"). Years such as 1947, 1987, 2002 and 2016 are distractors; 2016 is the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act.

How a clinical psychologist trains and registers today

For decades the route was simple to state. After a master's degree in psychology you competed for a seat in a two-year, full-time M.Phil in Clinical Psychology (at Ranchi first called the M.Phil in Medical and Social Psychology) at an RCI-recognised centre, completed it, and registered with the RCI as a Clinical Psychologist. The M.Phil was demanding: clinical postings across psychiatry, neurology and child and adult services, supervised assessments and therapies written up as case records, and a dissertation. Then the National Education Policy 2020 announced that the M.Phil would be discontinued, and the UGC withdrew the degree, allowing only the two professional M.Phil programmes, in clinical psychology and in psychiatric social work, a limited transition period (a 2024 UGC notice let them admit students until the 2025-26 session). The profession needed a new qualification that kept the clinical training intact.

The RCI's answer is a two-year, full-time master's programme introduced from the academic session 2025-26 "in place of present M.Phil. Clinical Psychology" and first named the M.A. (Clinical Psychology), RCI regulated. By an office memorandum of 3 May 2026, following a resolution of its General Council, the RCI renamed it the Master in Clinical Psychology (M.Clin.Psy), the new name applying from the start of the course (the undergraduate B.Sc. Clinical Psychology was renamed the B.Clin.Psy at the same time). The guidelines state the aim as preparing a trained clinical psychologist "to function as an independent clinician", and they spell the structure out in detail:

  • Entry. A four-year BA or BSc in psychology from a UGC-recognised university with at least 55 percent marks (50 percent for SC and ST candidates). Graduates of three-year degrees must first pass a fourth-year bridge course, or the first year of a master's programme in psychology, also with 55 percent.
  • Selection. An entrance examination in which the written test carries 70 percent of the weight and the interview 30 percent. Each centre's intake is capped by its number of full-time clinical psychology faculty and by the number of patients its department sees each month.
  • Year 1. Papers on the psychosocial foundations of behaviour and psychopathology, on psychiatry, and on statistics and research methodology; a practical examination in psychological assessment and clinical placement; and five full-length psychodiagnostic records, one of them on a child and one a neuropsychological assessment.
  • Year 2. Papers on the biological determinants of behaviour and neuropsychology, on behavioural medicine, and on psychotherapy and counselling; a practical examination in psychological therapy and clinical placement; five full-length psychotherapy records, one of them with a child; and a research thesis, which must include ethics committee clearance and a plagiarism report.
  • Rules. At least 80 percent attendance in each year, and completion of the course within four years of enrolment.

Look at what the records demand and you will see the training philosophy. Each psychodiagnostic record follows a fixed outline: socio-demographic data, presenting complaints, history of the present and past illness, family and personal history, premorbid personality, physical findings, the mental status examination, a diagnostic formulation and differential diagnosis, sociocultural and contextual factors, and a management plan, followed by the rationale for testing, the tests used, behavioural observations during testing, the findings and an impression. Each therapy record must state the formulation, the rationale for the intervention, its short- and long-term objectives, the techniques used, the course of therapy, any change of plan with its reasons, and the outcome, and every record is signed by the supervising consultant. In effect every case is written up as a small study. (The interview, the mental status examination and case formulation are taught in Chapter 2.)

Two other qualifications complete the picture. The Professional Diploma in Clinical Psychology (PDCP), a one-year RCI course for holders of an MA or MSc in psychology with at least 55 percent, was introduced to ease the shortage of clinicians. Its holders register on the CRR as Clinical Psychologist (Associate). The RCI's own syllabus describes them as competent for a broad range of clinical services in non-academic service settings, but not for independent responsibility in teaching, training or supervising clinical psychology students, which remains with fully qualified clinical psychologists. A doctorate (PhD) in clinical psychology, offered by centres such as the CIP and NIMHANS, is the usual route into senior academic and research posts, but it is a research degree added to the professional qualification, not a substitute for it. Table 1.11 sets the routes side by side, and Figure 1.8 traces the two that are open to a new student.

Table 1.11 Routes to registration as a clinical psychologist in India.
QualificationEntryLengthRegistered on the CRR asStatus
M.Phil in Clinical Psychology (earlier M.Phil in Medical and Social Psychology)MA or MSc in psychology2 years, full timeClinical PsychologistBeing phased out after NEP 2020
M.A. (Clinical Psychology), renamed Master in Clinical Psychology (M.Clin.Psy) in 20264-year BA or BSc in psychology, or a 3-year degree plus a bridge course or first year of an MA2 years, full timeClinical Psychologist (it replaces the M.Phil)From the 2025-26 session
Professional Diploma in Clinical Psychology (PDCP)MA or MSc in psychology1 yearClinical Psychologist (Associate)Clinical service posts; no independent teaching or supervision of trainees

Push one applicant through the system. Tanvi has a three-year BA (Honours) in psychology and wants to be a clinical psychologist. She cannot enter the M.Clin.Psy directly; she must first complete the fourth-year bridge course or the first year of an MA in psychology, with at least 55 percent. She then sits the entrance examinations of RCI-recognised centres, where the written test counts for 70 percent and the interview for 30, spends two years in full-time clinical training, submits her ten records and her thesis, and passes the final examinations. Only then may she apply for entry on the Central Rehabilitation Register, and only after that may she practise as a clinical psychologist, collecting CRE points to keep her registration current. Had she instead completed an MA in psychology and taken the one-year PDCP, she would be registered as a Clinical Psychologist (Associate), able to work in clinical service posts but not to take independent charge of training clinical psychology students. Because these rules changed so recently, and parts of the transition from the M.Phil have been challenged in the courts, the safe habit is to check the current RCI notices and each institute's admission notice before applying.

Exam trap

The RCI's two-year master's replaces the M.Phil in Clinical Psychology. It began with the 2025-26 session as the M.A. (Clinical Psychology) and was renamed Master in Clinical Psychology (M.Clin.Psy) in May 2026. The change followed NEP 2020, which discontinued the M.Phil. The PDCP takes one year and leads to registration as Clinical Psychologist (Associate), not as a full Clinical Psychologist.

Professional identity: the scientist-practitioner in an Indian clinic

What kind of professional is all this training meant to produce? The RCI writes its answer into the objectives of the M.Clin.Psy. On completion a trainee should be able to diagnose and assess mental health problems; understand the problems of children, adolescents, adults and older adults within a psychological framework that takes psychosocial and contextual factors into account, and treat them; apply psychological methods to the rehabilitation of people with mental health problems and disabilities; work with the psychological side of physical diseases; work with communities to promote health and well-being; carry out research, including research on stigma, discrimination and other social issues; teach and train; take on administrative and supervisory responsibilities; and give expert testimony in courts of law. Read that list against Section 1.4 and you will recognise it. The Indian clinical psychologist is meant to be a scientist-practitioner in the Boulder sense: a clinician trained in assessment and therapy who also produces research (both the M.Phil and the new master's require a thesis) and reads it critically.

In practice the Indian identity has three features that set it apart from the American picture. First, Indian clinical psychologists have mostly trained and worked inside medical institutions, alongside psychiatrists, psychiatric social workers and psychiatric nurses. The profession grew up in the hospitals of Ranchi and Bangalore, not in a school clinic, and its training still requires postings across psychiatry, neurology and general medicine. Second, it is a master's-level profession: the registrable qualification is a two-year professional master's (formerly the M.Phil), not a doctorate, and the PhD is an additional research degree rather than the licence to practise. Third, the clinical psychologist in India does not prescribe, so her distinctive contribution is psychological: assessment, formulation, psychotherapy, rehabilitation and research. How the professions in the team differ is set out in Section 1.1.

What working in India demands

Imagine that you have registered and taken your first job in a district hospital. What will you meet that a textbook written in Boston would not prepare you for? At least five things.

Too few of you. The World Health Organization's Mental Health Atlas of 2017 counted about 0.29 psychiatrists and 0.07 psychologists per 100,000 people in India, which works out at roughly one psychologist for every 1.4 million people. Most people who need help never reach any mental health professional; the size of this treatment gap, measured by the National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016, is given in Section 1.10. A clinical psychologist in India therefore cannot think only about the clients in front of her. She must also think about the many who will never come.

The family in the room. Indian clients usually come with relatives, decisions about treatment, work and marriage are often family decisions, and confidentiality has to be negotiated with the client and the family rather than simply assumed. Section 8.11 shows how Indian therapists have adapted psychotherapy to the joint family and to clients who expect an active, directive therapist, and Chapter 7 teaches family therapy.

Other healers first. Many families take a troubled relative first to a temple, a dargah, a church, an astrologer or a faith healer, because such help carries no psychiatric label and fits their own explanation of the illness. The clinical psychologist is often the second or third stop, and treatment goes better when she asks respectfully about these explanations and earlier treatments instead of dismissing them. The dark side of this pathway became a national scandal in August 2001, when 28 people with mental illness who were chained at a faith-healing shrine at Erwadi in Tamil Nadu died in a fire; the tragedy and what followed are described in Section 9.6.

Stigma. Mental illness can damage a family's standing and a young person's marriage prospects, so clients may present with bodily complaints, play down their symptoms or stop treatment early. Stigma and anti-stigma work are taught in Section 9.6.

New roles. Because specialists are so few, some of the most influential Indian work of recent decades has trained non-specialists to deliver psychological treatment, most famously the lay counsellors of the MANAS trial in Goa, led by the psychiatrist Vikram Patel, with the grandmothers of Zimbabwe's Friendship Bench as an African comparison. This task-shifting (Section 9.6) gives the clinical psychologist a new job as trainer, supervisor and evaluator of other people's therapy. Telephone and video services such as Tele-MANAS (Section 1.10) add another. All of this work is carried out within the ethical principles and laws taught in Sections 1.8 to 1.10.

Clinical link

Joseph, 24, is brought to a hospital outpatient department by his parents and an uncle after eight months at a prayer and healing centre, where they took him when he stopped sleeping and began hearing voices. The psychiatrist starts medication. The clinical psychologist's work begins with the family. She asks, without contempt, what they believe caused the illness and what was done at the healing centre; she agrees with Joseph which parts of his sessions will be shared with his parents; she explains the illness in the family's own words; and she plans psychoeducation and relapse prevention with the uncle, who has the most influence at home. Each of these steps (respect for the family's explanation, negotiated confidentiality, working through the family) is part of the professional competence an Indian clinician needs, and none of it appears in Witmer's 1907 definition.

Exam trap

Keep the places straight: the Erwadi fire (August 2001) was in Tamil Nadu; the MANAS lay-counsellor trial was in Goa; the Friendship Bench is in Zimbabwe. In the 2017 Mental Health Atlas India had about 0.29 psychiatrists and 0.07 psychologists per 100,000 people; the prevalence and treatment-gap figures come from the National Mental Health Survey (Section 1.10).

1.6 What Counts as AbnormalLower yield · not asked yet

Meet three people. Sunita is a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru. Since her mother died eight months ago and she was transferred to a school two hours from home, she has been waking at three in the morning, has lost six kilograms, cries in the staff room, and tells her husband that her pupils would be better off with someone else. Imran is a 23-year-old engineering student in Bengaluru. For the past ten days he has slept two or three hours a night, talked so fast that his friends cannot follow him, and paid an advance on a car he cannot afford; he says he has never felt better in his life and is annoyed that everyone keeps asking what is wrong with him. Devika is a 41-year-old farmer's wife from a village near Mandya. Every year at the festival of the village goddess she sways, her voice deepens, and the goddess is said to speak through her; her neighbours queue to put their questions to the goddess, and the next morning Devika is back in the fields, remembering little of the night.

Which of these three people has a mental disorder? Most readers answer "Sunita" at once, hesitate over Imran (he is not suffering, so is he ill?), and feel uneasy about Devika (a goddess speaking through you would alarm a Bengaluru office, but not a village festival). That unease is the subject of this section. Clinical psychology cannot begin until somebody decides that a pattern of behaviour is a problem, and that decision settles who is offered treatment, who may be admitted to hospital against their wishes, who receives a disability certificate, and who carries a label for the rest of their life. Over the last century five main criteria have been proposed for making it. Each captures something true, and each, used alone, fails. Pay attention to the failures; they teach as much as the successes.

The statistical criterion

The simplest answer is that abnormal means unusual. On the statistical criterion, a behaviour or trait is abnormal if it is rare in the population, that is, if it lies far from the average. The idea comes from the measurement tradition that began with Francis Galton (Section 1.4), and it works best for traits that can be measured on a scale and that follow the bell-shaped normal distribution. Intelligence is the textbook example. Intelligence tests are constructed so that scores have a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15. A score below 70 lies two standard deviations below the mean, and only about 2 percent of people score that low. Very low measured intelligence, together with difficulties in everyday adaptive functioning that begin during development, is part of the definition of intellectual disability, so here the statistical criterion seems to do its job.

Now look at the other end of the same curve (Figure 1.9). A score above 130 is exactly as rare as a score below 70, yet nobody calls a gifted child disordered. The statistical criterion cannot tell rare-and-harmful from rare-and-admirable, because rarity carries no judgement of value: an Olympic sprinter, a chess grandmaster and a singer with perfect pitch are all statistically abnormal. The criterion fails in the opposite direction too. Some disorders are common. Depression is sometimes called the common cold of psychopathology, and depressive and anxiety disorders together affect a large share of every population at some point in life (the Indian figures from the National Mental Health Survey are given in Section 1.10). Being common does not make them any less a problem. Finally, the criterion supplies no principled cut-off. Why two standard deviations and not one and a half? Where the line falls is a decision made by people, not a fact of nature.

Exam trap

"Abnormality is deviation from the average" is the statistical criterion; "abnormality is deviation from what society expects" is the social-norm criterion. Its classic weaknesses are always the same two: it would call desirable rarities (genius, exceptional talent) abnormal, and it would miss disorders that are common, such as depression.

Breaking social norms

A second answer is that abnormal behaviour is behaviour that breaks the rules of a society. Every community has social norms, the explicit and unspoken rules about how its members should behave, and on the social-norm criterion behaviour that violates them, especially in ways that disturb other people, is abnormal. This criterion explains why we notice the man who argues loudly with nobody on a bus or the woman who undresses in a temple courtyard, and it has one real strength: it forces the clinician to judge behaviour against the person's own cultural background rather than against the clinician's.

That strength is also its weakness, because norms differ from place to place and change over time, a problem known as cultural relativism. Devika's trance is the perfect illustration: in her village it is expected and valued, while in a city office the same behaviour would frighten everyone. The neuroscientist Robert Sapolsky, who spent many years studying baboons in East Africa, has told how he helped to subdue and take to a health centre a Maasai woman who had become violent, killed a goat with her own hands and was hearing voices. Curious about how the Maasai recognised madness, he asked his Maasai friend Rhoda how she knew the woman was crazy. Because she killed a goat, said Rhoda. But the Maasai kill goats all the time, Sapolsky objected. Only the men kill goats, she replied. And the voices? The Maasai, after all, hear voices in their ceremonies before long cattle drives. "But she hears voices at the wrong time." In that one sentence Rhoda summed up the cultural-relativist position: whether an experience is pathological depends partly on when, where and by whom it is had.

Norms can also be used to punish people who are merely inconvenient. In 1851 the American physician Samuel Cartwright described "drapetomania", a supposed mental disease that made enslaved Africans run away from their owners. In the Soviet Union, political dissidents were diagnosed with "sluggish schizophrenia" and confined in psychiatric hospitals. The most consequential example is homosexuality. It was listed as a mental disorder in the first two editions of the American Psychiatric Association's Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM) until, in December 1973, the Association's board of trustees voted to remove it. Gay activists had campaigned for the change, and research had undermined the old view: in 1957 the psychologist Evelyn Hooker gave projective tests such as the Rorschach to matched groups of gay and heterosexual men, and expert judges could not tell the two groups apart. Homosexuality met no criterion of abnormality except a social one. The World Health Organization removed it from the International Classification of Diseases (ICD) with ICD-10, approved in 1990. Nothing about gay people had changed; the norms had.

Indian context

Indian law has written the lesson of the social-norm criterion into statute. Section 3(3) of the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 says that mental illness shall not be determined on the basis of a person's political, economic or social status, membership of a cultural, racial or religious group, or "non-conformity with moral, social, cultural, work or political values or religious beliefs prevailing in a person's community". A woman who refuses an arranged marriage, a man who leaves his family's religion and a young person who is gay cannot be called mentally ill for that reason. In 2018 the Supreme Court, in Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India, read down Section 377 of the Indian Penal Code so that consensual sex between adults of the same sex was no longer a crime, and the Indian Psychiatric Society stated the same year that homosexuality is not a psychiatric disorder. The Act as a whole is taught in Section 1.10.

Maladaptiveness and personal distress

If rarity and rule-breaking cannot define abnormality, perhaps its consequences can. On the maladaptiveness criterion, behaviour is abnormal when it interferes with the person's ability to function in daily life: to work or study, look after themselves, keep relationships going and pursue their goals. The emphasis moves from how a behaviour looks to what it does. Sunita's sleeplessness and hopelessness matter because she can no longer teach; a man who washes his hands for three hours a day matters because he is about to lose his job. Maladaptiveness also explains why a trait that is perfectly ordinary in one degree becomes a problem in another. Most people are shy at a wedding where they know nobody, but a student so shy that she cannot ask a question in class, eat in the hostel canteen or face a viva is impaired.

The criterion has two problems. First, plenty of behaviour damages the person who does it without being a disorder: a mountaineer who climbs without oxygen and a businessman who works eighteen-hour days both put their health at risk. Second, "adaptive" always means adaptive to something, and someone must decide whose goals count. A sannyasi who renounces job, property and family has chosen a life that would look maladaptive to an employer or a worried parent, yet it serves his own goals and is honoured by his tradition.

The fourth criterion asks the person. On the personal distress criterion, behaviour is abnormal if it causes the person significant suffering: anxiety, sadness, guilt, agitation. It has the great virtue of respecting the person's own experience, and most people who come to a clinical psychologist do come because they are suffering. But look again at the three people at the start of this section. Sunita is distressed. Imran, in what a psychiatrist would recognise as a manic episode, is not distressed at all; he feels wonderful, and the people around him are the ones who suffer. Many people with antisocial patterns of behaviour, or with severe psychosis and little insight, are similarly untroubled. In the other direction, distress is often perfectly normal: grief after a death, fear before an examination and anger after an injustice are painful but healthy. Distress is neither necessary nor sufficient for abnormality.

Exam trap

A euphoric person in a manic episode, or a remorseless person with antisocial personality, is the standard example of the failure of the personal distress criterion: disordered but not distressed. A grieving widow is the standard example of distress without disorder. "Maladaptive" (the maladaptiveness criterion) and "dysfunction" (one of the four Ds below) mean essentially the same thing: interference with daily functioning.

Ideal mental health: Jahoda's six criteria

Every criterion so far defines abnormality by what is wrong. The social psychologist Marie Jahoda (1907 to 2001) turned the question around. Born in Vienna, she had made her name in the early 1930s with a study of Marienthal, an Austrian village in which almost everyone had lost their job when the local factory closed, and she had seen how unemployment drained people of structure, purpose and hope. Asked by the American Joint Commission on Mental Illness and Health to review what "mental health" meant, she published Current Concepts of Positive Mental Health in 1958. Mental health, she argued, is not merely the absence of mental illness; it should be defined positively, by the presence of certain qualities. On her ideal mental health criterion, abnormality is a falling short of positive functioning. Jahoda named six criteria:

  1. Positive attitudes towards the self: self-acceptance, self-esteem and an accurate sense of one's own identity.
  2. Growth, development and self-actualisation: the extent to which a person develops their abilities and invests themselves in life.
  3. Integration: a balance among the forces within the personality, a unifying outlook on life, and resistance to stress.
  4. Autonomy: a degree of independence from social pressures and the ability to regulate one's own behaviour from within.
  5. Accurate perception of reality: seeing the world without distorting it to fit one's needs, including empathy and sensitivity to other people.
  6. Environmental mastery: adequacy in love, work and play, in relationships, in meeting the demands of situations and in solving problems.

Jahoda's list was a landmark. It anticipated the positive psychology movement by forty years, and its stress on growth and self-actualisation echoes the humanistic psychologists Abraham Maslow and Carl Rogers (Section 1.7). But as a definition of abnormality it sets the bar impossibly high. Very few people show all six qualities all of the time, so on this criterion almost everyone is somewhat abnormal. The list is also culturally loaded. Autonomy and independence from social pressure are prized in individualist societies such as the United States; in an Indian joint family, a young adult who makes major decisions in consultation with parents and elders is showing maturity, not a failure of autonomy.

Exam trap

Jahoda's ideal mental health (1958) has six criteria: positive self-attitudes, growth and self-actualisation, integration, autonomy, accurate perception of reality, and environmental mastery. "Conformity to social norms", "high intelligence", "absence of anxiety" and "happiness" are not on the list; conformity is almost the opposite of her autonomy. Do not confuse Jahoda's list with the World Health Organization's 1946 definition of health as complete physical, mental and social well-being (Section 9.1).

The four Ds

Because no single criterion works, textbooks combine them. The best-known combination is the four Ds of Ronald J. Comer, from his widely used textbook of abnormal psychology. Abnormal functioning, Comer suggests, is usually marked by deviance, distress, dysfunction and danger:

  • Deviance: behaviour, thoughts and emotions that differ markedly from a society's ideas about proper functioning. Deviance takes in both statistical rarity and the breaking of norms, judged against the person's own culture.
  • Distress: the behaviour is unpleasant and upsetting to the person.
  • Dysfunction: the behaviour interferes with the person's ability to conduct daily life in a constructive way, so that they cannot look after themselves, take part in ordinary social life or work productively.
  • Danger: the behaviour puts the person or others at risk, through carelessness, poor judgement, hostility or suicidal intent.

Some teachers add a fifth D, duration, because a brief reaction (a sleepless week after a break-up) is treated differently from a pattern that persists. Comer himself is careful about danger. It is the exception rather than the rule: most people who are anxious, depressed or even psychotic are a danger to nobody, and the belief that "mental patients are violent" is one of the stereotypes that feed stigma.

The four Ds are a checklist, not a formula. Run the three people from the start of the section through it. Sunita shows deviance, distress and dysfunction, and her remark that her pupils would be better off without her is exactly the kind of statement that obliges a clinician to ask about suicidal thoughts, so danger must be assessed. Imran shows deviance, dysfunction (he has stopped attending classes) and danger (debts, and he has been riding a friend's motorbike at high speed late at night), but no distress. Devika shows deviance only if she is judged by someone else's norms; by her own community's standards there is no deviance, no distress, no dysfunction and no danger, and so no disorder. Table 1.12 brings the criteria of this section together.

Table 1.12 The criteria of abnormality compared.

Personal distressCauses the person sufferingRespecting the person's own experienceMania and antisocial patterns lack distress; grief is distressing but normal
