Exam trap

The four Ds are deviance, distress, dysfunction and danger. Distractors slip in "delusion", "disability", "diagnosis", "dependence" or "drug use"; none of these is one of the four. If a fifth D is offered, it is duration. Remember too that deviance covers both statistical and social deviance, and that danger is the least common of the four.

Clinical link

The four Ds quietly organise every first interview. Distress tells you why the person came (or, when it is absent, why someone else brought them). Dysfunction tells you what the problem has cost: work, study, sleep, marriage, self-care. Danger must always be asked about directly, because it decides what happens next; Section 2.7 shows how a clinician assesses suicide and violence risk. Deviance is the one to handle most carefully, since it is the one most easily distorted by the clinician's own culture.

Harmful dysfunction and the official definitions

The most influential philosophical attempt to say what a disorder is came from Jerome C. Wakefield, a philosopher and professor of social work, in a 1992 paper in American Psychologist, "The concept of mental disorder: On the boundary between biological facts and social values". Wakefield argued that a mental disorder is a harmful dysfunction, and that each of the two words does separate work. Dysfunction is meant as a scientific, factual term: some internal mental mechanism (for perception, memory, fear, mood or thought) is failing to perform the natural function for which it was designed by evolution. Harmful is a value term: the failure must cause harm, judged by the standards of the person's culture. Both are required. A dysfunction that harms nobody, such as a minor quirk of perception that the person never even notices, is not a disorder. Nor is harm without dysfunction: the sadness of grief and the fear of a soldier under fire hurt, but they are the sadness and fear mechanisms working exactly as designed.

The harmful-dysfunction account explains why grief is not depression, why a soldier's fear is not a phobia, and why rebels and eccentrics are not ill. It has also been turned against over-diagnosis: in The Loss of Sadness (2007), Wakefield and the sociologist Allan V. Horwitz argued that psychiatry had transformed normal sorrow in response to loss into depressive disorder. Its weakness is the phrase "natural function". We know very little about what mental mechanisms evolution actually designed, so deciding whether a mechanism has failed, or is working as designed in a modern world it was never designed for, is often guesswork.

The two official classifications define mental disorder in a way that closely follows this reasoning. The American Psychiatric Association's DSM-5-TR (2022) describes a mental disorder as "a syndrome characterized by clinically significant disturbance in an individual's cognition, emotion regulation, or behavior that reflects a dysfunction in the psychological, biological, or developmental processes underlying mental functioning", usually associated with "significant distress or disability in social, occupational, or other important activities". It then adds two exclusions that should now sound familiar. "An expectable or culturally approved response to a common stressor or loss, such as the death of a loved one, is not a mental disorder." And socially deviant behaviour (political, religious or sexual) and conflicts between the individual and society are not disorders "unless the deviance or conflict results from a dysfunction in the individual". The World Health Organization's ICD-11, in force since 1 January 2022, defines mental, behavioural and neurodevelopmental disorders in almost the same words. Read the definition again and you can find most of this section inside it: dysfunction (Wakefield), distress and disability (Comer's distress and dysfunction), and a guard against misuse of the social-norm criterion. The textbook of David H. Barlow and V. Mark Durand condenses it to three components: a psychological dysfunction, personal distress or impairment, and a response that is atypical or not culturally expected.

Indian law uses a narrower definition, because it governs admission and rights rather than diagnosis. The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 defines mental illness as "a substantial disorder of thinking, mood, perception, orientation or memory that grossly impairs judgment, behaviour, capacity to recognise reality or ability to meet the ordinary demands of life", including conditions associated with alcohol and drug use but excluding intellectual disability (Section 1.10). Notice the words "substantial" and "grossly": the law sets the threshold higher than the clinic does. How diagnostic definitions are used in practice, and the debate between categories and dimensions, are taught in Section 2.10; the disorders themselves belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book.

Exam trap

Harmful dysfunction is Wakefield's (1992): "dysfunction" is the factual, scientific part (a mechanism failing its evolved function) and "harmful" is the value part (harm judged by cultural standards). It is not Szasz, Jahoda or Comer. The DSM-5-TR definition explicitly excludes an expectable or culturally approved response to loss and socially deviant behaviour that does not arise from a dysfunction in the individual.

The critics: Szasz, Rosenhan and the power of labels

Every definition so far assumes that mental disorders exist and that the task is to describe them accurately. Some critics denied the assumption itself. The most radical was the Hungarian-born American psychiatrist Thomas Szasz (1920 to 2012), first in a 1960 article and then in his book The Myth of Mental Illness (1961). Szasz argued that illness in the strict sense means a bodily lesion, a demonstrable abnormality of the tissues. When such a lesion is found, as in general paresis (Section 1.3), the condition is a disease of the brain, not of the mind. Everything else that psychiatry calls mental illness is, he said, a metaphor. What people actually have are problems in living, conflicts with themselves and with society about how to live; calling them illnesses disguises moral and social judgements as medical facts and gives the state an excuse to lock people up. Szasz spent his long career opposing involuntary hospitalisation and the insanity defence.

His critics replied that the lesion requirement is arbitrary (migraine and many other accepted medical illnesses were diagnosed for centuries without any visible lesion) and that the suffering of a person with severe depression or schizophrenia is no less real for being called a problem in living. Szasz's challenge nevertheless forced psychiatry to justify its concepts, and his worry about what diagnostic labels do to people was soon given a dramatic test.

Key study: Rosenhan, "On being sane in insane places" (1973)

Can psychiatric staff tell the sane from the insane? To find out, the Stanford psychologist David L. Rosenhan (1929 to 2012) and seven other people with no psychiatric history, eight pseudopatients in all, sought admission to twelve psychiatric hospitals in five American states. Each complained of a single symptom: hearing voices that said "empty", "hollow" and "thud". Everything else they reported about their lives was true. All were admitted, and once on the ward they stopped simulating any symptom and behaved normally. None was detected by the staff. All but one were diagnosed with schizophrenia, and they were discharged, after stays of 7 to 52 days (19 on average), with the diagnosis "schizophrenia in remission". The other patients were harder to fool: during the first three admissions, 35 of the 118 patients on the wards voiced their suspicions ("You're not crazy. You're a journalist, or a professor"). Ordinary behaviour was read through the label; a nurse recorded one pseudopatient's note-taking as "patient engages in writing behavior".

Then the study was turned around. A research hospital that doubted the findings was told that over the next three months one or more pseudopatients would try to gain admission. Of 193 patients admitted, 41 were judged with high confidence by at least one staff member to be pseudopatients. Rosenhan had sent none. Published in Science in January 1973, the paper concluded that "we cannot distinguish the sane from the insane in psychiatric hospitals" and that a diagnostic label, once applied, colours everything that follows.

The criticism was immediate. The psychiatrist Seymour Kety (1974) observed that if he drank a quart of blood and arrived at an emergency room vomiting it, he would be diagnosed with a bleeding peptic ulcer, and that this would show nothing about whether medicine can diagnose ulcers: a patient who lies will deceive any diagnostician. Robert L. Spitzer (1975) added that "in remission" was a very rare discharge diagnosis, which suggested that the staff had in fact noticed the pseudopatients were well. In 2019 the journalist Susannah Cahalan, in The Great Pretender, reported discrepancies between Rosenhan's private records and his published account and could not trace most of the pseudopatients, which has cast doubt on the study itself. Its influence is undeniable all the same: it is often credited with pushing the American Psychiatric Association towards the explicit, observable diagnostic criteria of DSM-III (1980), a project Spitzer led.

Rosenhan's study is usually read alongside labelling theory, set out by the American sociologist Thomas J. Scheff in Being Mentally Ill (1966). Scheff noticed that everyone occasionally breaks the unwritten rules of social life (talking to oneself, staring, withdrawing, saying odd things), which he called residual rule-breaking. Most such episodes are ignored and fade away. But if, at a moment of crisis, a person's rule-breaking is publicly labelled as mental illness, the culture's stereotyped picture of "the mentally ill" supplies a ready-made role, and the person is rewarded for playing it and punished for trying to leave it. The label becomes a self-fulfilling prophecy. Few researchers now accept that labels cause major mental disorders. But in 1989 the sociologist Bruce G. Link and his colleagues showed, in what they called a modified labelling theory, that labels do real damage: people who have absorbed their society's belief that psychiatric patients are devalued and rejected come to expect rejection once they are labelled, become secretive and withdrawn, and lose jobs, income and friends as a result. The stigma that the sociologist Erving Goffman (1922 to 1982) analysed in 1963 as a "spoiled identity" is one of the main reasons people in India delay seeking help (Section 1.5). Goffman's earlier book, Asylums (1961), described the mental hospital as a total institution that strips inmates of their former identities, a theme taken up again in the story of deinstitutionalisation (Chapter 9).

Exam trap

Match the critic to the book: Szasz, The Myth of Mental Illness (1961), "problems in living"; Rosenhan, "On being sane in insane places" (Science, 1973), pseudopatients; Scheff, Being Mentally Ill (1966), labelling theory and residual rule-breaking; Goffman, Asylums (1961) and Stigma (1963). R. D. Laing's The Divided Self (1960) belongs to the same critical climate (Section 1.7). Rosenhan's pseudopatients reported hearing "empty", "hollow" and "thud", and were discharged "in remission", not "cured".

Culture and the shape of distress

Culture does more than set the norms against which behaviour is judged; it also shapes how distress is felt, explained and expressed. Older editions of the DSM spoke of culture-bound syndromes. Since DSM-5 that idea has been replaced by three cultural concepts of distress: a cultural idiom of distress is a way of talking about suffering shared by people of a similar background ("thinking too much", "my heart is sinking"); a cultural explanation or perceived cause is a culturally coherent account of why the suffering happened (stress, spirits, black magic, failure to perform a ritual); and a cultural syndrome is a cluster of co-occurring symptoms recognised in a particular group. The best-known South Asian example is dhat syndrome, in which young men attribute weakness, fatigue, anxiety, low mood and sexual complaints to the loss of semen through urine or night emissions (in Ayurveda, dhatu is one of the seven essential bodily substances whose balance keeps a person healthy). Despite its name, DSM-5-TR describes it as "not a discrete syndrome but rather a cultural explanation of distress". Possession and trance states such as Devika's are another: in many Indian communities they are a recognised way of expressing and resolving conflict, and DSM-5-TR states explicitly that a dissociative trance that is "a normal part of a broadly accepted collective cultural or religious practice" is not a disorder.

For the clinician, culture works in both directions. A behaviour that looks bizarre may be ordinary in the person's world, and a real disorder may hide behind a culturally acceptable idiom: many Indian patients with depression come first to a physician with headaches, body aches and tiredness rather than with sadness. The cultural concepts themselves, and the disorders they may resemble, are described in the Psychiatric Disorders book. What this section should leave you with is a principle. Abnormality is not a single measurable property like height. It is a judgement that combines facts (is something failing?) with values (does it harm, and by whose standards?), and the clinician's job is to make that judgement carefully, humbly and with the person's own world in view. How that judgement is explained once it is made is the business of the models in the next section.

Exam trap

Since DSM-5 the term "culture-bound syndrome" has been replaced by cultural concepts of distress, which come in three kinds: cultural idioms of distress, cultural explanations (perceived causes) and cultural syndromes. Dhat syndrome is the South Asian example most often asked; it is a cultural explanation of distress centred on semen loss, not a specific sexual dysfunction.

1.7 Models of Psychopathology and TreatmentHigh yield · 18 Qs

Suppose you have decided that Sunita's state is a disorder. The next question is why it happened, and the answer you give will decide what you do about it. If her depression is a disturbance of brain chemistry, she needs a medicine. If it is anger at her mother's death turned against herself, she needs to understand it. If it is a habit of self-critical thinking, she needs to learn to question her thoughts. If it is the product of an isolated life two hours from everyone who loves her, perhaps what needs to change is her situation. Each of these answers comes from a different model of psychopathology, also called a perspective or paradigm: a set of assumptions about what causes abnormal behaviour, which facts are worth collecting, and how disorder should be treated. The word paradigm was made famous by the historian of science Thomas Kuhn (1922 to 1996) in The Structure of Scientific Revolutions (1962), for the shared framework within which a scientific community works and which tells it what questions are worth asking.

This section works through eight models in roughly the order in which they appeared, from the supernatural to the family-systems model. For each you need three things: what it says causes disorder, what treatment follows from it, and where it falls short. Then come the two integrative models that modern clinicians actually use and that examiners ask about more than any other topic in this chapter: the biopsychosocial model and the diathesis-stress model. The therapies that grew out of each model have chapters of their own (Chapters 3 to 7); here the models are treated as explanations. Sunita's depression will be the test case throughout, so you can watch each model take the same facts and tell a different story.

The supernatural model

The oldest model explains abnormal behaviour by forces outside nature: possession by spirits or demons, the anger of gods or ancestors, sorcery, the evil eye, or punishment for sin. Section 1.2 tells its history, from trephining and exorcism to the witch hunts; here it is enough to see it as a model with its own logic. On the supernatural model the cause lies outside the person and outside the natural world, so the remedy must be supernatural too: exorcism, prayer, a ritual to appease the offended power, an amulet, a pilgrimage, or the services of a healer who can deal with spirits. In Sunita's extended family, an aunt might well suggest that so sudden a change must be nazar (the evil eye) cast by a jealous colleague, or black magic, and propose a visit to a temple, a dargah or a local healer.

As a scientific explanation the supernatural model fails, because its causes cannot be observed, measured or tested; no conceivable observation could show that a spirit was not responsible. Yet it is not a relic. It survives all over the world, and in India many people with mental illness visit a faith healer, a temple or a dargah before they see a doctor, or alongside one. The model also does some things a clinician should understand rather than dismiss. It places the blame outside the sufferer (the spirit, not the person, is at fault), it gathers the family and community around the patient, and its rituals can lower anxiety and supply hope. What it cannot do is treat a psychosis or a severe depression, and when it is used instead of treatment rather than alongside it, the delay can be long and the cost high. Section 1.5 describes how Indian clinicians work with these pathways to care.

Clinical link

Every patient and family arrives with a model of their own. The psychiatrist and anthropologist Arthur Kleinman called this the patient's explanatory model of illness: their beliefs about what the problem is, what caused it, why it started when it did, how serious it is and what treatment it needs. Asking "What do you think has caused this?" early in the first interview costs a minute and saves weeks. A family convinced that Sunita is the victim of black magic will not stay with a treatment that ignores their belief; a clinician who has listened to it can explain treatment in terms the family can accept, and can work alongside a ritual that does no harm.

The biological model

The biological model holds that psychological disorders are diseases of the body, above all of the brain, caused by faulty genes, abnormal brain chemistry, damaged or atypical brain structures, hormonal disturbances or infections. Its ancestor is Hippocrates' theory of the four humours (Section 1.2), and its great modern triumph was the discovery that general paresis, one of the commonest causes of admission to nineteenth-century asylums, was the late result of syphilitic infection of the brain (Section 1.3). If one mental disorder had a physical cause, why not all of them? That hope has driven biological psychiatry ever since.

The evidence comes from several directions. Family, twin and adoption studies show that many disorders run in families for genetic reasons. The most quoted figures come from the behaviour geneticist Irving Gottesman (1930 to 2016), who in 1991 pooled the European studies of schizophrenia: if one identical twin has schizophrenia, the other has it in about 48 percent of cases, against about 17 percent for fraternal twins and about 1 percent in the general population. Studies of neurotransmitters, the chemical messengers between nerve cells, have linked disorders to dopamine (psychosis), serotonin and noradrenaline (depression and anxiety), gamma-aminobutyric acid or GABA (anxiety) and glutamate. Brain imaging shows structural and functional differences, such as enlarged ventricles in many people with schizophrenia and an overactive amygdala in anxiety. Endocrine research has shown that chronic stress keeps the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis switched on, raising levels of the stress hormone cortisol. (The details belong to the Physiological Psychology and Psychiatric Disorders books.)

The biological model brought with it the language of medicine, and it is often simply called the medical model: abnormal behaviour is seen as the outward expression of an underlying illness, and the person is a patient who has symptoms, receives a diagnosis, has an illness with an aetiology (cause), a course and a prognosis (likely outcome), and is given treatment. In that language a symptom is what the patient experiences and reports (low mood, hearing voices), a sign is what the clinician observes (slowed movement, weight loss), and a syndrome is a cluster of signs and symptoms that tend to occur together. The hope of the medical model, set out by Emil Kraepelin (Section 1.3), is that each syndrome will turn out to be a disease with a single cause, a typical course and a specific treatment.

Treatment on this model is physical. The twentieth century tried insulin coma therapy (Manfred Sakel, 1933), electroconvulsive therapy (Ugo Cerletti and Lucio Bini, 1938) and prefrontal leucotomy (Egas Moniz, 1935, for which he shared the Nobel Prize in 1949). The revolution came with drugs: lithium for mania (John Cade, 1949) and chlorpromazine, the first antipsychotic (Jean Delay and Pierre Deniker, 1952), followed in the 1950s by the first antidepressants. Today the biological treatments are psychiatric medicines (antipsychotics, antidepressants, mood stabilisers, anxiolytics) and brain stimulation (modified ECT, transcranial magnetic stimulation, deep brain stimulation). Clinical psychologists do not prescribe (Section 1.1), but they work every day with patients who take these medicines, and the Indian law on ECT is set out in Section 1.10. Seen through this lens, Sunita's family history (her mother had depression), her early waking and her weight loss suggest a biological depression; she should have her thyroid checked and might be offered an antidepressant.

The biological model has been enormously productive, and for some disorders (bipolar disorder, schizophrenia) medication is indispensable. Its weaknesses are just as important. The first is reductionism: explaining a complex whole entirely in terms of its simplest parts, here a person's grief, guilt and hopelessness in terms of molecules. The second is the leap from correlation to cause. A brain difference found in people with a disorder may be a cause, but it may also be a consequence of the disorder, of years of stress, or of the treatment itself. The third is that the popular slogan of a "chemical imbalance" claims far more than the evidence shows: antidepressants change serotonin levels within hours but take weeks to help, and a 2022 umbrella review led by the psychiatrist Joanna Moncrieff found no consistent evidence that depression is caused by low serotonin. Finally, even disorders with a strong genetic component are not fully genetic: half of the identical co-twins of people with schizophrenia never develop it, although they carry the same genes. Keep that fact in mind; it is the empirical starting point of the diathesis-stress model later in this section.

The medical model has had fierce critics. Thomas Szasz's argument that mental illness is a myth was met in Section 1.6. The Scottish psychiatrist Ronald David Laing (1927 to 1989), in The Divided Self (1960), described schizophrenia as an understandable response to an unliveable family situation rather than a brain disease, and the South African-born psychiatrist David Cooper coined the word antipsychiatry in 1967 for the loose movement that rejected psychiatric diagnosis and the mental hospital. Their specific claims about schizophrenia have not survived, but their insistence on listening to the patient's experience did.

Exam trap

A symptom is reported by the patient, a sign is observed by the clinician, and a syndrome is a cluster of the two. Describing a disorder by its syndrome says what it looks like, not what causes it, so a "syndrome-sign" description can never be the answer to a question about predisposition and triggers. Reductionism is a criticism of the biological model, not a model of its own. Szasz wrote The Myth of Mental Illness (1961); Laing wrote The Divided Self (1960); Cooper coined "antipsychiatry" (1967).

The psychodynamic model

The psychodynamic model, founded by Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939), holds that abnormal behaviour arises from unconscious psychological conflicts, usually rooted in childhood, between wishes and the forces that oppose them. "Dynamic" refers to these forces in motion, pushing against one another. The model's history from hypnosis to the talking cure is told in Section 3.1, its therapies fill Chapter 3, and the full personality theory belongs to the Personality book. What follows is the core of it as an explanation of disorder, which is exactly what the examiners ask.

Freud's first map of the mind, set out in The Interpretation of Dreams (1900), was the topographic model: a small conscious region (what you are aware of now), a preconscious (what you can bring to mind if you try, such as your phone number), and a vast unconscious containing wishes, memories and feelings kept out of awareness because they are threatening. Textbooks often draw it as an iceberg with only its tip above the water. In The Ego and the Id (1923) Freud added his structural model, which divides the personality into three agencies according to what they do rather than where they are (Table 1.13).

  • The id is present from birth and is wholly unconscious. It is the reservoir of the instinctual drives, sexual (whose energy Freud called libido) and, after 1920, aggressive, and it is the source of all psychic energy. The id operates on the pleasure principle: it demands the immediate gratification of its wishes and the immediate release of tension, with no regard for reality, logic, morality or consequences. Its way of thinking is primary process thinking, wishful, illogical and timeless, the logic of dreams; if what it wants is not available, it can conjure up an image of it, as a hungry infant may picture the breast.
  • The ego develops out of the id during infancy, as the child collides with reality. It is partly conscious and partly unconscious, and it operates on the reality principle: it delays gratification until a realistic and safe way of satisfying the wish can be found. Its way of thinking is secondary process thinking: logical, realistic planning and problem solving. Freud compared the ego to a rider who must hold in check the superior strength of a horse, and he described it as serving three harsh masters at once: the id, the superego and the external world.
  • The superego develops last, at around five or six years, when the child resolves the Oedipus complex by identifying with the parent of the same sex and taking the parents' standards (and through them society's) inside; Freud called it the heir of the Oedipus complex. It strives for moral perfection rather than pleasure or realism, and textbooks divide it into two parts. The conscience holds the internalised prohibitions, what one must not do, and punishes violations with guilt. The ego ideal holds the internalised picture of what one should be and do, and rewards one with pride and self-esteem for living up to it. (Freud himself used the terms more loosely; the two-part division is the standard textbook form.)
Table 1.13 Freud's structural model of the personality (1923).
StructureGoverning principleKind of thinkingAwarenessAppears
IdPleasure principle: immediate gratificationPrimary process (wishful, illogical)Wholly unconsciousAt birth
EgoReality principle: delay until realisticSecondary process (logical, realistic)Conscious, preconscious and unconsciousInfancy
SuperegoMoral perfection; conscience (guilt) and ego ideal (pride)Moral judgementPartly conscious, largely unconsciousAbout 5 to 6 years, with the resolution of the Oedipus complex

When the three agencies clash, the ego experiences anxiety, which Freud came to see as a danger signal. He distinguished three kinds: realistic anxiety, fear of a real danger in the outside world; neurotic anxiety, fear that the id's impulses will break through and bring punishment; and moral anxiety, fear of the superego's condemnation, felt as guilt or shame. To protect itself the ego uses defence mechanisms, unconscious strategies that reduce anxiety by distorting or excluding some part of inner or outer reality: repression, projection, displacement, reaction formation and the rest. Freud's daughter Anna Freud (1895 to 1982) catalogued them in The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence (1936); Table 3.3 describes each one as a clinician meets it in a session.

On this model a disorder develops when conflict is intense and the ego's defences are too rigid, too primitive or simply overwhelmed. Symptoms are compromise formations, disguised partial expressions of a forbidden wish together with the defence against it. A person who was frustrated or overindulged at one of the psychosexual stages (oral, anal, phallic, latency, genital) may remain partly fixated there and, under later stress, regress to that stage's ways of coping. Sunita's depression would be read through Freud's paper "Mourning and Melancholia" (1917). In ordinary mourning the world feels poor and empty; in melancholia it is the self that feels poor and worthless. Freud suggested that when a loved person is lost, the hostile half of our ambivalent feelings towards them can be turned against the self, so that the bitter reproaches Sunita heaps on herself ("I failed her; I am useless") may be reproaches once felt, and never allowed, towards her demanding mother. The aim of treatment follows: to bring the conflict into awareness, so that it can be understood and worked through (Chapter 3).

Not all defences are equally unhealthy, and the most useful way of ranking them came from the American psychiatrist George Eman Vaillant (born 1934). Vaillant directed the Harvard Study of Adult Development, which had followed a group of Harvard men from their student days at the end of the 1930s, and in Adaptation to Life (1977) he arranged the defences these men used into a hierarchy of defences with four levels, from the least to the most mature (Table 1.14). The men whose dominant defences were mature had better physical health, happier marriages and more successful careers decades later than the men who relied on immature ones, and over a lifetime many men moved up the hierarchy. Psychodynamic clinicians still use the levels to judge how mature a patient's functioning is, and the direction of growth in therapy, on this view, is upwards.

Table 1.14 Vaillant's four levels of defence (1977).
LevelDefencesWhere they are typical
I. Psychotic (pathological)Delusional projection, psychotic denial, distortionPsychosis; also dreams and early childhood
II. ImmatureProjection, schizoid fantasy, hypochondriasis, passive aggression, acting outNormal in adolescence; in adults, personality disorders and substance misuse
III. Neurotic (intermediate)Intellectualisation (with isolation), repression, displacement, reaction formation, dissociationCommon in ordinary adults under stress; anxiety and obsessional problems
IV. MatureSublimation, altruism, suppression, anticipation, humourHealthy, well-adapted adults

The psychodynamic model gave psychology the idea that much of mental life is unconscious, that early relationships shape later ones, and that symptoms can have meaning. Its weaknesses are serious. The philosopher Karl Popper (1902 to 1994) argued that its claims are unfalsifiable: whatever a patient does can be explained after the event (if he loves his father, that is affection; if he hates him, that is a reaction formation against affection), so no observation could ever count against the theory. Its evidence came largely from case studies of a small number of middle-class Viennese patients, interpreted by the theorist himself, and its emphasis on sexuality and early childhood has been widely criticised. Modern psychodynamic research has kept the testable core (unconscious processes, defences, relationship patterns) and dropped much of the rest; whether psychodynamic therapy works is examined in Section 3.11.

Exam trap

The pairings examiners test are fixed: id: pleasure principle, ego: reality principle, and the superego's two parts: the conscience and the ego ideal. There is no "aggressive principle" or "sexual principle", and "conscious mind" and "unconscious mind" are regions of the topographic model, not principles or structures of the structural model. The id is not "the unconscious" either: the id is wholly unconscious, but the ego and superego are largely unconscious too.

Exam trap

In Vaillant's scheme, intellectualisation is neurotic, along with repression, displacement, reaction formation and dissociation. Projection is immature (with acting out, passive aggression, hypochondriasis and schizoid fantasy). Sublimation is mature (with altruism, suppression, anticipation and humour). Delusional projection, psychotic denial and distortion are psychotic. Splitting (seeing people as all good or all bad) is not in Vaillant's 1977 list; later schemes place it among the primitive, image-distorting defences below the neurotic level, and Kernberg made it the hallmark of borderline organisation (Section 3.4). So splitting is never the "neurotic" answer.

The behavioural model

The behavioural model starts from an opposite assumption. Abnormal behaviour is not the symptom of a hidden conflict; it is learned, by the same principles as all other behaviour, and what has been learned can be unlearned. The model grew out of the behaviourism launched by John Broadus Watson (1878 to 1958) in 1913, which insisted that psychology study observable behaviour rather than the mind. Three kinds of learning are used to explain disorder. In classical conditioning, discovered by Ivan Petrovich Pavlov (1849 to 1936), a neutral stimulus paired with something frightening comes to provoke fear itself: in 1920 Watson and Rosalie Rayner conditioned an eleven-month-old boy, "Little Albert", to fear a white rat by striking a steel bar behind him whenever he touched it. In operant conditioning, associated with Edward Lee Thorndike (1874 to 1949) and Burrhus Frederic Skinner (1904 to 1990), behaviour that is reinforced is repeated: a child whose tantrums reliably win attention will have more tantrums. In observational learning, studied by Albert Bandura (1925 to 2021), behaviour is acquired by watching others: a child may learn to fear dogs from watching a parent's panic. Section 4.1 shows how these combine in Mowrer's two-factor theory of fear and avoidance. Learning is not unlimited, however. Martin Seligman (born 1942) pointed out in 1971 that people readily develop phobias of snakes, heights and the dark, but almost never of electric sockets or cars, which are far more dangerous today; his concept of preparedness proposes that evolution has prepared us to learn some fears much more easily than others.

Through the behavioural lens, Sunita's depression looks like a problem of lost reinforcement. The psychologist Peter Lewinsohn proposed in the 1970s that depression follows a fall in response-contingent positive reinforcement: when a person's actions stop producing rewards, they do less, and doing less brings still fewer rewards. Since her transfer Sunita has lost her mother's company, her old colleagues and her evening walks with friends; she now spends her evenings in bed, which protects her from effort but cuts her off from every remaining source of pleasure. The behavioural answer is to schedule rewarding and meaningful activity step by step, the technique now called behavioural activation (Section 5.10).

Behaviour therapy, the subject of Chapter 4, therefore aims at the problem behaviour itself: it applies learning principles to eliminate unwanted behaviour and build desirable behaviour in its place. It does not look for an underlying cause, and it does not aim primarily at insight. The model's strengths are its testability and its results: exposure-based treatments are among the most effective treatments in the whole of clinical psychology. Psychoanalysts predicted that removing a symptom without resolving the conflict beneath it would only produce a new symptom (symptom substitution), but this has generally not been found. The model's weaknesses are that it neglects thoughts, feelings and biology, that much of its theory was built on rats and pigeons in simple laboratory situations, and that it struggles to explain disorders such as schizophrenia.

Exam trap

"Behavioural therapies apply learning principles to..." is completed by "eliminate the unwanted behaviour" (and strengthen desirable behaviour). "Identify and treat the underlying causes of dysfunction" describes the psychodynamic aim; "improve learning and insight" describes insight-oriented therapy; "improve communication and social sensitivity" describes encounter and sensitivity groups (Section 7.11). Behaviour analysis and functional analysis rest on operant conditioning; token economies use secondary (conditioned) reinforcers (Chapter 4).

The cognitive model

By the 1960s some behaviour therapists had begun to feel that their model left out the most human thing about their clients: they think. Two people can lose the same job; one says "the company is downsizing" and starts applying elsewhere, the other says "I am a failure and always will be" and goes to bed. The Stoic philosopher Epictetus had made the point nearly two thousand years ago: people are disturbed not by things but by the views they take of them. The cognitive model holds that disorder arises from the way people interpret events, from faulty assumptions, distorted interpretations and maladaptive beliefs, rather than from the events themselves.

Two clinicians built the model, independently, out of their dissatisfaction with psychoanalysis (Section 5.1). Albert Ellis (1913 to 2007) proposed that emotional disturbance follows from irrational beliefs, especially rigid demands ("I must succeed", "others must treat me well"), and summarised the process in his ABC model: an Activating event triggers a Belief, which produces the emotional and behavioural Consequence (Section 5.2). Aaron Temkin Beck (1921 to 2021) found that his depressed patients' thinking was dominated by the cognitive triad, negative views of the self, the world and the future, produced by automatic thoughts and supported by systematic errors of reasoning; beneath them, he argued, lay negative schemas laid down in childhood, which can lie dormant for years until a stressful event of the matching kind switches them on (Section 5.3). A third strand came from research on learned helplessness: in 1978 Lyn Abramson, Seligman and John Teasdale proposed that people become depressed when they explain bad events in an attributional style that is internal ("it is my fault"), stable ("it will always be like this") and global ("it spoils everything").

Sunita's own words show the cognitive triad almost perfectly: "I am useless" (self), "everyone at the new school is cold" (world), "it will never get better" (future), and "my pupils would be better off with someone else" (a hopeless conclusion drawn from all three). Cognitive therapy would teach her to catch these thoughts, test them against the evidence and replace them with more balanced ones (Chapter 5). The model's strengths are its clear, testable theory and the strong evidence for cognitive behaviour therapy. Its central weakness is the direction of cause. Negative thinking is clearly part of depression, but it may be a consequence rather than a cause: many depressed people's thinking returns to normal when they recover. In a famous study in 1979, Lauren Alloy and Abramson even found that mildly depressed students judged how much control they had over a light more accurately than non-depressed students, who overestimated their control; this depressive realism suggests that it is not always the depressed person whose thinking is distorted. The model also says little about biology or about the real social circumstances that may justify a person's gloom.

Exam trap

Beck's cognitive triad is the self, the world (or present experience) and the future; "the past", "others" and "the body" are distractors. Notice that Beck's own model is a diathesis-stress model in disguise: a latent negative schema (the diathesis) is activated by a matching stressful event (the stress). The attributional reformulation of learned helplessness (Abramson, Seligman and Teasdale, 1978) names three dimensions: internal, stable and global.

The humanistic-existential model

In the 1950s and 1960s a group of American psychologists rejected both of the dominant models: psychoanalysis for its pessimism (people driven by dark instincts) and behaviourism for its mechanism (people as bundles of conditioned responses). Abraham Harold Maslow (1908 to 1970) called their movement the third force in psychology. The humanistic-existential model actually joins two related traditions, and the examiners sometimes test them separately.

The humanistic view, led by Maslow and Carl Ransom Rogers (1902 to 1987), holds that people are basically good and are driven by an innate tendency towards growth, which Maslow called self-actualisation: becoming everything one is capable of becoming. Disorder arises when that growth is blocked. Rogers's account of how it is blocked is the one to know (Section 6.2 gives it in full). Every child needs positive regard, but many children receive it only conditionally: they are loved when they are good, clever or obedient. The child takes in these conditions of worth ("I am worthwhile only if I come first in class") and begins to deny or distort any experience that contradicts them. The result is incongruence, a gap between the self-concept and the person's actual experience, which is felt as anxiety, defensiveness and a vague sense of not being oneself. The cure follows: a relationship in which the therapist offers unconditional positive regard, accurate empathy and genuineness, so that the client can stop defending and resume growing. Notice what is missing from this list: rewards, reinforcement and technique. The humanistic therapist is a respectful companion, not an engineer of behaviour.

The existential view, developed by Rollo May (1909 to 1994), Viktor Emil Frankl (1905 to 1997) and later Irvin D. Yalom (born 1931), adds that every human being must face certain hard facts of existence. Yalom (1980) named four ultimate concerns: death, freedom (with the responsibility it brings), isolation and meaninglessness. Anxiety about these is normal; disorder arises when people evade it by living inauthentically, conforming to what others expect rather than choosing their own way. Frankl, who survived the Nazi concentration camps, described an existential vacuum, a sense of emptiness and meaninglessness, which can produce what he called noogenic neurosis; his logotherapy helps people find meaning (Section 6.5).

Sunita looks different again through these lenses. A Rogerian would wonder whether she grew up valued above all as the dutiful daughter and the dedicated teacher; with her mother gone and her teaching faltering, both conditions of her worth have collapsed at once. An existential therapist would notice that her mother's death has confronted her, perhaps for the first time, with her own mortality and with the question of what her life is for. The humanistic-existential model restored the person's own experience and choices to the centre of psychology, and Rogers was a pioneer of psychotherapy research, one of the first to record and analyse real therapy sessions. Its weaknesses are that its key concepts (self-actualisation, authenticity, meaning) are hard to define and test, that it has least to offer people with severe disorders such as schizophrenia, and that its optimism about human nature is a value judgement rather than a finding.

Exam trap

The humanistic perspective is built on belief in human potential (self-actualisation), the availability of positive regard (Rogers's unconditional positive regard) and a respectful, non-judgemental attitude in the therapist. Availability of reinforcement is a behavioural concept and is the odd one out. The "third force" is humanistic psychology; the first two forces are psychoanalysis and behaviourism. Conditions of worth and incongruence are Rogers's terms, not Maslow's.

The sociocultural model

All the models so far look inside the person. The sociocultural model looks outwards. It holds that abnormal behaviour is best understood in the light of the social and cultural forces that act on a person: the labels and roles society assigns (Section 1.6), family structure and communication, social networks and support, poverty, discrimination, gender, cultural values and rapid social change. Its roots lie in sociology rather than medicine, and one of its founding studies is still quoted.

In 1939 two Chicago sociologists, Robert E. L. Faris and H. Warren Dunham, published Mental Disorders in Urban Areas. They plotted the home addresses of thousands of people admitted to hospital with psychoses onto a map of Chicago and found a striking pattern: first admissions for schizophrenia were highest in the poor, socially disorganised districts around the city centre, full of rooming houses and transient residents, and fell steadily towards the prosperous suburbs. Manic-depressive psychosis showed no such pattern. Two decades later August B. Hollingshead and Fredrick C. Redlich, in Social Class and Mental Illness (1958), found that in New Haven, Connecticut, schizophrenia was far more common in the lowest social class than in any other.

Why should poverty and schizophrenia go together? Two explanations compete. The social causation hypothesis says that the stresses of poverty, discrimination, crowding and disorganised neighbourhoods cause or trigger the disorder (Faris and Dunham's own interpretation, sometimes called the breeder hypothesis). The social drift hypothesis, also called social selection, says that the causation runs the other way: people who are developing a disorder drift down the social scale, because illness disrupts their education and work, and end up living in poor areas. In 1963 E. M. Goldberg and S. L. Morrison tested the two in England: the fathers of young men with schizophrenia had jobs spread across the whole social range, but the men themselves had slipped down the occupational ladder before and after the illness began, which supported drift. The neatest test came from Bruce P. Dohrenwend and his colleagues (1992) in Israel, who compared people of European origin with people of North African origin, an ethnic group that faced more discrimination. If social causation were true, the disadvantaged group should show more disorder at every social level. For schizophrenia it did not, supporting social selection; for depression in women and for antisocial personality and substance use in men it did, supporting social causation. Both hypotheses are true, but for different disorders.

The sociologists George Brown and Tirril Harris applied the same thinking to depression in Social Origins of Depression (1978), a study of women in Camberwell, south London. Depression usually followed a "provoking agent", a severe life event or a major long-term difficulty. But only some women who suffered such an event became depressed, and those who did were more likely to have one or more vulnerability factors: no intimate, confiding relationship with a husband or partner, three or more children under 14 at home, no job outside the home, and the loss of their mother before the age of 11. A confiding relationship was the most powerful protection. Read that list again and you will see a diathesis-stress model built entirely out of social facts. Sunita fits it uncomfortably well: she lost her mother (as an adult, not a child), she has been moved away from her family, her husband works long hours, and at the new school she has nobody to confide in. Women worldwide are about twice as likely as men to suffer depression, and the sociocultural model asks how much of that gap lies in women's social circumstances rather than in their biology.

The sociocultural model's treatments work at two levels: helping the individual (strengthening social support, culturally adapted therapy, Section 8.11) and changing the conditions that produce disorder (community mental health, prevention and policy, Chapter 9). Its weakness is that most of its evidence is correlational, so the direction of cause is hard to establish, and that social factors alone cannot explain why only some people in the same poor neighbourhood become ill. For that, the social facts must be combined with facts about the individual, which is exactly what the integrative models below do.

Exam trap

Social causation: poverty and stress cause disorder. Social drift (social selection): disorder causes the fall into poverty. Faris and Dunham (Chicago, 1939) found the pattern; Goldberg and Morrison (1963) supported drift for schizophrenia; Dohrenwend (1992) found selection for schizophrenia but causation for depression in women and antisocial personality and substance use in men. Brown and Harris's four vulnerability factors are a confiding relationship lacking, three or more children under 14 at home, no outside employment, and loss of the mother before age 11.

The family-systems model

The family-systems model treats the family, not the individual, as the unit that becomes disordered. Borrowing from general systems theory and cybernetics (Section 7.1), it sees a family as a system of interdependent parts, held in balance (homeostasis) by rules and feedback loops that nobody may be aware of. A change in one member changes all the others, and causes run in circles rather than in straight lines. The member who shows the symptoms is called the identified patient, and the symptom may serve a function for the system: a boy's refusal to go to school may keep his quarrelling parents united in worrying about him, so that when he improves, the parents' marriage visibly worsens.

The early family theories of schizophrenia blamed families directly. The psychoanalyst Frieda Fromm-Reichmann (1889 to 1957) spoke in 1948 of the "schizophrenogenic mother", cold and domineering yet overprotective, and in 1956 the anthropologist Gregory Bateson (1904 to 1980) and his colleagues proposed that schizophrenia arises in children repeatedly caught in a double bind: contradictory messages at two levels (a mother who says "come and give me a hug" but stiffens when the child does), with no way to comment on the contradiction or to leave. Neither idea survived research, and both caused great pain to families (Section 7.2). What did survive is the study of expressed emotion, the attitudes relatives express about a patient: critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement. George Brown, James Birley and John Wing showed in 1972 that patients with schizophrenia who return to high-expressed-emotion homes relapse much more often than those who return to calmer ones, and the finding has been repeated for depression and other disorders. Expressed emotion affects the course of an illness; it is not a cause of it, and family psychoeducation that lowers it reduces relapse (Section 7.2).

Through this lens the clinician would look at Sunita's household. Her husband, frightened by her change, has taken to telling her to "just snap out of it", and her mother-in-law has begun to comment that a good wife would not lie in bed; each criticism deepens her sense of failure, and her withdrawal provokes more criticism, a circle that no single member started or can stop alone. The family-systems model's strength is that it sees what individual models miss, and family interventions in schizophrenia are among the best-supported psychosocial treatments there are. Its weaknesses are its history of blaming families and the difficulty of testing claims about circular causality. Its therapies are the subject of Chapter 7.

Table 1.15 sets the eight models side by side. Read across a row to see a model's whole argument; read down a column to compare what the models say about the same question.

Table 1.15 Eight models of psychopathology compared.
ModelCause of disorderAim of treatmentMain limitation
SupernaturalSpirits, sorcery, the evil eye, divine punishmentExorcism, prayer, ritualCauses cannot be tested; delays treatment
Biological (medical)Genes, brain chemistry, brain structure, hormones, infectionMedication, ECT and other physical treatmentsReductionist; correlation is not cause
Psychodynamic (Chapter 3)Unconscious conflict among id, ego and superego, rooted in childhoodInsight: make the unconscious conscious and work it throughUnfalsifiable; case-study evidence
Behavioural (Chapter 4)Learning: conditioning, reinforcement, modellingEliminate unwanted behaviour, build desirable behaviourNeglects thought, feeling and biology
Cognitive (Chapter 5)Irrational beliefs, negative schemas, distorted thinkingIdentify and change maladaptive thinkingThoughts may be a consequence, not a cause
Humanistic-existential (Chapter 6)Blocked growth: conditions of worth, incongruence; evasion of existential anxietyGrowth through an accepting, empathic, genuine relationship; finding meaningVague concepts; little for severe disorder
Sociocultural (Chapter 9)Poverty, discrimination, labels, weak social support, cultureSocial support, community change, culturally adapted careCorrelational; cannot explain individual differences
Family-systems (Chapter 7)Dysfunctional family rules, communication and expressed emotionChange the family system, not just the patientHistory of blaming families; hard to test

The biopsychosocial model

Look back at Table 1.15 and a problem is obvious. Every model has caught something real about Sunita: her family history and her early waking, her unspoken anger at her mother, her lost sources of reward, her self-critical thoughts, her collapsed conditions of worth, her isolation and her critical household. No single model can hold all of it. By the 1970s the strongest objection to this state of affairs came not from a psychologist but from inside medicine itself.

George Libman Engel (1913 to 1999) was an American internist and psychiatrist, trained also in psychoanalysis, who spent most of his career at the University of Rochester in New York. He had long been interested in how emotions affect bodily illness, and in 1961 he had provoked his colleagues with a paper asking "Is grief a disease?" (it has a recognisable cause, a typical course, distress and impaired functioning, and often a recovery, he pointed out, so by the usual medical standards it qualifies). On 8 April 1977 he published in Science the paper that made his name, "The need for a new medical model: A challenge for biomedicine". The dominant biomedical model, Engel wrote, assumes that disease can be fully accounted for by deviations from the norm of measurable biological variables. It is reductionist, because it explains illness entirely at the level of cells and molecules, and dualist, because it separates the body (medicine's business) from the mind (somebody else's). Such a model cannot explain why one person with abnormal laboratory findings feels well while another with normal findings feels ill, why illnesses so often begin after a loss or a life crisis, why the same disease runs a different course in different people, or why the relationship between doctor and patient affects whether the patient recovers.

Engel's alternative was the biopsychosocial model. Drawing on the general systems theory of the biologist Ludwig von Bertalanffy (1901 to 1972), he described nature as a hierarchy of systems, from molecules and cells through organs and the nervous system to the person, and beyond the person to the family, the community, the culture and society. Each level is a whole made of smaller parts and at the same time a part of a larger whole, and a change at any level ripples up and down to the others. Health and illness are therefore always the product of biological, psychological and social factors acting together, and a clinician who attends to only one level will misunderstand both the cause and the cure. Engel used diabetes and schizophrenia to make the point: both have a biological basis, yet in both, psychological and social factors influence when the illness appears, how it runs, and whether the person comes to think of themselves as sick. In 1980, in "The clinical application of the biopsychosocial model" in the American Journal of Psychiatry, he worked the idea through a single case, a man he called Mr Glover, admitted with chest pain from a heart attack, whose dangerous disturbance of heart rhythm was precipitated by the way his doctors handled him, without care for how he felt. A purely biomedical account, Engel argued, would have missed the cause of the most dangerous event of his admission.

Figure 1.10 applies the model to Sunita. At the biological level are a family history of depression, broken sleep and weight loss. At the psychological level are her self-critical thinking and a grief she has never been able to work through. At the social level are a transfer far from her family and the lack of anyone to confide in at the new school. The overlaps matter as much as the circles. Her sleeplessness (biological) deepens her low mood (psychological); a long, tiring daily commute and missed meals (social pressures with bodily effects) drain her further; and her fear that colleagues will gossip about her "weakness" (stigma, a social fact felt as shame) keeps her from asking for help. Each level also suggests its own treatment, which is the model's practical point: an antidepressant and a thyroid check, psychotherapy for the thinking and the grief, and practical help with the transfer, her husband's understanding and a source of support at work.

The biopsychosocial model has become the official philosophy of clinical psychology, psychiatry and much of medicine; it underlies the way health psychology approaches physical illness (Section 9.1) and the biopsychosocial case formulation that Chapter 2 teaches (Section 2.9). It has its critics. The psychiatrist S. Nassir Ghaemi, in The Rise and Fall of the Biopsychosocial Model (2010), argued that it is too vague to guide practice: it tells the clinician to consider everything but not how to weigh one factor against another, so it can be used to justify almost any treatment, an eclecticism in which "anything goes". The model is better described as a framework, a reminder of what must not be left out, than as a theory that makes testable predictions. For a theory of how the factors combine, clinicians turn to the diathesis-stress model.

Exam trap

The biopsychosocial model was proposed by George L. Engel, in Science, in 1977, as an alternative to the biomedical model. The distractors are all famous for something else. Sigmund Freud: the psychodynamic model. Walter B. Cannon (1871 to 1945): the fight-or-flight response, homeostasis (The Wisdom of the Body, 1932) and the Cannon-Bard theory of emotion. Richard S. Lazarus (1922 to 2002): the transactional model of stress and coping, with primary and secondary appraisal (with Susan Folkman, 1984). Emil Kraepelin: the classification of dementia praecox and manic-depressive insanity (Section 1.3). Aaron T. Beck: the cognitive model and the cognitive triad. Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939): coined the term schizophrenia (1908, in his book of 1911).

The diathesis-stress model

Go back to the twin figures quoted earlier. If one identical twin has schizophrenia, the other, who carries exactly the same genes, develops it only about half the time. The genes clearly matter, since the figure for fraternal twins is far lower; but they cannot be the whole story, since half the identical co-twins stay well. Something else must decide whether a genetic risk turns into an illness. The same puzzle appears everywhere in clinical work. Two sisters lose the same mother; one grieves and recovers, the other sinks into depression. Two soldiers survive the same ambush; one has nightmares for a month, the other for twenty years. Why?

The answer that modern clinicians give is the diathesis-stress model. A diathesis (from the Greek for "disposition") is a predisposition or vulnerability to a disorder. It may be biological (genes, an abnormality of brain chemistry, a complication in the womb or at birth), psychological (negative schemas, a pessimistic attributional style, low self-esteem) or social (the early loss of a parent, a childhood of abuse). In this model, stress is the environmental event or circumstance that triggers the vulnerability: a death, a divorce, a failed examination, a move, a physical illness, a traumatic experience, a period of heavy drug use. The model's central claim is that the disorder emerges from the interaction of the two. A diathesis alone usually produces no disorder: the vulnerable person who is lucky enough to meet little stress may never fall ill. Stress alone usually produces no disorder either: most people who meet hardship cope. And the two trade off against each other. The greater the vulnerability, the less stress it takes to trigger the disorder; the smaller the vulnerability, the more stress it takes.

The idea grew out of schizophrenia research. In 1962 the American psychologist Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003) used his presidential address to the American Psychological Association, published as "Schizotaxia, schizotypy, schizophrenia" in American Psychologist, to propose a three-step theory. What is inherited, Meehl suggested, is not schizophrenia but a subtle defect in the way the nervous system integrates information, which he called schizotaxia. Growing up in virtually any environment, a schizotaxic person develops a characteristic personality organisation, schizotypy, marked by what Meehl called "cognitive slippage" (mildly loosened thinking), social aversiveness, a reduced capacity for pleasure (anhedonia) and ambivalence. Most schizotypes, he argued, remain compensated all their lives, odd perhaps but never psychotic. Only those who also meet adverse experiences, without the protection of favourable ones, decompensate into clinical schizophrenia. Meehl's guess that a single dominant gene was responsible has not survived (schizophrenia is now known to be influenced by very many genes of small effect), but his logic, an inherited vulnerability that becomes illness only under the wrong conditions, is the diathesis-stress model, and the term "schizotypy" (Meehl built it on the psychoanalyst Sandor Rado's word "schizotype") lives on in schizotypal personality disorder.

In 1977 Joseph Zubin (1900 to 1990) and Bonnie Spring gave the model its best-known form in "Vulnerability: A new view of schizophrenia", in the Journal of Abnormal Psychology. Their vulnerability model made four claims. First, every person has some degree of vulnerability to schizophrenia, from very low to very high. Vulnerability may be inborn, laid down in the genes and the developing nervous system, or acquired, through traumas, specific diseases, complications around birth, family experiences, adolescent peer relationships and other life events. Second, vulnerability is a relatively enduring trait. Third, an episode of illness occurs when the stress produced by challenging life events, coming from outside or from within the person, exceeds the person's threshold, and that threshold is set by the person's vulnerability; how well the person copes, and how competent they are, can raise or lower it. Fourth, episodes are time-limited: when the stress subsides, the person tends to return to roughly their previous level of functioning. Schizophrenia, on this view, is not a permanent illness but a permanent vulnerability to episodes of illness, which is a far more hopeful message for a patient and family.

Figure 1.11 shows the model in the form Zubin and Spring drew it. Vulnerability runs along the bottom and stress up the side, and the curved line is the threshold. Anyone whose combination of vulnerability and stress falls below the curve stays well; anyone above it has an episode. The curve slopes down from left to right because the more vulnerable a person is, the less stress it takes to push them over. Now place the three people from Section 1.6 on it. Sunita and Devika both lost their mothers in the past year, the same moderate stressor. Devika, with no family history and a life full of support, grieved and recovered; she falls below the threshold. Sunita, whose mother and maternal uncle both had depression and who faced the loss alone, falls above it. Imran's father has bipolar disorder, so his vulnerability is high, and for him a small stressor, a few weeks of sleepless nights before his examinations, was enough to tip him into mania.

Since 1977 the model has spread far beyond schizophrenia. Beck's theory of depression, in which a latent negative schema is activated by a matching loss, is a cognitive diathesis-stress model (as noted in the cognitive model above), and so is the hopelessness theory that grew out of attributional research. Brown and Harris's vulnerability factors make a social one. The same reasoning is applied to post-traumatic stress, substance dependence, eating disorders and personality disorders. And in 2003 a single study showed the interaction of a gene and an environment so neatly that it became one of the most cited papers in psychiatry.

Key study: Caspi and the serotonin transporter gene (2003)

Avshalom Caspi, Terrie Moffitt and their colleagues had followed 847 people born in Dunedin, New Zealand, from the age of three, recording the stressful events in their lives. They now asked whether a gene that affects the transport of serotonin in the brain changed the effect of those events. The gene comes in a short and a long form, and each person carries two copies. Among people with two short copies, the risk of a major depressive episode rose steeply with the number of stressful life events, and severe maltreatment in childhood roughly doubled their risk of depression as adults (about 63 percent against about 30 percent for those who had not been maltreated). Among people with two long copies, childhood maltreatment made little difference. Neither the gene alone nor the experience alone predicted depression; the combination did. The conclusion was a textbook demonstration of gene-environment interaction. The criticism followed: later large studies and meta-analyses, beginning with Risch and colleagues in 2009, failed to confirm this particular gene's effect, and researchers now believe that many genes, each of tiny effect, are involved. The specific finding is doubtful; the principle it illustrated is not.

Exam trap

Whenever a question describes "a predisposition and a trigger", "a vulnerability set off by stress" or "a biological predisposition set off by a stressful situation", the answer is the diathesis-stress model. The options to reject are each wrong for a reason. "Genetic factors only", "environmental factors only", "neurobiological abnormalities only", "purely biological causes", "learned experiences alone", "stress alone regardless of constitution", "unconscious conflicts only" and "supernatural influences" all name one factor, and the model's whole point is the combination. Remember also that the diathesis is the predisposition and the stress is the trigger (not the other way round), that the diathesis need not be genetic, and that the stress-vulnerability model is the same model under another name. Meehl (1962) gave it schizotaxia; Zubin and Spring (1977) gave it the vulnerability threshold.

Three other names appear beside the diathesis-stress model in examination options, and you should know exactly what each is so that you can reject it with confidence (Table 1.16). The neurodevelopmental model is a real and important theory, but a narrower one. Proposed for schizophrenia in 1987 by Daniel Weinberger and, independently, by Robin Murray and Shôn Lewis, it holds that the disorder begins with a disturbance of brain development in the womb or early life (from genes, prenatal infection, malnutrition or birth complications), which stays silent until the brain matures in adolescence and early adulthood, when the symptoms appear. It is a theory about when and how the brain abnormality arises, not a general account of predisposition and trigger, and it is compatible with the diathesis-stress model rather than a rival to it. (The neurodevelopmental disorders, such as autism and intellectual disability, are a separate DSM chapter and belong to the Disorders book.) "Clinical-reductionist model" and "syndrome-sign model" are not established models at all. Each borrows a real word to sound plausible: reductionism is a criticism of explaining disorder at a single, usually biological, level (see the biological model above), and signs and symptoms clustered into syndromes are the descriptive vocabulary of the medical model, which tells you what a disorder looks like but says nothing about why one person develops it and another does not.

Table 1.16 Model names that appear together in examination options.
Name in the optionsWhat it really isPredisposition plus trigger?
Diathesis-stress (stress-vulnerability) modelDisorder from the interaction of a vulnerability and a triggering stress (Meehl 1962; Zubin and Spring 1977)Yes: this is its definition
Biopsychosocial modelBiological, psychological and social factors interact in all illness (Engel 1977)Not specifically; a broad framework
Neurodevelopmental modelEarly disturbance of brain development, silent until adolescence or adulthood (Weinberger; Murray and Lewis, 1987)No: a theory of when and how brain abnormality arises
Biomedical (medical) modelDisorder as disease of the body, explained by biological variablesNo: one level only
"Clinical-reductionist model"Not an established model; reductionism is a criticismNo
"Syndrome-sign model"Not an established model; the descriptive vocabulary of medicineNo: describes, does not explain

Vulnerability, protection and resilience

The original diathesis-stress model has two ingredients. The modern versions add a third, because clinical experience shows that two people with the same vulnerability and the same stress can still end up differently. In 1984 Keith H. Nuechterlein and Michael E. Dawson set out a vulnerability-stress model of schizophrenic episodes that named the missing ingredient. Alongside personal vulnerability factors (such as reduced information-processing capacity, autonomic hyperreactivity to stress and poor social competence) and environmental stressors (a critical, emotionally over-involved family, an overstimulating social environment, stressful life events), it listed protective factors, features of the person or the environment that reduce the chance that a vulnerable person under stress will become ill. Personal protective factors included good coping skills, self-efficacy and antipsychotic medication; environmental ones included a family that solves problems effectively and supportive psychosocial intervention. The model explains in one picture why so many different treatments help: medication lowers biological vulnerability, family psychoeducation reduces stress at home, and skills training strengthens coping. In the language of Figure 1.11, each of them either lowers the stress or raises the threshold. This expanded vulnerability-stress-protective factors model is the one most clinicians now carry in their heads.

The other side of vulnerability is resilience, positive adaptation in spite of significant adversity. Its most famous demonstration is a study begun on the Hawaiian island of Kauai by the developmental psychologist Emmy Werner (1929 to 2017) and Ruth Smith. They followed all 698 children born on the island in 1955, from before birth into adulthood. About a third of them were at high risk: born into poverty, with complications at birth, family discord, or a parent with mental illness or alcohol problems. Two-thirds of these high-risk children did develop serious learning or behaviour problems, delinquency or mental health problems by late adolescence, but about one in three of them grew into competent, confident and caring adults, a result Werner and Smith reported in Vulnerable but Invincible (1982). What protected them was not extraordinary: an easy temperament, at least one stable relationship with a caring adult (often a grandparent, an older sibling or a teacher rather than a parent), reasonable problem-solving ability, and a sense of faith or purpose. The developmental psychologist Ann Masten summed up this whole line of research in 2001 by calling resilience "ordinary magic": it comes not from rare qualities but from the ordinary human adaptive systems (close relationships, a healthy brain, self-regulation, the motivation to master one's world) working as they should.

Research since the 1970s has added two further twists to the relation between genes and environments. The first is that they are not independent. In gene-environment correlation, also called the reciprocal gene-environment model, a person's genes help to shape the environments they meet. The behaviour geneticist Robert Plomin and his colleagues distinguished three kinds in 1977: passive (parents give a child both their genes and a home that reflects those genes, so a child of anxious parents inherits both an anxious temperament and an anxious household), evocative, also called reactive (a child's inherited temperament draws particular responses from others, so an irritable child is scolded more) and active (people seek out environments that suit their inherited dispositions, so an impulsive young man drifts towards risky friends and risky situations). The stress in the diathesis-stress model, in other words, is not always independent of the diathesis. The second twist reverses the meaning of vulnerability. The developmental psychologist Jay Belsky and Michael Pluess argued in 2009, in a paper titled "Beyond diathesis stress", that some of the traits treated as vulnerabilities are better described as sensitivity to the environment: the same highly reactive children who do worst in harsh homes do best of all in supportive ones. Their differential susceptibility hypothesis is popularly described with the image of orchids, which wilt in poor conditions but flourish spectacularly in good ones, and dandelions, which grow moderately well almost anywhere. For a clinician the message is hopeful: the child who is most vulnerable may also be the one who gains most from help.

Clinical link

The vulnerability-stress-protective factors model is the backbone of psychoeducation. Telling Sunita, or the family of a young man after his first psychotic episode, "you have an illness that you will always have" is frightening and often false. Telling them "you have a vulnerability; episodes come when stress goes beyond what you can carry; and there are things we can do to lower the stress, raise your threshold and catch the warning signs early" is accurate, removes blame and gives everyone a job to do. It also tells the clinician what to ask about: the vulnerabilities, the recent stressors and the protective factors (Sunita's husband, her faith, her pride in her teaching) on which recovery can be built. The 5 Ps formulation in Section 2.9 is this model written down for one person.

One model or many?

Each of the eight single models in this section began as a rebellion against the others, and for much of the twentieth century their followers behaved like rival schools, each convinced that it alone had the truth. Today most clinicians see the models as lenses that bring different levels of the same person into focus. The biological lens shows Sunita's genes and sleep, the psychodynamic lens her ambivalent grief, the behavioural lens her lost rewards, the cognitive lens her self-critical thoughts, the humanistic-existential lens her collapsed sense of worth and meaning, the sociocultural lens her isolation, and the family-systems lens the critical circle at home. None of these stories is false, and none is complete. The biopsychosocial model insists that all the levels be kept in view, and the diathesis-stress model, with its protective factors, explains how they combine in a particular person at a particular time.

The models still differ in what they recommend, and choosing a treatment means choosing among them, or combining them. Combining techniques from different therapies according to what works for a given problem is called eclecticism; building a single coherent therapy out of several models is called integration. How therapists combine therapies, the four routes to integration, and the evidence on whether any one therapy is better than another are the subject of Chapter 8 (Sections 8.3 and 8.11). Before any therapy can begin, however, the clinician must be able to work ethically, and that is where the next two sections turn.

Remember

Single models answer "what causes disorder?" at one level each: supernatural (spirits), biological (body and brain), psychodynamic (unconscious conflict), behavioural (learning), cognitive (thinking), humanistic-existential (blocked growth and meaning), sociocultural (society and culture) and family-systems (the family as a system). The two integrative models answer "how do the levels combine?": the biopsychosocial model (Engel, 1977) says all three levels always interact; the diathesis-stress model (Meehl, 1962; Zubin and Spring, 1977) says disorder appears when stress exceeds a threshold set by vulnerability, and its modern form adds protective factors and resilience.

1.8 Ethics I: Principles, Competence, Consent and ConfidentialityHigh yield · 15 Qs

Picture your first month as a trainee in the outpatient department of a large hospital. A young man you have seen twice leans forward at the end of the session and says, "Can I tell you something? But first promise me you won't tell anyone." What do you say? If you promise, you may have to break the promise a minute later, because what he is about to tell you could be that he plans to kill himself, or someone else. If you refuse, he may say nothing at all, and you will never learn what he was carrying. There is a third answer, and by the end of this section you will be able to give it without hesitating. Almost every idea in professional ethics is folded inside that one exchange: what you owe the person in front of you, what you owe other people, what you are competent to do, what the client has a right to know before he speaks, and where the walls of the consulting room end.

Why a helping profession needs a code of ethics

ethics, in the sense used in this chapter, is the set of standards that tells the members of a profession how they ought to behave in their professional roles. It is worth separating three words that students often run together. Morals are a person's own beliefs about right and wrong, learned from family, religion and culture. Law is the set of rules that the state enforces with courts and penalties. Professional ethics sits between them: rules and ideals that a profession writes for itself and enforces through its own bodies, by warnings, suspension or removal from the register.
