The three usually agree, but not always. A romantic relationship with a former client may break no law and still be a serious ethical violation. Law sets the floor; ethics asks for more. The code of the American Psychological Association (APA) says so directly: where the code sets a higher standard than the law, psychologists must meet the higher standard, and where ethics and law truly conflict, psychologists make their commitment to the code known and try to resolve the conflict responsibly, but may never use the law as an excuse for violating human rights.

Why does a profession of helpers need written rules at all? There are three reasons. The first is power. People come to a clinical psychologist when they are frightened, confused or desperate, and in the course of treatment they reveal secrets they have told nobody else. They may develop intense feelings towards the therapist (the transference of Section 3.3), and they often cannot judge whether the treatment they are getting is any good. That combination of vulnerability and dependence makes exploitation easy and its detection hard. The second reason is public trust. Society allows a profession to regulate itself, to decide who may practise and to discipline its own members (in India through the Rehabilitation Council of India, Section 1.5), and in return the profession promises to hold its members to published standards. The third reason is guidance. Good intentions are not enough when two good intentions collide, and the collisions are predictable enough that it is worth thinking them through in advance.

The counselling writer Gerald Corey and his colleagues distinguish two levels of ethical functioning that map neatly onto how codes are built. mandatory ethics is the minimum: obeying the rules in order to stay out of trouble. aspirational ethics is the highest level: trying to do what is best for the client and for society, following the spirit of the code and not merely its letter. A practitioner who never technically breaks a rule but treats clients carelessly is functioning at the mandatory level only. You will see the same split in a moment, when the APA code divides itself into aspirational principles and enforceable standards.

Not every ethical problem is a dilemma. Sometimes you know exactly what is right and are simply tempted to do something else, such as accepting an expensive gift or reading a celebrity client's file out of curiosity. An ethical dilemma, by contrast, is a situation in which two or more ethical obligations conflict, so that honouring one means compromising another: keeping a client's confidence versus protecting the person he has threatened, respecting a teenager's privacy versus keeping her parents informed. Dilemmas are where ethical theory earns its keep.

Psychology's first code was itself built from real dilemmas. When the APA decided after the Second World War that its rapidly growing clinical membership needed written standards, a committee chaired by Nicholas Hobbs (1915 to 1983) did not draft rules from an armchair. It asked members to describe actual situations in which they had had to make an ethically difficult decision, and built the code out of those incidents. The result, Ethical Standards of Psychologists, appeared in 1953. It was revised many times (the code itself lists versions of 1959, 1963, 1968, 1977, 1979, 1981, 1990 and 1992) before the version in force today was adopted in 2002.

Ways of reasoning about right and wrong

Before looking at the codes, it helps to know the three or four ways in which philosophers have answered the question "what makes an action right?", because entrance papers ask about them directly, and because every code mixes them.

The first answer is that some actions are right or wrong in themselves, whatever their consequences. This is deontological ethics (from the Greek deon, duty), the ethics of duties and rules. Its most famous exponent, the German philosopher Immanuel Kant (1724 to 1804), argued that a moral rule must be one you could will to be a universal law for everyone (the categorical imperative), and that people must always be treated as ends in themselves and never merely as means to someone else's goals. For a deontologist, lying to a client is wrong even when the lie would comfort her, and using a client to further your research career is wrong even if the research would help thousands. The strength of the approach is that it protects individuals from being sacrificed for the greater good. Its weakness shows when two duties clash (the duty to keep a confidence and the duty to prevent harm), because a pure rule-based system has no principled way to rank them.

The second answer is that an action is right if it produces the best consequences. This is utilitarianism, the best-known form of consequentialism. The English philosopher Jeremy Bentham (1748 to 1832) proposed judging every action by the amount of pleasure and pain it produces, and John Stuart Mill (1806 to 1873) refined the idea into the principle of the greatest happiness for the greatest number. A utilitarian runs, in effect, a cost-benefit analysis: breaking a confidence is justified if the harm it prevents outweighs the harm it does. The strength of this view is that it takes outcomes seriously; its weakness is that, applied crudely, it can justify harming one person for the benefit of many, which is exactly what happened in some of the research scandals described below.

Notice how a real rule blends the two. The APA's standard on deception in research (described in Section 1.8.7) is utilitarian at its core, since deception is allowed only when the study's prospective value is significant and no honest method is feasible, but it is fenced by deontological limits: no deception about anything expected to cause physical pain or severe emotional distress, whatever the benefits.

The third answer moves the question from the act to the person. virtue ethics, which goes back to the Greek philosopher Aristotle (384 to 322 BCE; Section 1.2), asks not "which rule applies?" but "what would a good person, a person of practical wisdom, do here?" In 1996 Naomi M. Meara, Lyle Schmidt and Jeanne Day argued that psychology needed both principles and virtues, and named four virtues for psychologists: prudence, integrity, respectfulness and benevolence. Rules cannot anticipate every situation; character fills the gaps.

A fourth voice is the ethics of care, associated with the American psychologist Carol Gilligan (born 1936), whose book In a Different Voice (1982) argued that Lawrence Kohlberg's stages of moral reasoning rewarded an abstract ethic of justice and rights and undervalued an ethic of care, rooted in relationships and responsibility to particular people, which she found more often in women's reasoning. Kohlberg's stages and Gilligan's critique belong to the Developmental book; for a clinician, the ethics of care is a reminder that ethical action happens inside relationships, not only in the application of rules.

Finally, the Scottish philosopher William David Ross (1877 to 1971), in The Right and the Good (1930), offered a bridge between rules and judgement. He proposed a set of prima facie duties (fidelity, reparation, gratitude, justice, beneficence, self-improvement and non-injury), each binding "at first appearance" but able to be outweighed by a stronger duty in a particular situation. That idea, that principles are binding but not absolute and must be weighed against each other case by case, is the foundation of the principle-based ethics that dominates health care today.

Exam trap

"How does the deontological approach differ from the utilitarian perspective?" Deontology judges an action by its intrinsic nature, as right or wrong in itself and in keeping with duty (Kant); utilitarianism judges it by its consequences, in a cost-benefit way (Bentham, Mill). The commonest distractor simply reverses the two ("deontology emphasises cost-benefit analysis, utilitarianism focuses on duties"); others claim they are identical or that both focus on duties. Virtue ethics (Aristotle) asks about character, not acts or outcomes.

The four principles of biomedical ethics

In 1979 two American scholars, the philosopher Tom L. Beauchamp and the religious ethicist James F. Childress, published Principles of Biomedical Ethics, revised through many editions since and still the most influential book in health-care ethics. Beauchamp had also worked on the staff of the American commission that wrote the Belmont Report on research ethics in the same years (Section 1.8.7), and the two documents share their spirit. Beauchamp and Childress argued that the ethics of health care can be organised around four prima facie principles, in Ross's sense: each is binding, none automatically trumps the others, and in a hard case they must be specified and balanced. This approach is often called principlism, and its four principles are the "four pillars" that examiners ask about.

  • respect for autonomy: the obligation to respect the right of a capable person to make decisions about his own life and treatment, including decisions you disagree with. Informed consent, truth-telling and confidentiality all flow from it. India's Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 puts the same idea into law: every person, including a person with mental illness, is deemed to have the capacity to make decisions about his mental health care or treatment if he has the ability to understand the information relevant to the decision, or to appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of a decision or of not deciding, or to communicate the decision by speech, expression, gesture or any other means (the Act joins the three abilities with "or"); and a decision that others think inappropriate or wrong does not by itself show a lack of capacity (Section 4 of the Act; Section 1.10 covers the Act as a whole).
  • nonmaleficence (also spelt non-maleficence): the obligation not to inflict harm. The word comes from the Latin for "not doing evil", and the principle is usually summed up in the Latin maxim primum non nocere, "first, do no harm". In psychology, harm can come from incompetence, from exploitation, from breaking a confidence carelessly, or from the treatment itself: some clients get worse in therapy (Section 8.9), and a few "therapies" have killed. In April 2000 a ten-year-old American girl, Candace Newmaker, died of suffocation during a so-called rebirthing session in which she was wrapped tightly in a sheet to simulate birth; her therapists were convicted, and Colorado banned the practice.
  • beneficence: the positive obligation to act for the client's good, to promote his welfare and to provide help that actually works. Beneficence is why a psychologist has a duty to offer treatments with evidence behind them (Section 8.8) and to refer a client she cannot help. Pushed too far, beneficence turns into paternalism, overriding a person's choices "for his own good", and that is where it collides with autonomy.
  • justice: the obligation to distribute benefits, risks and costs fairly and to treat people equally regardless of caste, religion, gender, class, language or disability. In India justice is not an abstraction: most people who need mental health care never receive it (Section 1.5 describes the treatment gap), and who gets a psychologist's time is itself an ethical question.

The principles constantly pull against one another, which is why they are prima facie and not absolute. Exposure therapy for a phobia (Section 4.3) deliberately causes distress, a short-term harm, for a long-term benefit; nonmaleficence yields to beneficence, with the client's informed consent. A man with depression who refuses admission is exercising autonomy, but if he is acutely suicidal, beneficence and nonmaleficence may justify overriding his choice within the law. No principle settles such cases mechanically. What the principles do is make you name what is at stake.

Psychology then added a fifth principle of its own. In 1984 Karen Strohm Kitchener, writing for counselling psychologists, adopted Beauchamp and Childress's four principles and added fidelity: faithfulness to commitments, keeping promises, loyalty and honesty, which are the foundation of any relationship of trust. Kitchener also proposed that ethical reasoning works at two levels: an intuitive level, the ordinary moral sense that handles most everyday decisions quickly, and a critical-evaluative level, used when intuition is unclear or conflicted, at which the professional reasons explicitly from rules and codes, then from principles, and finally from ethical theory. The American Counseling Association's code of 2014 lists six principles, Kitchener's five plus veracity, the obligation to be truthful. Table 1.17 lines up these frameworks, because examiners like to swap a principle from one list into another.

Table 1.17 Principle-based frameworks compared. Watch which list a principle belongs to.
FrameworkYearPrinciplesNumber
Beauchamp and Childress, Principles of Biomedical Ethics1979Respect for autonomy, nonmaleficence, beneficence, justice4
Belmont Report (research ethics)1979Respect for persons, beneficence, justice3
Kitchener (counselling psychology)1984Autonomy, nonmaleficence, beneficence, justice, fidelity5
APA General Principles2002Beneficence and Nonmaleficence; Fidelity and Responsibility; Integrity; Justice; Respect for People's Rights and Dignity5
American Counseling Association code2014Autonomy, nonmaleficence, beneficence, justice, fidelity, veracity6
Exam trap

The four pillars of principle-based ethics (Beauchamp and Childress) are beneficence, nonmaleficence, justice and respect for autonomy. Integrity is not one of them (it is an APA General Principle and one of Meara's virtues), so options containing integrity are wrong; an option offering "autonomy, beneficence, justice and respect" has dropped nonmaleficence. Questions on single principles are matching exercises (Real PYQ 2024): nonmaleficence = refrain from causing harm; beneficence = act in the client's best interest and promote welfare; autonomy = respect the client's right to self-determination; justice = be fair in distributing services and resources. Primum non nocere is a later Latin maxim, not a line of the Hippocratic Oath (the closest Hippocratic text, in the Epidemics, says "to help, or at least to do no harm").

The APA Ethics Code: five General Principles and ten sections of Standards

The code that entrance papers ask about by name is the APA's Ethical Principles of Psychologists and Code of Conduct. It is binding only on APA members (and on psychologists in jurisdictions that adopt it), so an Indian psychologist is not legally governed by it; but it is the model on which most other codes, and most textbook teaching, are built. Get its dates right first. It was adopted by the APA Council of Representatives on 21 August 2002 and became effective on 1 June 2003. It was amended in 2010 (effective 1 June 2010), when the Introduction and Standards 1.02 and 1.03 were changed to say that conflicts between ethics and the law, or between ethics and an employer's demands, may never be used to justify violating human rights; and again on 3 August 2016 (effective 1 January 2017), when Standard 3.04 was expanded to forbid psychologists from taking any part in torture or cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment. Both amendments followed a long controversy over psychologists' involvement in the interrogation of detainees by the United States military, which an independent review commissioned by the APA (the Hoffman report of 2015) found the association's own officials had helped to enable. The APA has since set up a task force to draft a new code; until a revision is adopted, the 2002 code as amended is the one you will be examined on.

The code has four parts: an Introduction and Applicability section, a Preamble, five General Principles (labelled A to E), and the specific Ethical Standards. The distinction between the last two is the single most important thing to understand about the code's structure. The General Principles are, in the code's own words, "aspirational in nature": they are meant "to guide and inspire psychologists toward the very highest ethical ideals of the profession", they "do not represent obligations and should not form the basis for imposing sanctions". The Ethical Standards, by contrast, "set forth enforceable rules for conduct", and a psychologist who breaks one can be disciplined; ignorance of a standard is no defence. The code covers psychologists' professional activities only, not their purely private conduct, and it applies whether the work is done in person, by post, by telephone or over the internet.

Table 1.18 sets out the five General Principles. Read them slowly: most questions on the code are about which principle contains which idea.

Table 1.18 The five General Principles of the APA Ethics Code (2002, amended 2010 and 2016) and what each demands.
PrincipleCore ideaWhat it asks of psychologists
A. Beneficence and NonmaleficenceBenefit people and do no harmSafeguard the welfare and rights of those they work with (and of research animals); resolve conflicts so as to avoid or minimise harm; guard against misuse of their influence; be aware of how their own physical and mental health affects their ability to help
B. Fidelity and ResponsibilityTrust and professional responsibilityEstablish relationships of trust; uphold professional standards; clarify roles; accept responsibility for their behaviour; manage conflicts of interest; consult and refer; care about colleagues' ethical conduct; give some time for little or no payment
C. IntegrityAccuracy, honesty and truthfulnessDo not steal, cheat, commit fraud or misrepresent facts; keep promises and avoid unclear commitments; where deception is justified (as in some research), consider its need and consequences and correct any resulting mistrust or harm
D. JusticeFairness and equal accessAll persons are entitled to access and benefit from psychology and to equal quality of services; guard against their own biases and the limits of their competence leading to unjust practices
E. Respect for People's Rights and DignityDignity, privacy, self-determinationRespect the dignity and worth of all people and their rights to privacy, confidentiality and self-determination; give special safeguards to those whose vulnerabilities impair autonomous decision making; respect cultural, individual and role differences and eliminate the effect of bias

Three details in Table 1.18 are worth fixing in memory because they are counter-intuitive. First, there is no principle called "Autonomy" in the APA code; autonomy appears as "self-determination" inside Principle E, alongside privacy and confidentiality. Second, keeping promises is mentioned in Principle C, Integrity, not in Fidelity, even though fidelity in Kitchener's sense means promise-keeping. Third, self-care has an ethical basis: the last sentence of Principle A asks psychologists to be aware of the effect of their own physical and mental health on their ability to help (Section 8.12 covers burnout and self-care).

The enforceable part of the code, the Ethical Standards, is arranged in ten sections containing 89 numbered standards. Table 1.19 lists the sections, with an example from each that this chapter uses. Standards are cited by number ("Standard 4.05"), and the rest of this section and Section 1.9 refer to them that way.

Table 1.19 The ten sections of the APA Ethical Standards, with examples used in this chapter.
SectionTitleExamples of standards
1Resolving Ethical IssuesConflicts between ethics and law (1.02); informal resolution and reporting of violations (1.04, 1.05)
2CompetenceBoundaries of competence (2.01); emergencies (2.02); maintaining competence (2.03); personal problems (2.06)
3Human RelationsAvoiding harm (3.04); multiple relationships (3.05); third-party requests (3.07); informed consent (3.10)
4Privacy and ConfidentialityMaintaining confidentiality (4.01); discussing its limits (4.02); disclosures (4.05)
5Advertising and Other Public StatementsNo false or deceptive statements (5.01); no testimonials from current clients (5.05)
6Record Keeping and FeesDocumentation and records (6.01, 6.02); fees (6.04); barter (6.05)
7Education and TrainingAssessing supervisees (7.06); no sexual relationships with students or supervisees (7.07)
8Research and PublicationInformed consent to research (8.02); deception (8.07); debriefing (8.08); plagiarism (8.11)
9AssessmentInformed consent in assessment (9.03); release of test data (9.04); test security (9.11)
10TherapyInformed consent to therapy (10.01); sexual intimacies with current and former clients (10.05, 10.08)
Remember

The order of the five principles is fixed and lettered: the two double-barrelled ones come first (A Beneficence and Nonmaleficence, B Fidelity and Responsibility), then the two single words (C Integrity, D Justice), and the longest closes the list (E Respect for People's Rights and Dignity). Principles are aspirational; the ten sections of Standards are enforceable.

Exam trap

"How many General Principles?" Five (A to E), not three (the Belmont Report has three), four (Beauchamp and Childress) or seven; do not confuse them with the ten sections of Ethical Standards. "Which is NOT a General Principle?" Equity is not one (the principle is Justice); nor are Autonomy, Confidentiality, Competence or Veracity as such. "Which principle emphasises accuracy, honesty and truthfulness in the science, teaching and practice of psychology?" Integrity (Principle C), quoted almost word for word from the code; Fidelity and Responsibility is about relationships of trust and professional responsibility. The current code is the 2002 code (effective 2003), amended in 2010 and 2016 (effective 2017); 1992 was the previous version.

Competence

Of all the obligations in the code, competence is the one most often violated without anyone noticing. professional competence means having the knowledge, skills, judgement and attitudes needed to provide a particular service to an acceptable standard, and it is always competence for something: a psychologist can be expert in cognitive therapy for adult depression and incompetent to assess a three-year-old for autism. Standard 2.01 therefore speaks of the boundaries of competence: psychologists provide services, teach and do research only with populations and in areas within the boundaries of their competence, based on their education, training, supervised experience, consultation, study or professional experience. Before working in an area new to them, they obtain the relevant training, supervision or study.

Standard 2.01 also makes cultural competence an ethical requirement rather than an optional extra. Where an understanding of a client's age, gender, gender identity, ethnicity, culture, religion, sexual orientation, disability, language or socioeconomic status is essential to effective work, psychologists must have or obtain the training, consultation or supervision needed, or else refer. In India this clause bites daily. A psychologist trained in Delhi who is posted to a district hospital in coastal Karnataka may meet clients who speak only Tulu, who explain their distress through spirit possession or the evil eye, and whose families expect to sit in on every session. Competence there means learning how such clients understand illness, working with a trained interpreter (Section 2.2), and knowing which tests have never been standardised on people like them.

Two standards soften the rule where rigidity would leave people without help. Under Standard 2.01(d), when a psychologist is asked to serve people for whom no appropriate services exist, a psychologist with closely related training may provide the service while making a reasonable effort to obtain the competence required. Under Standard 2.02, in an emergency, a psychologist may provide services outside her competence so that services are not denied, but must stop as soon as the emergency ends or appropriate services become available. A child psychologist who is the only mental health professional at the scene of a bus accident does what she can for the injured adults; she does not then take them on for long-term trauma therapy that she has never been trained to give.

Competence is not acquired once. Standard 2.03 requires ongoing efforts to develop and maintain competence, and Standard 2.04 requires that work be based on established scientific and professional knowledge, which is the ethical root of evidence-based practice (Section 8.8). In India, the RCI links the renewal of registration to points earned through Continuing Rehabilitation Education (Section 1.5 describes registration). Competence can also be lost. Standard 2.06 asks psychologists not to begin work when their personal problems are likely to prevent them from performing competently, and, when they become aware of such problems, to seek consultation or help and to decide whether to limit, suspend or end their work. A psychologist in the middle of a painful divorce, or drinking heavily, or exhausted by caring for a dying parent, may be temporarily unfit to see certain clients. The term professional impairment is used for this state, in which a practitioner's own distress, illness or substance use reduces the quality of her work below an acceptable standard.

Finally, there is competence at ethics itself. ethical competence is the ability to practise ethically, and it has several parts: knowing and understanding the relevant codes and laws; being able to recognise that a situation raises an ethical issue at all (many violations begin with a failure to notice); being able to analyse and resolve ethical dilemmas; and accepting responsibility for one's own actions. This breakdown echoes the four-component model of moral behaviour proposed by the American psychologist James R. Rest (1941 to 1999) in the 1980s: moral sensitivity (noticing that a situation has a moral dimension), moral judgement (working out what ought to be done), moral motivation (giving moral values priority over others such as money or convenience) and moral character (having the persistence and courage to carry the decision through). Competency models for training clinical psychologists, such as the cube model of Emil Rodolfa and colleagues (2005), list ethical and legal standards as one of the foundational competencies on which every functional skill (assessment, intervention, supervision, research) rests.

Exam trap

"What does ethical competency refer to?" The keyed answer is all of the above: knowledge and understanding of ethics codes, the ability to recognise, analyse and resolve ethical situations, and responsibility for one's own actions. Any single option is incomplete. On competence itself: a degree or a registration does not make a psychologist competent in every technique or population (boundaries of competence, Standard 2.01); the emergency exception (2.02) ends when the emergency ends; and impairment from personal problems is an ethical issue (2.06), not only a private one.

Informed consent to assessment and therapy

Respect for autonomy becomes practical through one procedure. informed consent is a person's voluntary agreement to a specific intervention, given while he has the capacity to decide and after he has been told, in a way he can understand, what the intervention involves, its likely benefits and risks, and the alternatives. India's Mental Healthcare Act defines it in almost exactly those terms: consent given for a specific intervention, without any force, undue influence, fraud, threat, mistake or misrepresentation, and obtained after disclosing to the person adequate information, including the risks and benefits of, and alternatives to, the intervention, in a language and manner he understands (Section 2(1)(i)).

Barlow and Durand summarise the basic components as four: competence (the person is capable of deciding), voluntariness (he is not coerced), full information (he has what he needs to decide) and comprehension (he understands it). Each can fail in a clinic. Capacity can be impaired by acute psychosis, intoxication, dementia or severe intellectual disability. Voluntariness is undermined when a husband says "she will do whatever the doctor says", or when a client fears that refusing one treatment will cost him all treatment. Information fails when the risks are skipped because "patients worry too much". Comprehension fails when a client who reads only Kannada signs an English form he cannot read. A signature on such a form records consent; it does not create it.

What must the client be told? For therapy, Standard 10.01 requires psychologists to inform clients, as early as feasible, about the nature and anticipated course of therapy, fees, the involvement of any third parties, and the limits of confidentiality, and to give them a chance to ask questions and receive answers. Three further rules matter in training settings and in new areas of work. If a treatment has no generally recognised techniques and procedures, the client must be told that it is still developing, what the potential risks and alternatives are, and that participation is voluntary (10.01b). If the therapist is a trainee and legal responsibility lies with a supervisor, the client must be told that the therapist is in training and being supervised, and must be given the supervisor's name (10.01c). And when services are court ordered or otherwise mandated, the client must be told so, and told the limits of confidentiality, before anything begins (3.10c). For assessment, Standard 9.03 requires consent covering the nature and purpose of the assessment, fees, the involvement of third parties and the limits of confidentiality, with three exceptions: when testing is mandated by law, when consent is implied because testing is a routine institutional activity (applying for a job that requires a test), and when one purpose of the assessment is to evaluate decisional capacity itself. Consent, permission and assent must be documented (3.10d).

Informed consent is best understood as a process, not an event. It begins in the first session, it is revisited when the plan changes (moving from talking about a phobia to exposure exercises in a crowded market), and the client may withdraw it at any time. In Indian practice it also has a family dimension that Western textbooks rarely discuss. An adult client is often brought by relatives who expect to be told everything and to take the decisions. The family's involvement can be a great therapeutic asset (Chapter 7), but the decision to accept treatment, and to share information, still belongs to the adult client if he has capacity. The clinician's task is to involve the family with the client's agreement, not instead of it.

Children and adolescents raise a special problem, because a minor cannot legally consent. In India a minor is a person who has not completed eighteen years (the Indian Majority Act, 1875; the Mental Healthcare Act uses the same definition). Standard 3.10(b) sets out what psychologists do for any person legally incapable of consenting: they give an appropriate explanation, seek the person's assent (his own agreement, which is not legally sufficient but is ethically required), consider his preferences and best interests, and obtain permission from a legally authorised person, usually a parent or guardian. In England, the House of Lords' 1985 decision in the Gillick case established that a child under sixteen who has sufficient understanding and intelligence may consent to medical treatment on his own behalf, a standard now called Gillick competence; Indian law has no settled equivalent for psychological treatment, so parental consent with the young person's assent remains the safe practice. (How the Mental Healthcare Act handles the admission and treatment of minors is described in Section 1.10.)

Consider Zoya, a fifteen-year-old Class 10 student in Mysuru whose mother brings her for "exam stress and mood swings". The mother consents; Zoya, asked directly, agrees to come and assents. But the first real conversation must be about confidentiality, with both of them in the room. Zoya will not talk freely if she believes every word goes home. Her mother, who is paying and who is responsible for her, reasonably wants to know how she is doing. The usual agreement is that the mother will hear about general progress and the plan, but not the content of what Zoya says, unless Zoya is at serious risk (of self-harm, of abuse, or of harming someone), in which case the psychologist will tell the parent, and will tell Zoya first. Negotiating this at the start prevents the far worse conversation of breaking a trust that was never properly defined.

Exam trap

Informed consent is a principle and a process; the consent form is only its written record, which is why "consent form" is a distractor when the stem describes "obtaining participants' permission after informing them about the study". Assent is the agreement of a person who cannot legally consent (a child); it is ethically required but does not replace the consent (permission) of a legally authorised person. A trainee must tell clients that he is a trainee and give the supervisor's name (Standard 10.01c). Mandated and court-ordered clients still receive full information, including that the service is mandated and the limits of confidentiality (3.10c).

Informed consent in research: from Nuremberg to the ICMR

The rules on consent did not grow gently out of good manners. They were forced on the research professions by scandals, and each scandal left a mark you can still see in today's codes. Table 1.20 sets out the landmarks; the stories behind them follow.

Table 1.20 Landmarks in the ethics of research with human participants.
YearEvent or documentWhat it established or exposed
1947Nuremberg CodeTen principles after the trial of Nazi doctors; "the voluntary consent of the human subject is absolutely essential"
1932 to 1972Tuskegee syphilis studyDeception and denial of treatment to poor Black men in the United States; exposed in 1972
1963Milgram's obedience studies publishedDeception and severe stress in psychological research; Baumrind's critique (1964)
1964Declaration of Helsinki (World Medical Association)Physicians' own code for medical research; later revisions added review by an independent ethics committee
1966Beecher's article in the New England Journal of Medicine22 examples of unethical studies published in respectable journals
1979Belmont ReportRespect for persons, beneficence, justice; applied as informed consent, risk-benefit assessment, fair selection of participants
2017ICMR National Ethical GuidelinesIndia's guidelines for biomedical and health research with human participants; review by institutional ethics committees

At the end of the Second World War, American judges at Nuremberg tried twenty-three German doctors and officials for experiments performed on concentration camp prisoners without their consent: freezing, high-altitude, infection and sterilisation experiments, many of them fatal. The judgement, delivered in August 1947, set out ten principles for permissible medical experiments that became known as the Nuremberg Code. Its first principle is its most famous: "The voluntary consent of the human subject is absolutely essential." The others require that the experiment be likely to yield results for the good of society, that it avoid unnecessary suffering, that the risk never exceed the humanitarian importance of the problem, that it be conducted by qualified scientists, that the participant be free to stop at any time, and that the scientist be ready to stop if continuing is likely to cause injury or death. In 1964 the World Medical Association, the international body of national medical associations, adopted its own code at its meeting in Helsinki. The Declaration of Helsinki has been revised many times; from 1975 it required research protocols to be reviewed by an independent committee, the origin of today's ethics committees.

Codes on paper did not stop abuse at home. In 1966 the Harvard anaesthetist Henry K. Beecher (1904 to 1976) published "Ethics and clinical research" in the New England Journal of Medicine, describing 22 examples of unethical studies that had appeared in leading journals, including the deliberate infection of children at an institution for the intellectually disabled with hepatitis. The case that finally forced change, however, was Tuskegee.

Key study: The Tuskegee syphilis study (1932 to 1972)

The question. In 1932 the United States Public Health Service set out to observe the natural course of untreated syphilis in Black men in Macon County, Alabama. The method. About 600 poor, mostly illiterate men were enrolled, roughly 400 with latent syphilis and 200 without it as controls. They were never told they had syphilis; they were told they were being treated for "bad blood", and were offered free meals, medical examinations and burial insurance. Painful spinal taps, done purely for research, were presented as "special free treatment". When penicillin became the standard cure in the late 1940s, it was withheld, and the men were steered away from treatment elsewhere.

The result. The study ran for forty years. It ended only in 1972, after a Public Health Service employee, Peter Buxtun, took his objections to the press and the Associated Press reporter Jean Heller made the story public. By then dozens of the men had died of syphilis or its complications, wives had been infected and children born with congenital syphilis. The conclusion. The scandal led directly to the National Research Act of 1974, the national commission that wrote the Belmont Report, and federal rules requiring ethics review of research. In 1997 President Bill Clinton formally apologised to the survivors. Tuskegee is the textbook illustration of two principles at once: consent is meaningless without disclosure, and justice is violated when the burdens of research fall on the poor and powerless.

Psychology had its own reckoning. In 1963 Stanley Milgram (1933 to 1984) of Yale University reported that 26 of his first 40 participants (65 percent) obeyed an experimenter's instructions to give what they believed were increasingly painful electric shocks, up to 450 volts, to a protesting man in the next room. (What the studies show about obedience is taught in the Social Psychology book.) Milgram's participants had been deceived about the purpose of the study, and many showed extreme stress: sweating, trembling, nervous laughter, and in a few cases uncontrollable seizures. In 1964 the developmental psychologist Diana Baumrind (1927 to 2018) argued in American Psychologist that the studies had damaged participants' dignity, self-esteem and trust in authority, and that no scientific gain justified this. Milgram replied that every participant had been debriefed and reunited with the unharmed "victim", and that in a follow-up questionnaire 84 percent said they were glad to have taken part and only about 1 percent were sorry. The debate was never fully settled, but it changed the rules: Milgram's procedure in its original form would not pass a modern ethics committee. When Jerry M. Burger partially replicated it in 2009, he stopped the procedure at 150 volts and screened out vulnerable participants.

The American response to these scandals was the Belmont Report (1979), named after the conference centre where its authors met. It reduced research ethics to three principles: respect for persons (treating individuals as autonomous agents and protecting those with diminished autonomy), beneficence (do not harm; maximise possible benefits and minimise possible harms) and justice (distribute the burdens and benefits of research fairly). Each principle has an application: respect for persons requires informed consent, with its three elements of information, comprehension and voluntariness; beneficence requires a systematic assessment of risks and benefits; and justice requires the fair selection of participants, so that the poor, the institutionalised or minorities are not chosen simply because they are easy to recruit.

The APA code turns these ideas into specific rules. Under Standard 8.02, participants must be told the purpose, expected duration and procedures of the research; their right to decline and to withdraw once it has begun; the foreseeable consequences of declining or withdrawing; the risks, discomforts or adverse effects that might influence their willingness to take part; any prospective benefits; the limits of confidentiality; any incentives for participation; and whom to contact with questions. Where the research tests an experimental treatment, they must also be told about the control group, how assignment to groups will be made, and what alternative treatments are available. Standard 8.04 protects clients, students and subordinates from adverse consequences if they decline, and requires an equitable alternative when research participation is a course requirement. Standard 8.05 allows consent to be dispensed with only for research that would not reasonably be expected to cause distress or harm, such as anonymous questionnaires, naturalistic observation and archival research, or where law or institutional regulations permit. Standard 8.06 forbids inducements so large that they coerce.

Two terms are tested again and again. deception in research means giving participants false information, or withholding information, about the purpose or procedures of a study. Standard 8.07 allows it only when the study's significant prospective scientific, educational or applied value justifies it and effective non-deceptive alternatives are not feasible; never when the research is reasonably expected to cause physical pain or severe emotional distress; and always with the deception explained as early as feasible, at the latest at the end of data collection, with participants allowed to withdraw their data. debriefing is the procedure after participation in which participants are given full information about the nature, results and conclusions of the research and any misconceptions are corrected (Standard 8.08); if procedures have harmed a participant, the researcher takes reasonable steps to minimise the harm. In 1976 the American psychologist David S. Holmes described the two jobs a debriefing must do after deception: dehoaxing, convincing participants that they were deceived and telling them the truth, and desensitising, removing any distress or damage to self-esteem the deception caused (for example, by explaining that most people behaved as they did).

Barlow and Durand list the rights that follow for anyone who takes part in psychological research: to be informed about the purpose of the study, to privacy, to be treated with respect and dignity, to be protected from physical and mental harm, to choose to participate or refuse without prejudice or reprisal, to anonymity in the reporting of results, and to the safeguarding of their records. Note the difference between two words in that list. Anonymity means that not even the researcher can link the data to a particular person; confidentiality means that the researcher knows who gave the data but undertakes not to reveal it.

Who checks that all this happens? In the United States, research in universities and hospitals must be approved in advance by an institutional review board (IRB), a committee of scientists and lay members from the community who decide whether participants' rights and welfare are adequately protected; Standard 8.01 requires psychologists to give such bodies accurate information and to follow the approved protocol. In India the equivalent is the institutional ethics committee, which operates under the Indian Council of Medical Research's National Ethical Guidelines for Biomedical and Health Research Involving Human Participants (2017). The ICMR guidelines rest on the same four principles you met in Section 1.8.3, respect for persons (autonomy), beneficence, nonmaleficence and justice, and the ICMR issued separate national guidelines for research involving children in the same year. Dissertation research in Indian clinical psychology programmes normally passes through such a committee before data collection begins.

For research with people who have a mental illness, Indian law adds its own safeguard. Section 99 of the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 requires free and informed consent from every person with mental illness for any research involving interviews or psychological, physical, chemical or medicinal interventions. If a person cannot give consent but does not resist taking part, the research needs the permission of the State Mental Health Authority, which may allow it on the consent of the nominated representative, but only if the research cannot be done with people able to consent, is necessary to promote the mental health of the population the person represents, aims at knowledge relevant to the needs of people with mental illness, involves no conflict of interest, and has institutional ethics committee approval. Anonymised research on case notes is still allowed, and consent may be withdrawn at any time. Barlow and Durand describe why such safeguards matter: in 1988 Greg Aller, a 23-year-old with early schizophrenia, joined a study at the University of California at Los Angeles that later withdrew his medication; he relapsed severely, and federal administrators later found that he and his family had not been given all the information about the risks and the alternatives.

Exam trap

Match the term to the time. Informed consent comes before: participants are told enough to decide whether to take part, and they agree voluntarily (RML 2021, RML 2025, RML practice). Debriefing comes after: participants are given full information about all aspects of the study once it is done (IHBAS 2020); "description", "explanation" and "interpretation" are not technical terms. Confidentiality protects the data throughout ("ensuring confidentiality of data" is the keyed description of research ethics, RML 2023). "Random assignment" is a design procedure, not an ethical principle; "expectation management" and "de-concealment" are invented distractors. Research ethics is never "following legal regulations only": law is the floor, and the Nuremberg Code, Helsinki, Belmont, the APA code and the ICMR guidelines all ask for more.

Confidentiality, privileged communication and privacy

Three words are used loosely in everyday speech and precisely in ethics and law. confidentiality is the professional's ethical (and sometimes legal) duty not to disclose what she learns about a client in the course of her work without the client's consent. It belongs to the professional relationship and binds the professional. privileged communication (or testimonial privilege) is a narrower, purely legal concept: the client's right to prevent the professional from revealing his communications in a court or other legal proceeding. Privilege belongs to the client, who is called its holder; only the client can claim it or waive it. privacy is the broadest of the three: a person's right to decide what information about himself is shared, with whom and when, and to be free from intrusion. Table 1.21 sets them side by side.

Table 1.21 Privacy, confidentiality and privileged communication.
ConceptWhat it isWhose is itWhere it operates
PrivacyThe right to control information about oneself and to be free from intrusionEvery personEverywhere; a constitutional right in India
ConfidentialityThe duty not to disclose what is learnt in a professional relationship without consentA duty of the professional, owed to the clientProfessional practice; set by ethics codes and, in India, Section 23 of the Mental Healthcare Act
Privileged communicationThe right to stop a professional's testimony about one's communications in legal proceedingsThe client (the holder), who may waive itCourts, where a statute or ruling grants it

Why does the difference matter? Because a psychologist can keep a perfect ethical record of confidentiality and still be ordered by a court to testify. In the United States, the Supreme Court recognised a psychotherapist-patient privilege in federal courts in Jaffee v. Redmond (1996), a case about a police officer's counselling sessions with a licensed clinical social worker, reasoning that effective psychotherapy depends on an atmosphere of confidence and trust. India has no such privilege. The Indian Evidence Act of 1872 (replaced from 2024 by the Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam, 2023) protects communications between a client and his legal adviser, but gives no comparable protection to communications with doctors or psychologists, so an Indian court can summon a psychologist and her records. What the psychologist can still do is release only what is ordered and ask the court to limit the disclosure.

Indian law does, however, protect privacy and confidentiality directly. In Justice K. S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India (2017), a nine-judge bench of the Supreme Court held that privacy is a fundamental right protected by Article 21 of the Constitution. The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 gives a person with mental illness a statutory right to confidentiality about his mental health, mental health care, treatment and physical health care (Section 23, whose place among the Act's rights was introduced in Section 2.9.10), forbids the release of photographs or other information about a person being treated in a mental health establishment to the media without his consent, and extends the right to confidentiality to all information stored in electronic or digital form (Section 24). In India, therefore, confidentiality is not merely an ethical courtesy; for a person with mental illness it is a legal right.

Exam trap

Confidentiality is an ethical duty of the professional; privilege is a legal right of the client, used in court, which the client can waive; privacy is the person's broad right to control information about himself. "The privilege belongs to the therapist" is wrong. In India there is no psychotherapist-patient privilege in evidence law, but there is a statutory right to confidentiality for persons with mental illness (MHCA 2017, Section 23), and privacy is a fundamental right (Puttaswamy, 2017).

The limits of confidentiality

Confidentiality is a cornerstone of therapy, but it has never been absolute. The APA code states the duty and its limits in the same breath: psychologists have a "primary obligation" to protect confidential information obtained or stored in any medium, while recognising that the extent and limits of confidentiality may be regulated by law or set by institutional rules (Standard 4.01). They must discuss those limits with clients, including, as far as feasible, those who cannot consent and their legal representatives, at the outset of the relationship and again whenever new circumstances arise (4.02), and they must warn clients who use electronic services about the extra risks to privacy (4.02c). In reports and consultations they include only information germane to the purpose (4.04). When consulting colleagues they do not reveal identifying information unless the client has agreed or it cannot be avoided (4.06), and in lectures and writing they disguise clients or obtain written consent (4.07).

When may a psychologist disclose without consent? Standard 4.05 allows it only when the law requires it, or where the law permits it for a valid purpose: to provide needed professional services, to obtain appropriate professional consultation, to protect the client, the psychologist or others from harm, or to obtain payment, in which case the disclosure is limited to the minimum necessary. Indian law spells out its own list. Section 23(2) of the Mental Healthcare Act requires all health professionals caring for a person with mental illness to keep the information confidential, with these exceptions:

  • release to the nominated representative, to enable him to fulfil his duties under the Act (clause a);
  • release to other mental health professionals and health professionals, to enable them to provide care and treatment (clause b);
  • release if it is necessary to protect any other person from harm or violence (clause c), with the rule that only the information necessary to protect against the harm identified may be released (clause d);
  • release of only such information as is necessary to prevent a threat to life (clause e);
  • release on an order of the Mental Health Review Board, the Central Mental Health Authority, a High Court, the Supreme Court or any other competent statutory authority (clause f); and
  • release in the interests of public safety and security (clause g).

Put the codes and the laws together and the usual limits of confidentiality in clinical practice are these. The client can waive confidentiality himself, by signing a release so that you can send a report to his doctor or speak to his wife. Information is shared within the circle of care: with your supervisor, the treating team and other professionals treating him, which is why he must be told about supervision at the start. Confidentiality yields when there is a serious risk to the client's own life (the suicidal client) or a serious danger of violence to another person (the subject of the next subsection). It yields to the law: in India, the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences (POCSO) Act, 2012 requires any person who knows, or even apprehends, that a sexual offence against a child has been or is likely to be committed to inform the police or the Special Juvenile Police Unit (Section 19), and makes failure to report an offence in itself (Section 21); Chapter 10 describes how this works with children and adolescents. It yields to a court or statutory order, including an order to produce information that a court considers probative of guilt or innocence in a criminal case. And a psychologist may disclose what is needed to defend herself if a client sues her or complains against her. Figure 1.12 arranges these limits in order of how strong the grounds must be.

Two further points complete the picture. In group, family and couples therapy, the psychologist is bound by confidentiality but the other members are not bound in the same way, and Standards 10.02 and 10.03 require the limits to be explained at the outset, including what will happen to secrets one partner tells the therapist alone. And the minimum-necessary rule applies at every step. A psychologist who must tell a threatened person that he is at risk does not also tell him the client's diagnosis, childhood history and sexual secrets.

Clinical link

Here is the third answer to the young man at the start of this section, the one that neither promises nor refuses: "I'm glad you want to tell me, and almost everything you say here stays between us. I can't promise before I hear it, though, because there are a few situations where I would have to act: if you told me you were at serious risk of ending your life, or that someone else was in danger from you, or that a child was being harmed, or if a court ordered me to. If anything like that came up, I would talk to you first about what I need to do. Within those limits, what you tell me stays here." The best time to say this is the first session, before the question is ever asked; the second-best time is the moment it is asked.

Exam trap

"All of the following are TRUE about exceptions to the rule of confidentiality EXCEPT" (RML 2020): the false statement, and so the answer, is "there are never exceptions to the rule of confidentiality". The other three are genuine exceptions: a court order requiring release; information essential to safeguard the health of others (the duty to warn and protect); and information that a court treats as probative of guilt or innocence in a criminal matter. Remember too that ordinary sharing with a supervisor or the treating team is not a breach, provided the client was told, and that even a justified disclosure is limited to the minimum necessary.

Tarasoff and the duty to warn and protect

Of all the limits of confidentiality, the most famous comes from a single American court case about a student from India. It is the case every examiner expects you to know.

Key study: Tarasoff v. Regents of the University of California (1974 and 1976)

The case. Prosenjit Poddar, a graduate student from Bengal, entered the University of California at Berkeley in 1967. In the autumn of 1968 he met Tatiana Tarasoff at a folk dancing class. She did not return his affection, and he became obsessed with her. In 1969 he went for therapy at the university's student health service, where he was seen by a psychologist, Lawrence Moore. Poddar told Moore that he intended to kill a girl, unnamed but readily identifiable as Tatiana, when she returned from spending the summer in Brazil. Moore took the threat seriously. He wrote to the campus police, describing Poddar as suffering from acute and severe paranoid schizophrenia and recommending that he be detained for civil commitment. Three officers took Poddar into custody, but released him when he appeared rational and promised to stay away from Tatiana. The director of the department of psychiatry, Moore's supervisor Harvey Powelson, then ordered that no further action be taken to detain him. Nobody warned Tatiana or her parents. Poddar stopped attending therapy, and on 27 October 1969 he went to her home and stabbed her to death.

The rulings. Tatiana's parents sued the university's Regents, the therapists and the campus police. The lower courts dismissed the claim, on the ground that the therapists owed no duty to someone who was not their patient. In 1974 the Supreme Court of California disagreed: a therapist who has reason to believe that a patient will injure or kill another person has a duty to warn the intended victim (this first decision is often called Tarasoff I). The court then took the unusual step of rehearing the case, and on 1 July 1976 it issued a new opinion (Tarasoff II) that replaced the first and broadened it into a duty to protect: "When a therapist determines, or pursuant to the standards of his profession should determine, that his patient presents a serious danger of violence to another, he incurs an obligation to use reasonable care to protect the intended victim against such danger." The duty might be met by warning the intended victim or others likely to tell the victim of the danger, by notifying the police, or by taking whatever other steps are reasonably necessary. The majority opinion, written by Justice Mathew Tobriner, contains the sentence that sums up the case: "The protective privilege ends where the public peril begins." Justice William Clark dissented, warning that the ruling would deter people from seeking treatment, destroy the trust on which therapy depends and increase unnecessary commitments.

What followed. Poddar was convicted of second-degree murder, but the conviction was overturned because the jury had been inadequately instructed, and he was released on condition that he return to India. The ruling itself spread. Courts and legislatures across the United States adopted versions of it; by 2012, 23 states had a statutory duty to warn or protect, 10 had one in case law, 11 had a permissive duty (disclosure allowed but not required), and 6 had no guidance. The criticism. Clinicians objected that violence cannot be predicted reliably (Section 2.7 shows how poor long-term prediction is) and that the duty puts therapists in the role of police. Supporters reply that the ruling asks only for reasonable professional care, and that warning is only one of several ways to discharge it.

Notice what the 1976 ruling did and did not say. It did not require therapists to warn in every case; it required reasonable care to protect, judged by the standards of the profession. Warning the victim is one option, but so are notifying the police, intensifying treatment, or arranging admission. Later decisions drew the limits. In Thompson v. County of Alameda (1980) the California Supreme Court held that there is no duty to warn when a person makes nonspecific threats against nonspecific people; the danger must be serious and the victim reasonably identifiable. In 1985 California put the duty into its Civil Code, limiting liability to cases in which the patient has communicated a serious threat of physical violence against a reasonably identifiable victim, and providing that the duty is discharged by reasonable efforts to communicate the threat to the victim and to a law enforcement agency. And in Ewing v. Goldstein (2004) a California appeal court held that a threat reported to the therapist by a member of the patient's family can trigger the duty just as a threat from the patient himself can. The same logic of protection applies when the person at risk is the client: with a client at serious risk of suicide, a psychologist may break confidence to the extent needed to protect his life, and the Mental Healthcare Act expressly permits release of the information necessary to prevent a threat to life (Section 23(2)(e)).

Figure 1.13 turns the duty into a sequence of decisions. Its first box is the hardest: deciding whether a threat represents a serious danger of violence is a clinical judgement, made with the risk-assessment methods described in Section 2.7 (past violence, current intent and plan, access to weapons, substance use, and the like).

Where does India stand? No Indian court has laid down a Tarasoff-type duty for therapists, and no Indian statute imposes one in those terms. What Indian law provides is permission rather than a specific command: Section 23(2) of the Mental Healthcare Act allows a professional to release information "if it is necessary to protect any other person from harm or violence", and only so much as is necessary to protect against the harm identified. In the background sits a general duty that applies to every citizen: the Code of Criminal Procedure required any person aware of the commission of, or of another person's intention to commit, certain grave offences, murder among them, to inform a magistrate or the police unless he had a reasonable excuse (Section 39 of the 1973 Code, carried over into Section 33 of the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023). How that general duty applies to a psychologist's confidential sessions has not been worked out by the courts. In practice, an Indian clinician faced with a credible threat against an identifiable person is expected to do what Figure 1.13 shows: assess carefully, take the least intrusive step that will genuinely protect, disclose only what is necessary, consult and document. Section 1.9.11 works through such a case step by step.

Exam trap

The duty to warn or protect an identifiable third party comes from the Tarasoff case. The other options in such questions are forensic rules taught in Chapter 10: the M'Naghten rule (England, 1843: a person is not criminally responsible if, because of a disease of the mind, he did not know the nature and quality of the act or that it was wrong; the basis of Section 84 of the Indian Penal Code, now Section 22 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023); the Durham rule (United States, 1954: not responsible if the act was the product of mental disease or defect); and the Daubert standard (United States Supreme Court, 1993: how judges decide whether expert scientific testimony is admissible). Know the two Tarasoff rulings apart: 1974 (Tarasoff I) = duty to warn; 1976 (Tarasoff II) = the broader duty to protect. It was the Supreme Court of California, not the United States Supreme Court; the therapist was a psychologist; and the victim must be reasonably identifiable (Thompson, 1980).

1.9 Ethics II: Boundaries, Records, Assessment and New SettingsMedium yield · 2 Qs

Section 1.8 dealt with the principles and with the two duties that frame every case: getting consent and keeping confidences. This section moves to the places where ethical trouble actually happens, often slowly and with good intentions: the relationship that becomes something more than professional, the file that is badly kept or carelessly shared, the test result handed to the wrong person, the video session taken from a crowded flat, the trainee who is out of his depth. It ends with a method for reasoning through a dilemma, applied to three cases of the kind an Indian clinician meets in her first year.

Multiple relationships

A multiple relationship (the older and still common term is dual relationship) exists when a psychologist is in a professional role with a person and also has another, different relationship with that person or with someone close to him. The APA code (Standard 3.05) defines three forms. The psychologist may, at the same time, be in another role with the same person: therapist and employer, therapist and teacher, therapist and landlord, therapist and friend. She may be in a relationship with a person closely associated with or related to the client: treating a man while socialising with his wife. Or she may promise to enter into another relationship with the client, or someone close to him, in the future: "once therapy is over, let's go into business together".

Two things about the definition are often missed. First, the code does not ban multiple relationships. It says a psychologist refrains from entering one "if the multiple relationship could reasonably be expected to impair the psychologist's objectivity, competence, or effectiveness" or "otherwise risks exploitation or harm", and it adds, explicitly, that multiple relationships that would not reasonably be expected to cause impairment or risk exploitation or harm "are not unethical". Second, the code expects that some will arise unexpectedly; when they do, the psychologist takes reasonable steps to resolve them with due regard for the client's best interests (3.05b). Related standards cover conflicts of interest (3.06), in which personal, financial or other interests could impair objectivity, and exploitative relationships (3.08), which forbid exploiting anyone over whom the psychologist has supervisory, evaluative or other authority.

What makes some multiple relationships harmful and others harmless? In 1988 Kitchener offered three tests that are still the clearest guide. The potential for harm rises, she argued, as the expectations of the two roles become more incompatible (a friend expects you to take her side; a therapist must sometimes confront); as the obligations of the two roles diverge, dividing your loyalties (an employer must sometimes discipline; a therapist must not); and as the power and prestige differential between professional and client grows, because the greater the power, the greater the room for exploitation. A therapist who is also her client's landlord fails all three tests: she has a financial interest in him, she may one day have to evict him, and he cannot easily complain about either role without endangering the other.

Some multiple relationships cannot be avoided. In a small town, a tribal area, a close religious community, a university campus or the armed forces, the psychologist will meet clients at the market, the temple, the school gate and the wedding hall. Consider Shalini, a 34-year-old schoolteacher in a small hill town in Kodagu, who comes to the only clinical psychologist at the district hospital for help with depression after a miscarriage. She teaches at the school the psychologist's son attends. Refusing to see her would leave her with no service at all; seeing her means that the two will meet at parent-teacher meetings. The defensible course is to accept the case and manage the overlap openly: discuss it with Shalini at the first session, agree that the psychologist will not greet her in public unless she greets first, never discuss school matters in therapy or therapy matters at school, keep the secondary contact as limited as possible, consult a colleague, and note the arrangement in the file. Indian social life creates such overlaps constantly, through extended families, neighbourhood networks and the expectation that a known professional will help "one of our own". The ethical question is never simply "is there another relationship?" but "could this one impair my judgement or harm the client, and what am I doing about it?"

Exam trap

"A dual relationship occurs when" (JEMAS 2025): a psychologist has both a professional and a personal (or other) relationship with a client. It is not two psychologists treating the same client collaboratively (that is co-therapy or team care), a client seeing two therapists at once (a separate issue covered by Standard 10.04, which asks the psychologist to consider the client's welfare and consult the other provider), or a psychologist practising in two clinics. The code does not forbid all multiple relationships, only those that could reasonably be expected to impair objectivity, competence or effectiveness or risk exploitation or harm.

Boundary crossings and boundary violations

Section 3.3 introduced boundaries from the psychodynamic side, as the frame that keeps the transference safe. professional boundaries are the limits that define the therapeutic relationship and keep it focused on the client's welfare: who meets whom, when, where, for how long, for what payment, with what physical contact, with what personal disclosure, in what language and clothing. In 1993 the American psychiatrists Thomas G. Gutheil and Glen O. Gabbard proposed the distinction that every later discussion uses. A boundary crossing is a departure from the usual professional frame that is benign, and sometimes helpful: a brief, considered self-disclosure; accepting a box of sweets at Diwali; walking a frail client to the lift; a session held at the client's workplace during exposure work. A boundary violation is a departure that harms or exploits the client and serves the therapist's needs: business dealings with clients, using them for emotional support, and above all any sexual relationship. Crossings are usually isolated, open, discussed and documented; violations are usually repeated, hidden and rationalised. Table 1.22 summarises the contrast.

Table 1.22 Boundary crossings and boundary violations (after Gutheil and Gabbard, 1993).
FeatureBoundary crossingBoundary violation
Whose needs it servesThe client's, or neutralThe therapist's
EffectHarmless, sometimes helpfulHarmful or exploitative
PatternIsolated, open, discussed and documentedRepeated, secret, rationalised
ExamplesAccepting a small festival gift; brief self-disclosure; a home visit in community care; an in vivo exposure session at the client's officeA romantic or sexual relationship; borrowing money from a client; hiring a client; regular social outings; burdening the client with the therapist's problems

Gutheil and Gabbard also described how violations happen: rarely by a sudden leap, almost always by a slippery slope of small crossings that go unexamined. The sessions run long, then they are scheduled last in the day; first names replace titles; the therapist begins to share her own troubles; there is a coffee after the session, then a meal; and the relationship is sexual before either has admitted where it was heading. This is why the warning sign is not any single crossing but a pattern, especially one the therapist would rather not mention to a supervisor.

Boundaries can also be drawn too rigidly. The psychologist Arnold A. Lazarus argued in 1994 that some boundary rules, applied mechanically, make therapists cold and defensive and reduce their effectiveness, and Gutheil and Gabbard themselves later warned that their distinction was being misused by regulators who treated every crossing as misconduct. The sensible position is that crossings are judged by their purpose and effect. A few practical rules follow, and Indian practice gives each of them its own flavour.

  • Gifts. A token gift (sweets at a festival, a child's drawing, a modest handmade item at termination) can usually be accepted graciously, because refusing it would hurt and would ignore the culture's way of expressing gratitude. Expensive gifts, money, repeated gifts, or gifts that seem to carry an expectation should be declined, kindly, and their meaning explored in therapy.
  • Touch. A handshake, or a hand briefly on the shoulder of a grieving client, may be appropriate; hugging, prolonged touch or any touch the client might experience as sexual is not. An Indian client who bends to touch the therapist's feet in respect, as he would a teacher or elder, is following a cultural norm, not crossing a boundary; the therapist can accept the gesture graciously or gently redirect it without shaming him.
  • Self-disclosure. Brief disclosures that serve the client ("I have also lived away from my family; I know how hard the first year can be") can strengthen the alliance. Disclosures that shift attention to the therapist, or burden the client with the therapist's own problems, are crossings on the way to violations.
  • Time, place and language. Home visits are legitimate in community and rehabilitation work (Sections 9.6 and 9.8), and in vivo exposure takes therapy out of the office by design. Being called "didi", "bhaiya" or "aunty" is ordinary Indian courtesy; what matters is whether the familiarity starts to change what the client expects from the relationship.
Exam trap

"Which of the following is an example of a boundary violation?" (JEMAS 2025): engaging in a romantic relationship with a current client. Providing therapy at the client's workplace can be a legitimate boundary crossing (in vivo work, occupational settings), while maintaining professional rapport and scheduling appointments at convenient times are simply good practice. The crossing-versus-violation distinction is Gutheil and Gabbard's (1993); the "slippery slope" describes how crossings drift into violations.

Sexual relationships with clients

On one boundary, every code is absolute. The APA code (Standard 10.05) says simply: "Psychologists do not engage in sexual intimacies with current therapy clients/patients." The surrounding standards close the obvious loopholes. Psychologists do not engage in sexual intimacies with people they know to be close relatives, guardians or significant others of current clients, and may not end therapy in order to get around this rule (10.06). They do not accept as clients people with whom they have had sexual relationships (10.07). And with former clients the code sets what is often called the two-year rule (10.08): no sexual intimacies for at least two years after therapy ends, and even after two years, not "except in the most unusual circumstances". A psychologist who does form such a relationship after two years bears the burden of showing that there has been no exploitation, judged by seven factors: the time that has passed, the nature, duration and intensity of the therapy, the circumstances of termination, the client's personal history, the client's current mental state, the likelihood of harm, and any statements or actions made by the therapist during therapy suggesting or inviting a later romantic relationship. The same prohibition extends to students and supervisees over whom the psychologist has or may have evaluative authority (7.07).

Why is the rule so strict, when the client may be an adult who appears to consent? Because the consent is not free in the way it would be between equals. The client came for help in a state of need, revealed his most vulnerable self, and may be in the grip of a transference that makes the therapist seem uniquely understanding. The therapist holds the power, the secrets and the professional responsibility. The American psychologist Kenneth S. Pope, who has written extensively on the subject, compared the situation to incest, and in 1988 described a "therapist-patient sex syndrome" whose features include ambivalence towards the therapist, guilt, emptiness and isolation, an impaired ability to trust, confusion about roles and boundaries, suppressed rage and an increased risk of suicide. Surveys since the 1970s have found that the therapists who offend are mostly men and that some offend with more than one client. The harm, the power imbalance and the impossibility of genuinely free consent are why the client's apparent willingness, or even the client's initiative, is never a defence.

Exam trap

Sexual intimacy with a current client is always unethical, whoever initiates it. With a former client the APA rule is at least two years after termination, and even then only in the "most unusual circumstances", with the burden on the psychologist to show no exploitation; the two-year rule is a minimum, not a permission. Ending therapy in order to date the client's sister does not avoid the rule (10.06). Sexual relationships with students or supervisees under one's evaluative authority are also prohibited (7.07).

Money, advertising and social media

Money creates its own boundary questions. Under Standard 6.04, psychologists agree on fees and billing as early as feasible, do not misrepresent their fees, discuss in advance any limits on services that limited finances will impose, and, before using a collection agency or legal action for unpaid fees, tell the client and give him a chance to pay. They may not withhold records needed for a client's emergency treatment because he has not paid (6.03). barter, accepting goods or services instead of money, is allowed only if it is not clinically contraindicated and the arrangement is not exploitative (6.05); accepting a farmer's produce for a few sessions in a village may be reasonable, while accepting a client's labour on the therapist's house invites exactly the incompatible roles Kitchener warned about. Payments for referrals ("kickbacks") are forbidden: fees divided between professionals must reflect services actually provided, not the referral itself (6.07).

Public statements are governed by Section 5 of the code. Psychologists make no false or deceptive statements about their training, degrees, credentials, services, fees or results (5.01); in India, a person who advertises as a "clinical psychologist" without the RCI registration that the title requires (Section 1.5), or who promises a "100 percent cure for depression in six sessions", is violating both this principle and, in the first case, the law. Psychologists do not solicit testimonials from current clients or others vulnerable to undue influence (5.05), and they do not solicit business in person from potential clients who are vulnerable (5.06), though offering help at a disaster site is allowed.

Social media has multiplied these questions. A client sends a friend request; the safe course, written into the consent document, is a policy of not connecting with current or former clients on personal accounts. A therapist is tempted to search for a client online; doing so without a clear clinical reason, and ideally without the client's knowledge, is an intrusion on privacy, and whatever is found has to be either discussed or concealed, both awkward. A client posts an angry review; replying in a way that confirms he was a client, let alone discusses his treatment, breaches confidentiality. And posting even a "disguised" case on Instagram can identify a client to his own family. Standard 4.07 applies here exactly as it does to lectures: disguise thoroughly, or obtain written consent.

Records
