Why keep records at all? The APA code (Standard 6.01) gives five purposes: to make later services possible, by the same professional or by others; to allow research to be replicated; to meet institutional requirements; to ensure accurate billing; and to comply with the law. Two more purposes are implicit. A good record protects the client, because the next clinician will not have to start from nothing, and it protects the clinician, because a record written at the time is the best evidence that she acted with reasonable care. Clinicians put the second point bluntly: if it was not documented, as far as anyone else can tell, it was not done.

A clinical record typically contains identifying information and the referral; the consent obtained and the limits of confidentiality that were explained; the history, mental status examination and test results (Chapter 2); the diagnosis and formulation (Section 2.9); the treatment plan; dated progress notes for each contact; every risk assessment, the decision taken and the reasoning behind it; consultations with colleagues; contacts with family or other agencies and the consent for each; and a closing summary. Progress notes are often written in a fixed format such as the SOAP note: Subjective (what the client reports), Objective (what the clinician observes and measures), Assessment (the clinician's interpretation) and Plan (what happens next). What should stay out is just as important: gossip, speculation presented as fact, judgemental language ("manipulative", "attention-seeking"), and intimate details that are not needed for care, since Standard 4.04 asks that only information germane to the purpose be recorded and reported. In the United States, a therapist's private process notes, kept separately from the main record (often called psychotherapy notes), receive extra protection under the privacy rule of the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) of 1996. A useful discipline in India is to write every note as though the client will read it, because under Section 25 of the Mental Healthcare Act he has a right to access his basic medical records, subject to narrow exceptions (Section 2.9.9).

Records must be kept securely and confidentially "in creating, storing, accessing, transferring, and disposing of" them, whether on paper or electronically (6.02a): locked cabinets, password-protected and encrypted computers, no client files on a personal phone, and identifiers removed when information enters databases that others can access (6.02b). Psychologists also plan ahead for what happens to their records if they leave a job, retire or die (6.02c), so that clients are not stranded. How long should records be kept? The APA's Record Keeping Guidelines (2007) suggest that, in the absence of a stricter legal requirement, full records be kept for seven years after the last service for an adult, and for a minor until three years after he reaches the age of majority or seven years after the last service, whichever is later. India has no single rule for psychologists. The Medical Council of India's ethics regulations of 2002 required doctors to keep the records of in-patients for three years, and hospitals usually set their own, often longer, periods; a psychologist should follow her institution's policy and keep records longer when legal proceedings are possible. Digital records also come under the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023, whose rules (notified in November 2025) bring its main duties into force in phases, most of them from May 2027: personal data must be processed for a lawful purpose, with the person's consent or for certain other legitimate uses, and protected by reasonable security safeguards.

Exam trap

The APA code lists five purposes of records (6.01): later services, replication of research, institutional requirements, accurate billing, compliance with law. Records cannot be withheld for non-payment when they are needed for emergency treatment (6.03). Write only what is germane (4.04). In India the client has a right of access to his basic medical records under Section 25 of the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017, and the professional may withhold specific information only if disclosure would cause him serious mental harm or is likely to harm others.

The ethics of assessment

Psychological tests are powerful precisely because their results are trusted. A report that a child's IQ is 68 can change his school, his future and his family's view of him; a personality profile can decide a custody case or a job. Section 9 of the APA code therefore governs assessment closely. (The tests themselves are taught in the Assessment book; this subsection is about the ethics of using them.)

The first rule is about the basis of opinions. Psychologists base their opinions on information and techniques sufficient to support them, and give opinions about a person's psychological characteristics only after an examination adequate to support their conclusions (9.01). This forbids the television expert who diagnoses a politician or a film star from news clips. Psychiatry has a named version of the same rule, the "Goldwater rule", adopted by the American Psychiatric Association in 1973 after a magazine had published the opinions of psychiatrists who had never met the presidential candidate Barry Goldwater about his mental fitness. The second rule is about the instruments. Tests must be used in ways and for purposes supported by evidence; their reliability and validity must have been established for the population being tested, or their limitations must be stated; and they must suit the person's language preference and competence (9.02). Using a test standardised on American children to label a child from rural Bihar, without saying how far the norms apply, violates this rule. Psychologists do not base decisions on obsolete tests or outdated results (9.08): certifying a young adult's intellectual disability today on the basis of a test he took at eight, or using an old edition whose norms are out of date, is an ethical failure as well as a technical one.

The code then separates two kinds of material, and the difference is tested often. test data are the person's own raw and scaled scores, his responses to test questions or stimuli, and the psychologist's notes and recordings of what he said and did during the examination; a test form on which the client's answers are written counts as test data. test materials are the manuals, instruments, protocols and the test questions or stimuli themselves. Test data belong, in effect, to the client: with the client's release, psychologists provide test data to the client or to whoever he names, though they may refrain in order to protect the client or others from substantial harm or to prevent misuse, and without a release they provide test data only as required by law or court order (9.04). Test materials must be protected: psychologists make reasonable efforts to maintain the integrity and test security of test materials, consistent with law and contracts (9.11), because a test whose items are known is no longer a valid test. Table 1.23 sets the two side by side.

Table 1.23 Test data and test materials in the APA code.
FeatureTest data (Standard 9.04)Test materials (Standard 9.11)
What they areRaw and scaled scores; the client's responses; the examiner's notes on what he said and didManuals, instruments, protocols, test questions and stimuli
Main dutyRelease to the client or named persons on his release; withhold only to prevent substantial harm or misuseMaintain security and integrity, within law and contracts
Without the client's releaseOnly as required by law or court orderNot applicable; materials are not the client's

Why does security matter so much? In 2009 the ten Rorschach inkblots, together with the responses most commonly given to each, were posted on Wikipedia, and many psychologists protested that the test had been compromised for anyone who read the page before being assessed. The same logic applies in India to photocopied test forms and coaching videos that teach applicants how to "pass" personality tests: a copied test violates copyright, and a test whose stimuli circulate freely can no longer measure what it claims to.

The remaining standards protect the person tested. Consent for assessment is required, with the exceptions already described in Section 1.8.6 (9.03); when an interpreter is used, the client must agree to it and the limitations it places on the results must be reported. Psychologists do not encourage unqualified people to use psychological tests, except trainees under supervision (9.07); publishers commonly restrict the sale of tests according to the qualifications needed to use them. Psychologists who use computerised scoring and interpretation services remain responsible for how the results are applied (9.09). And they ensure that results are explained to the person or his representative, unless the nature of the relationship precludes it (as in some pre-employment, security or forensic evaluations) and the person was told this in advance (9.10). How to give feedback well is described in Section 2.9.10.

Assessment is also where third-party requests are most common: a school, an employer, a court or an insurer asks for an assessment, or for results. Standard 3.07 requires the psychologist to clarify at the outset her role, who the client is, how the information will probably be used, and the limits of confidentiality. A psychologist engaged by a company to screen job applicants has the company as her client and must tell applicants so; a psychologist engaged by parents to assess their daughter must not hand the report to the girl's school without the parents' consent and the girl's agreement. Section 1.9.11 works through exactly that request.

Exam trap

Test data (scores, responses, notes on behaviour) are released to the client or to persons he names on his release; test materials (manuals, items, stimuli) are kept secure. A completed answer sheet containing the client's responses is test data, even though it also shows the items. No opinion about a person's psychological characteristics without an adequate examination (9.01). Do not base decisions on obsolete tests or outdated results (9.08). The psychologist stays responsible for automated interpretations (9.09).

Research integrity for clinicians

Clinical psychologists in India do research as part of their training (most programmes require a dissertation) and throughout their careers, often with their own clients. Section 1.8.7 covered the protection of participants; the other half of research ethics is honesty about the research itself. Three forms of misconduct are treated as the gravest: fabrication, making up data or results; falsification, manipulating materials, procedures or data, or omitting inconvenient data, so that the record misrepresents what was found; and plagiarism, presenting another person's work or data as one's own. The APA code states the first and third bluntly ("Psychologists do not fabricate data", 8.10; and plagiarism is forbidden "even if the other work or data source is cited occasionally", 8.11), and requires psychologists who discover significant errors in their published data to correct them (8.10b). Fabrication is not a theoretical danger. In 2011 the Dutch social psychologist Diederik Stapel was found to have invented the data for dozens of studies over many years, and more than fifty of his papers were eventually retracted. In clinical research the consequences can be measured in lives: the 1998 Lancet paper by Andrew Wakefield that claimed a link between the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine and autism was retracted in 2010 after it was shown to be fraudulent, and Wakefield was struck off the British medical register, but the vaccine fears it started persisted for years.

Credit must also be honest. Authorship goes only to those who actually did or substantially contributed to the work, and the order of authors must reflect their relative contributions "regardless of their relative status"; being head of department does not by itself earn a place on a paper, and a student is normally the principal author of an article based substantially on his dissertation (8.12). Psychologists do not publish the same data twice as if they were new (8.13), they share their data with competent colleagues who want to verify their conclusions (8.14), and as reviewers they respect the confidentiality of the work they review (8.15). In India, clinical trials are registered with the Clinical Trials Registry-India (CTRI), set up under the ICMR in 2007; registration of regulated trials became mandatory in 2009, and ethics committees and journals now expect trials of psychological treatments to be registered too, so that unfavourable results cannot simply disappear. And when a clinician recruits her own clients into her research, she has created a multiple relationship: Standard 8.04 requires her to protect them from any adverse consequence of declining, so that no client ever feels that his treatment depends on his participation. (Research design, including the ethics of placebo and waiting-list controls, is covered in Section 8.7 and in the Research Methodology book.)

Exam trap

The three forms of research misconduct are fabrication (inventing), falsification (manipulating or selectively omitting) and plagiarism (taking credit for others' work). "Manipulating data to support hypotheses" is falsification and is never part of research ethics. Plagiarism is not excused by occasional citation. Authorship reflects contribution, not seniority. Duplicate publication of the same data as new is also misconduct.

Teletherapy and digital practice

Before 2020 video therapy was a specialty; within months of the COVID-19 lockdowns it was ordinary practice, and in India it now reaches clients through private platforms and through the national Tele-MANAS service (Section 1.10). The APA code already covered it, since it applies to work done "in person, postal, telephone, Internet, and other electronic transmissions". More specific guidance came in 2013, when a joint task force of the APA, the Association of State and Provincial Psychology Boards (the association of American and Canadian licensing boards) and the APA Insurance Trust published the Guidelines for the Practice of Telepsychology (the APA approved a revised version in 2024). The 2013 guidelines define telepsychology as the provision of psychological services using telecommunication technologies, and they address eight areas: the psychologist's competence with the technology and with remote work; maintaining the same standard of care as in person; informed consent; confidentiality; the security and transmission of data; the disposal of data and devices; testing and assessment at a distance; and interjurisdictional practice, working across state or national borders. In India, the Telemedicine Practice Guidelines issued in March 2020 apply to registered medical practitioners rather than to psychologists, but the principles they set out (identify the patient, obtain consent, keep records, know when remote care is not enough) are the same ones a psychologist follows.

Each area turns into a concrete question. Is this client suitable for remote work? A client in acute suicidal crisis, with severe psychosis, or with no private space may need to be seen in person. What does consent cover? Beyond the usual elements, the client must understand the particular risks of electronic communication (Standard 4.02c), what happens if the connection fails, whether sessions are recorded (they should not be, without explicit permission, Standard 4.03), and how emergencies will be handled. Is the platform secure? A service with end-to-end encryption and no unauthorised recording is acceptable; a session on a shared family laptop, with chat logs that anyone can open, is not. Is the client's space private? In many Indian homes it is not: clients take sessions in a parked car, on the terrace, or in a bathroom with the tap running, and it helps to agree in advance on a signal that someone has come into the room. What is the emergency plan? At the start of each session the psychologist should know where the client actually is, have a local emergency contact, and know the nearest hospital, because a video call cannot call an ambulance to an address no one has recorded. Which law applies? RCI registration is national, so moving between Indian states is not the problem it is in the United States; but a client who has moved to Dubai or Toronto may be subject to that country's rules on who may practise there. Can tests be given remotely? Many standardised tests have never been validated for video administration, and sending stimuli by screen-share puts test security at risk (Section 1.9.6).

Return to Shalini from Section 1.9.1. A year into therapy her husband is transferred to Bengaluru, and she asks to continue by video from her in-laws' flat. Continuing is reasonable: the alliance is strong, and finding a new therapist would set her back. But the move requires a fresh consent conversation about the platform and its risks, an agreed time when the flat is empty or a room she can lock, her new address and an emergency contact in Bengaluru, a plan for what happens if her mood drops sharply, and clarity about messaging. Clients who can message their therapist on WhatsApp at midnight will, and a clear policy (messages for scheduling only, replies within working hours, emergencies to a helpline or hospital) protects both sides from a boundary that dissolves without anyone deciding it should.

Clinical link

A first video session checklist: confirm the client's identity and exact location; record a local emergency contact and the nearest hospital; check that the client is alone and agree a signal for interruptions; use a secure platform and do not record without written permission; explain what happens if the connection drops; agree the rules for messages between sessions; and note in the record that all of this was discussed. Repeat the location check at every session.

Supervision and the trainee

Every clinical psychologist in India trains under supervision, and supervision has its own ethics. (What supervision is, its functions and its models are covered in Section 8.12; this subsection is about the duties it creates.) The first duty is honesty with the client. Under Standard 10.01(c), when the therapist is a trainee and legal responsibility lies with the supervisor, the client must be told that the therapist is in training and is being supervised, and must be given the supervisor's name. Clients must also know that what they say will be discussed in supervision, and recordings of sessions need their permission (4.03).

The supervisor's duties follow from the fact that she shares responsibility for the trainee's clients. She delegates only work the trainee can perform competently with the level of supervision provided, and checks that it is performed competently (2.05). She gives timely and specific feedback through a process explained at the start, and evaluates the trainee on actual performance against established requirements (7.06). She does not require trainees to disclose personal information about their sexual history, abuse, psychological treatment or relationships, unless the programme stated this requirement at admission or the information is needed because the trainee's personal problems are preventing competent work or posing a threat (7.04). Faculty who evaluate a student do not also act as his therapist (7.05), which is why a supervisor who notices that a trainee is struggling personally recommends therapy elsewhere instead of providing it. And sexual relationships with students or supervisees under her evaluative authority are forbidden (7.07). Legally, a supervisor and the institution can be held responsible for a trainee's negligent acts committed in the course of supervised work, under the doctrine of vicarious liability (in Latin, respondeat superior, "let the superior answer"). That is the legal reason supervisors read case notes and check risk assessments, not only the clinical one.

The trainee has duties too: not to exceed his competence, to bring his difficulties and mistakes to supervision instead of hiding them, and to tell the supervisor at once about any risk to a client or to others. A trainee who conceals a client's suicidal statement because he is embarrassed not to have asked about it puts the client, the supervisor and himself in danger.

Exam trap

A client seen by a trainee must be told that the therapist is a trainee, that he is supervised, and the supervisor's name (10.01c). Discussing a case in supervision is not a breach of confidentiality, provided the client was told. A supervisor must not become the trainee's therapist (a multiple relationship; 7.05). Supervisors can be vicariously liable for their trainees' negligence.

The RCI code and professional accountability in India

The APA code is the one textbooks quote, but the code that binds an Indian clinical psychologist is the Rehabilitation Council of India's. Section 21 of the Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1992 empowers the Council to prescribe standards of professional conduct and etiquette and a code of ethics for rehabilitation professionals, a category that includes clinical psychologists, and allows its regulations to specify which violations amount to professional misconduct. The Council did so in the Rehabilitation Council of India (Standards of Professional Conduct, Etiquette and Code of Ethics for Rehabilitation Professionals) Regulations, 1998. The Act also supplies the sanction: after giving the professional a reasonable opportunity to be heard, the Council may remove from the Central Rehabilitation Register the name of anyone who has been convicted of an offence, or found guilty of infamous conduct in a professional respect, or who has violated the prescribed standards or code of ethics, if it considers him unfit to remain on the Register; it may also bar him from re-registration permanently or for a period. Since only registered professionals may practise (Section 1.5), removal from the Register ends a clinical psychologist's right to practise.

Indian context

An Indian clinical psychologist answers to several authorities at once. The RCI sets the code of ethics and controls registration (RCI Act, 1992, Section 21; Regulations of 1998). The Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 defines clinical psychologists, counts them among mental health professionals and imposes legal duties on all professionals who treat persons with mental illness, such as confidentiality (Section 23) and the rules on research (Section 99); Section 1.10 describes the Act. POCSO (2012) imposes mandatory reporting. The courts hear negligence claims, and since the Supreme Court's judgement in Indian Medical Association v. V. P. Shantha (1995), paid health services have been treated as "services" under consumer protection law, so a dissatisfied client may also approach a consumer forum. Professional associations such as the Indian Association of Clinical Psychologists bring practitioners together, but they do not license anyone.

Reasoning through an ethical dilemma

Codes cannot list every situation, so every profession teaches a method for working through the ones they do not settle. Many such step models have been published, among them those of the American psychologists Gerald P. Koocher and Patricia Keith-Spiegel in their textbook Ethics in Psychology and the Mental Health Professions, and the ten-step model in the Canadian Psychological Association's code. They differ in detail but share a core, condensed in Figure 1.14. Kitchener's two levels (Section 1.8.3) explain why a method is needed at all: when intuition gives a clear and comfortable answer, most decisions are made quickly and well, but when intuition is uncertain or conflicted, the professional must slow down and reason explicitly.

Three quick self-checks are often added at step 5. Would I be comfortable if this decision were reported in the newspaper or described to my colleagues (publicity)? Would I recommend the same action to another psychologist in the same situation (universality)? Would I treat another client in the same circumstances the same way (justice)? Now push three cases through the method.

Case 1: the cousin. Your cousin Ramesh, thirty years old, phones. He has been having panic attacks for months, he trusts only you, he cannot afford private fees, and he does not want anyone in the family to know. Recognise: seeing him would create a multiple relationship (Standard 3.05). Facts: you will meet him at every family function, you know his wife and parents, your own mother will ask how he is, and if therapy goes badly or you have to confront him, family relationships will suffer; on the other side, he is suffering and reluctant to seek help. Sources: the code does not forbid the relationship outright but asks whether it could reasonably impair your objectivity or risk harm, and here it plainly could; principles of beneficence (he needs help) and nonmaleficence (the dual role may harm both the therapy and the family) pull against each other. Consult a colleague. Options: treat him yourself; refuse and leave him to find help alone; or decline the therapist role but act as a cousin, by finding him a competent and affordable colleague, a hospital outpatient department or a low-cost service, and keeping his confidence. The third option meets his need without the dual role. Decide and act: refer, warmly, and explain why the refusal protects him. If you lived in a remote district where no other help existed, a brief, clearly bounded intervention with consultation might be defensible (Standard 2.01d); in a city it is not.

Case 2: the school. You have assessed Zoya, the fifteen-year-old from Section 1.8.6, at her parents' request, and found a specific learning disorder with anxiety. The principal of her school phones: "We are deciding whether to promote her. Just tell me her IQ and the diagnosis." Recognise: a third-party request for confidential information (Standards 3.07, 4.05). Facts: your clients are Zoya and her parents, not the school; the school's purpose (a promotion decision) is not one Zoya's family agreed to; a bare IQ figure or diagnostic label, without context, could harm her. Sources: disclosure needs consent (4.05a); only germane information may be shared (4.04); test data go only to persons named in a release (9.04); and the Mental Healthcare Act's right to confidentiality applies. Consult if unsure. Options: refuse everything; give the principal what she asks for; or tell the principal politely that you cannot discuss any client without consent, then discuss the request with Zoya and her parents. Decide and act: with the parents' written consent and Zoya's assent, send a short report that explains her difficulties in plain language and recommends specific accommodations (extra time, a reader, remedial teaching), without raw scores or test items; document the request, the consent and what was sent. Notice how the third option serves Zoya's interests better than either extreme.

Case 3: the threat. Nikhil, a 32-year-old software engineer in Bengaluru being treated for depression and heavy drinking, says angrily near the end of a session: "If my team lead humiliates me again in Monday's meeting, I'll finish him. I know where he parks." Recognise: a possible duty to protect an identifiable person (Section 1.8.10). Facts: assess before you act, using the methods of Section 2.7. Has he ever been violent? Does he have a plan beyond these words, any weapon, any history of acting on threats? Is he intoxicated or likely to be on Monday? What does he say when asked, calmly, what he meant? Suppose he has a past assault conviction from a bar fight, has been drinking daily, and repeats the threat when asked. Sources: Section 23(2)(c) and (d) of the Mental Healthcare Act permit release of the information necessary to protect another person, and only that; the code permits disclosure to protect others from harm (4.05b); Tarasoff is not Indian law but shows the professional standard. Consult a senior colleague the same day. Options, from least to most intrusive: explore and de-escalate the anger in the session; increase the frequency of sessions; with his consent, involve his wife; arrange an urgent psychiatric review for his drinking and mood; ask him to take leave or avoid Monday's meeting; consider admission if the danger is high (the Act's admission procedures are described in Section 1.10); and, if he leaves still intending violence, warn the team lead or the company's security, or inform the police, telling them only what is needed to protect the team lead. Decide and act: in this version, with a past assault, ongoing drinking and a repeated specific threat, protective action is required; where possible, tell Nikhil what you are going to do and why, unless doing so would increase the danger. Document the threat in his words, your assessment, the consultation, the options considered and the reasons for your choice. Review at the next session, and with your supervisor.

Run the three cases side by side and a pattern appears. The worst choices were nearly always the extreme ones (treat your cousin, or abandon him; tell the school everything, or nothing; ignore the threat, or call the police at the first angry word). The good choice was usually a third option that honoured more than one principle at once, found by slowing down, consulting and asking what the person in front of you actually needs. That is what ethical competence looks like in practice.

Exam trap

In decision-making models the first step is to recognise that an ethical problem exists; consultation comes before the decision (except in a true emergency), and documentation of the reasoning comes with it. The least intrusive effective option is preferred. When a question asks what to do first about a client's threat, the answer is usually to assess the risk, not to call the police or to do nothing.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Judging an action by its consequences, as Bentham and Mill did, is ethics; judging it by its conformity to duty, as Kant did, is deontological ethics.
  2. Beauchamp and Childress's four principles are respect for autonomy, beneficence, justice and .
  3. The APA's five General Principles are , whereas its ten sections of Ethical Standards are enforceable.
  4. APA Principle C, which emphasises accuracy, honesty and truthfulness, is called .
  5. A child's own agreement to treatment, which supplements the consent of a parent, is called .
  6. Giving research participants full information about the study after it is over is called .
  7. The client's legal right to prevent a professional from testifying about his communications in court is called communication.
  8. The 1976 Tarasoff ruling broadened the 1974 duty to warn into a duty to .
  9. Gutheil and Gabbard distinguished a harmless boundary from a harmful boundary violation.
