Answers: (1) utilitarian (consequentialist), (2) nonmaleficence, (3) aspirational, (4) Integrity, (5) assent, (6) debriefing, (7) privileged, (8) protect, (9) crossing, (10) data.

1.10 Mental Health Law and Policy in IndiaHigh yield · 7 Qs

Picture a family in a district town in Karnataka. Their son Bharat, aged 28, has hardly slept for a week. He shouts at the neighbours, has stopped eating at home because he is sure his food is being poisoned, and last night he waved a kitchen knife at his father. In the morning the family bring him to the district hospital and ask the doctor to "keep him inside until he is cured". Who has the power to decide that Bharat must stay in hospital when he says he will not? For how long? Who checks that the decision was right, and which treatments may be given to him while he is there? Every society has to answer these questions, and the answers it chooses say a great deal about how it sees people with mental illness.

Indian law has answered them three times in a little over a century and a half. The colonial laws of 1858 and 1912 gave the decision to the magistrate: a person of "unsound mind" was a danger to be removed from society, and the law's business was to confine him safely. The Mental Health Act 1987 renamed the "lunatic" a "mentally ill person" and put doctors at the centre, but it kept much of the old machinery of orders and custody. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 starts from the other end. It presumes that Bharat, like anyone else, can make decisions about his own treatment; it gives him rights that the government must honour; and it lets him be admitted against his wishes only under strict conditions that an independent board can review. Beside these mental health laws stand the disability and rehabilitation laws, which turn mental illness, intellectual disability and autism into grounds for rights and entitlements and decide who may practise as a clinical psychologist, and a set of national programmes that try to bring care within reach of the people the laws protect. Figure 1.15 lays the whole story out in order. A clinical psychologist in India works inside this framework every day, and Indian entrance papers ask about it more often than about almost any other part of the profession.

The colonial laws: custody, not care

The first Indian laws on mental illness were passed in 1858, the year the British Crown took over the government of India from the East India Company. Three Acts appeared that year. Two of them, the Lunacy (Supreme Courts) Act and the Lunacy (District Courts) Act, dealt with how a court could declare a person to be of unsound mind and appoint someone to look after his property. The third, the Indian Lunatic Asylums Act 1858 (Act 36 of 1858), laid down how asylums were to be established and how people were to be admitted to them. The question these laws asked was who might be confined, on whose order, and what should happen to the confined person's estate. Treatment hardly came into it.

In 1912 the colonial government consolidated the law into the Indian Lunacy Act 1912 (Act IV of 1912), which governed Indian psychiatry for the next eighty years. Its vocabulary tells you its outlook. A "lunatic" was defined as "an idiot or a person of unsound mind", so intellectual disability and mental illness were lumped together under one word; the place of care was an "asylum"; and the people in it were lunatics of different legal kinds. The central instrument of the Act was the reception order, defined in the Act as an order "for the reception into an asylum of a lunatic". To obtain one on petition, the husband or wife of the alleged lunatic (or, failing them, the nearest relative) applied to a magistrate, and the petition had to be supported by two medical certificates, one of them from a government medical officer. The magistrate considered the petition, usually examined the person himself, and could order the person's detention while he made up his mind. The police had a duty to take charge of anyone found "wandering at large" whom they believed to be a lunatic, or anyone dangerous by reason of lunacy, and to bring him before a magistrate; a lunatic who was cruelly treated or neglected by the relatives responsible for him could be dealt with in the same way. A criminal lunatic, a person ordered by a criminal court to be detained because of his mental state, came in on that court's order. Finally, the High Courts and District Courts could hold a judicial inquisition into whether a person was of unsound mind and incapable of managing himself and his affairs, and then appoint a guardian of his person and a manager of his estate.

There was a door for people who wanted treatment, but it was narrow. A person "desirous of submitting himself to treatment" could be received as a voluntary boarder, but only on his written application and with the consent of two of the asylum's visitors (the official inspectors appointed for each asylum), and he was free to leave again on giving notice in writing. Everything else ran through magistrates, the police and the courts. Laws of this kind are described as following a custodial model: their aim is to protect society from the mentally ill person and to keep him safely confined, not to restore him to health or protect his rights. That is the model the next two Acts set out to replace.

Exam trap

The 1912 Act is the Indian Lunacy Act (Act IV of 1912); the 1858 law is the Indian Lunatic Asylums Act. Its key words are lunatic ("an idiot or a person of unsound mind"), asylum, reception order and magistrate. Do not assume that voluntary admission was invented in 1987: the 1912 Act already allowed voluntary boarders, though only with the consent of two visitors. The 1912 Act was repealed by the Mental Health Act 1987, not by the 2017 Act.

The Mental Health Act 1987: from lunatics to patients

Soon after Independence, the Indian Psychiatric Society, formed in 1947, decided that the 1912 Act had to go, and in 1950 it sent the Government of India a draft Mental Health Bill. Nothing happened for a long time. The government began the formal process only in 1978, when a Mental Health Bill was introduced in the Lok Sabha, and after years of revision Parliament passed the Mental Health Act 1987 (Act 14 of 1987), which received the President's assent on 22 May 1987. Even then the wheels turned slowly: the Mental Health Rules were framed in 1990, and the Act came into force in all the states and union territories only on 1 April 1993. Its long title called it "An Act to consolidate and amend the law relating to the treatment and care of mentally ill persons", and the word "treatment" in that title marks the change of direction. It repealed the Indian Lunacy Act 1912.

What did the 1987 Act change? First, the language. The "lunatic" became a "mentally ill person", defined as a person who needs treatment because of any mental disorder other than mental retardation, and the "asylum" became a psychiatric hospital or psychiatric nursing home. Second, it created regulators: a Central Authority for Mental Health Services and a State Authority for Mental Health Services in each state, with official visitors to inspect hospitals. Third, it required psychiatric hospitals and psychiatric nursing homes, private as well as public, to be licensed, so that standards could in principle be enforced. Fourth, it made admission at the person's own request the first route into hospital. An adult could ask to be admitted as a voluntary patient (a minor through his guardian); a relative or friend could arrange admission "under special circumstances", without going to court, for a person who needed care but could not express a willingness to be admitted; and the magistrate's reception order was kept for the remaining cases. The Act also stated that a mentally ill person under treatment must not be subjected to indignity or cruelty, and it provided legal aid at state expense in some proceedings.

Yet the 1987 Act was still, at heart, a law about hospitals and procedures. Commentators at the time described it as concerned mainly with licensing mental hospitals, regulating admissions and settling guardianship of the person and property, with human rights and the delivery of care treated as afterthoughts. Its procedures were complicated, and some of them, such as the requirement that certificates for admission come from a gazetted government medical officer, were hard to meet in private and general hospitals. Because it excluded mental retardation from its definition, people with severe intellectual disability had no clear place in it. It gave nobody a right to receive treatment, said nothing about community care, rehabilitation or the families who did most of the caring, and was written almost entirely around psychiatrists, medical officers and magistrates, so that clinical psychologists and other mental health professionals had no defined role. Most of all, it assumed that a person with mental illness could not decide for himself.

The push to replace it came from outside psychiatry. On 13 December 2006 the United Nations General Assembly adopted the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCRPD), which came into force on 3 May 2008. The Convention treats disability, including the psychosocial disability that can accompany mental illness, as the product of an interaction between a person's impairment and the barriers society puts in his way, and its Article 12 requires states to recognise that persons with disabilities enjoy legal capacity on an equal basis with others in all aspects of life. India ratified the Convention on 1 October 2007, and a state that ratifies it promises to bring its laws into line. A law built on magistrates' orders and on the assumption of incapacity plainly did not fit. Two new laws followed: the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 for disability (taught later in this section) and the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 for mental health care.

Exam trap

Watch the spelling and the dates. The Mental Health Act (two words) was passed in 1987 but came into force only on 1 April 1993; the Mental Healthcare Act (one word, "Healthcare") is the 2017 law, in force from 2018. There is no "Mental Healthcare Act 1987". The RCI Act also came into force in 1993, so "1993" cannot by itself tell you which Act is meant. The 1987 Act had no advance directives, no nominated representatives and no Mental Health Review Boards; its regulators were called the Central and State Authority for Mental Health Services.

The Mental Healthcare Act 2017: a rights-based law and its key definitions

The new law took nearly four years to pass. The Mental Health Care Bill was introduced in the Rajya Sabha on 19 August 2013 by the Union Health Minister of the time, Ghulam Nabi Azad. The Lok Sabha passed it on 27 March 2017, it received the President's assent on 7 April 2017 as the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (Act 10 of 2017), and it came into force on 29 May 2018. Section 126 repealed the Mental Health Act 1987. The Act's long title states its purpose in a single sentence that is worth knowing by heart: it is "An Act to provide for mental healthcare and services for persons with mental illness and to protect, promote and fulfil the rights of such persons during delivery of mental healthcare and services". Its preamble then recites the UNCRPD and India's ratification of it, and says that "it is necessary to align and harmonise the existing laws with the said Convention".

That is why the Act is called a rights-based approach to mental health law. The 1912 and 1987 Acts asked what society may do to a person with mental illness. The 2017 Act asks what that person is entitled to, and treats every restriction on him as an exception that has to be justified, recorded and open to review. The Act runs to 126 sections in sixteen chapters. You do not need all of them, but you do need to know the shape: definitions and capacity (Chapters I and II, sections 1 to 4), advance directives (Chapter III, sections 5 to 13), nominated representatives (Chapter IV, sections 14 to 17), rights (Chapter V, sections 18 to 28), duties of government (Chapter VI, sections 29 to 32), the Central and State Mental Health Authorities (Chapters VII and VIII), the registration of mental health establishments (Chapter X), the Mental Health Review Boards (Chapter XI, sections 73 to 84), admission, treatment and discharge (Chapter XII, sections 85 to 99), the duties of the police, magistrates and prisons (Chapter XIII), offences and penalties (Chapter XV), and a miscellaneous chapter that contains section 115 on attempted suicide.

The definitions in section 2. Several definitions are examined directly, and one of them defines your own profession.

  • Mental illness (section 2(s)) is "a substantial disorder of thinking, mood, perception, orientation or memory that grossly impairs judgment, behaviour, capacity to recognise reality or ability to meet the ordinary demands of life, mental conditions associated with the abuse of alcohol and drugs, but does not include mental retardation". Two things follow. Alcohol and drug conditions are inside the Act, so a man with alcohol dependence has all its rights. Intellectual disability is outside it, and is dealt with by the disability laws later in this section.
  • A clinical psychologist (section 2(g)) is a person with a recognised qualification in clinical psychology from an institution approved and recognised by the Rehabilitation Council of India, or a person with a postgraduate degree in psychology, clinical psychology or applied psychology together with a Master of Philosophy in Clinical Psychology or in Medical and Social Psychology, obtained after a full-time course of two years that includes supervised clinical training, from a university recognised by the University Grants Commission and approved and recognised by the RCI. The definition is tied to the RCI from both directions, which is why the new two-year RCI professional master's degrees described in Section 1.5 fit into it.
  • A mental health professional (section 2(r)) is a psychiatrist; or a professional registered with the State Mental Health Authority under section 55, which is how clinical psychologists, mental health nurses and psychiatric social workers enter the definition; or a professional with a postgraduate degree in the mental health branch of Ayurveda, Homoeopathy, Unani or Siddha. The Act also defines the psychiatric social worker (a postgraduate degree in social work plus a two-year M.Phil in Psychiatric Social Work), the mental health nurse and the psychiatrist.
  • Informed consent (section 2(i)) is consent for a specific intervention, given without force, undue influence, fraud, threat, mistake or misrepresentation, after the person has been told the risks, benefits and alternatives "in a language and manner understood by the person". The ethics of consent are taught in Section 1.8; here the point is that the Act turns them into law.
  • The least restrictive alternative (section 2(j)) is the option for treatment, or the setting for it, that meets the person's needs while imposing the least restriction on his rights. Every admission against a person's wishes must be certified as the least restrictive option available.
  • A mental health establishment (section 2(p)) is any health establishment, government or private, including Ayurveda, Yoga and Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha and Homoeopathy establishments and general hospitals, where persons with mental illness are admitted and reside for care, treatment, convalescence or rehabilitation; a family home where a person lives with relatives or friends is excluded. A minor (section 2(t)) is anyone who has not completed 18 years.

Determining mental illness (section 3). Chapter 2 of this book relies on this section, so state it precisely. Mental illness is to be determined in accordance with nationally or internationally accepted medical standards, including the latest edition of the WHO's International Classification of Diseases, as notified by the Central Government. Nobody may classify a person as mentally ill except for purposes directly related to treatment or as allowed by law. Mental illness must not be determined on the basis of political, economic or social status, membership of a cultural, racial or religious group, or non-conformity with the moral, social, cultural, work or political values or religious beliefs of the person's community. Past treatment or hospitalisation, though relevant, does not by itself justify a present or future finding of mental illness. And a finding of mental illness does not mean that the person is "of unsound mind" unless a competent court has declared him so. Notice how directly this section answers the critics of psychiatric labelling whom Section 1.6 introduced.

Capacity (section 4). Now comes the idea on which the whole Act stands. Every person, including a person with mental illness, "shall be deemed to have capacity" to make decisions about his mental health care or treatment if he is able to understand the information relevant to the decision, to appreciate any reasonably foreseeable consequence of making the decision or of not making it, or to communicate the decision by speech, expression, gesture or any other means. The information must be given in simple language that the person understands, or in sign language, visual aids or any other means that help him understand. Then comes the sentence that does the most work: a decision "perceived by others as inappropriate or wrong" does not by itself mean that the person lacks capacity. This is the presumption of capacity. It is not a test of whether the person agrees with his doctors. It is a test of whether he can understand, weigh and communicate (the section joins the three abilities with the word "or", though clinicians usually check all three). Section 14(9) puts the same idea in its strongest form: all persons with mental illness "shall have capacity" to make treatment decisions, but may need varying levels of support from their nominated representative to make them. This is the Act's version of supported decision-making: help the person decide, rather than decide for him.

Take an example. Kamala, a 41-year-old schoolteacher in Mysuru, has bipolar disorder and is now well. Her psychiatrist recommends that she continue lithium; she refuses, because the weight she gained on it was humiliating at school, and she prefers another mood stabiliser that her doctor considers a little less effective. Does Kamala have capacity? She can explain what lithium does and what the alternative is, she knows that stopping it raises her risk of relapse and has weighed that risk, and she can state her decision clearly. She has capacity, and her choice stands, even though the treating team would have chosen differently. If, months later, she becomes manic, cannot follow an explanation of her treatment and cannot hold information long enough to weigh it, she may lose capacity for that decision for as long as the episode lasts. Capacity in the Act is specific to a decision and to a time; it is never a permanent label on a person.

Clinical link

Capacity is assessed by clinicians, and clinical psychologists are often asked to contribute: a structured interview about what the person understands of his illness, the proposed treatment and the alternatives, together with tests of attention, memory and reasoning where cognition is in doubt. Because the Act defines a registered clinical psychologist as a mental health professional, he can be one of the two professionals who examine a person for a supported admission or for the admission of a minor, and the Act even allows the mental health professional in charge of an establishment, not only the medical officer, to decide on independent admissions. Your reports may therefore end up before a Review Board, so write them as legal documents: what you asked, what the person said, and why you concluded what you did.

Exam trap

Under the MHCA, a clinical psychologist is defined in section 2(g) by an RCI-recognised qualification (or a postgraduate degree in psychology plus a two-year M.Phil in Clinical Psychology or Medical and Social Psychology with supervised clinical training). A clinical psychologist registered with the State Authority is a mental health professional; so an option saying "only psychiatrists can provide therapy; psychologists are excluded" is false. The Act's "mental illness" includes conditions associated with alcohol and drug abuse but excludes mental retardation. Capacity is presumed, and a decision that others think "wrong" does not prove its absence.

Advance directives and nominated representatives

If capacity can come and go, a person who knows his illness may want to decide in advance what should happen when he can no longer decide. Chapter III of the Act gives him that power. Under section 5, every person who is not a minor has a right to make an advance directive in writing, specifying any or all of three things: the way he wishes to be cared for and treated for a mental illness, the way he wishes not to be cared for and treated, and the individual or individuals, in order of precedence, whom he wants to appoint as his nominated representative. He may make it irrespective of his past mental illness or treatment. The directive is invoked only when he ceases to have capacity to make mental health care or treatment decisions, and remains effective only until he regains capacity. Any decision he makes while he has capacity overrides anything he wrote earlier, and a directive that is contrary to any law in force is void from the start (so nobody can direct, for example, that he be given a treatment the Act prohibits).

The rest of the chapter makes the directive work. The manner of making it is laid down in regulations of the Central Mental Health Authority (section 6). Every Mental Health Review Board keeps an online register of the advance directives registered with it and makes them available to the professionals concerned (section 7). The person may revoke, amend or cancel his directive at any time (section 8). It does not apply to emergency treatment (section 9). The medical officer in charge and the psychiatrist in charge have a duty to treat the person in accordance with his valid directive (section 10). If a mental health professional, a relative or a care-giver does not want to follow it, he cannot simply ignore it: he must apply to the Board, which hears everyone, including the person who made the directive, and then upholds, modifies or cancels it after asking whether it was made freely, whether the person intended it to apply to the present circumstances, whether he was well informed, whether he had capacity when he made it, and whether it is contrary to law (section 11). The same section gives the legal guardian of a minor the right to make an advance directive for the minor. The Central Authority reviews how directives are being used (section 12), and a professional is not liable for unforeseen consequences of following a valid directive, nor for failing to follow one of which he was not given a copy (section 13).

Return to Kamala. While she is well she writes a directive: if she becomes manic she wishes to be treated at a named hospital in Mysuru, with the mood stabiliser she prefers; she does not wish to be given a particular antipsychotic that once made her dangerously stiff; and she names her sister as her nominated representative, with her husband second. She registers it with the Board. Two years later she is admitted in a manic episode, and the team find that she cannot follow an explanation of her treatment. The directive is now invoked. Her husband, alarmed, wants her moved to a different hospital and given the drug she refused. He cannot overrule her written wishes by himself; his remedy is an application to the Board under section 11. And the day Kamala recovers capacity, her own current decisions replace the directive again.

The second new figure in the Act is the nominated representative. Under section 14, every person who is not a minor has a right to appoint one, in writing on plain paper, signed or thumb-printed. The nominated representative must not be a minor, must be competent to do the job, and must consent in writing. If the person has not appointed anyone, the Act supplies one in a fixed order of precedence: the individual named in his advance directive; otherwise a relative; otherwise a care-giver; otherwise a suitable person appointed by the Board; and if nobody is available, the Director of the Department of Social Welfare (or his representative), appointed by the Board. While the Board is deciding, a representative of a registered organisation working for persons with mental illness may act temporarily. The person can revoke or change the appointment at any time. Two sub-sections guard against the old assumption of incapacity: appointing a nominated representative, or being unable to appoint one, does not mean that the person lacks capacity (section 14(8)); and every person with mental illness has capacity, though he may need support from his representative to use it (section 14(9)).

For minors the rule is different. Section 15 makes the legal guardian the nominated representative of a minor, unless the Board, on an application by a mental health professional or anyone acting in the minor's best interests, finds that the guardian is not acting in those interests or is otherwise unfit, and appoints someone else (or, failing anyone, the state's Director of Social Welfare). A minor therefore cannot appoint his own nominated representative, just as he cannot make his own advance directive.

What does the nominated representative actually do? Section 17 lists the duties, and they describe a supporter, not a master. He must consider the person's current and past wishes, life history, values, cultural background and best interests; give particular credence to the person's own views to the extent that the person understands the decision; support the person in making treatment decisions during a supported admission; seek information on diagnosis and treatment; have access to family-based or home-based rehabilitation services on the person's behalf; be involved in discharge planning; apply for admission and, to the Board, for discharge; complain to the Board about violations of the person's rights; appoint an attendant for an admitted minor; and give or withhold consent for research in the limited cases the Act allows. Only in one situation does he decide for the person: when a person in supported admission needs "nearly hundred per cent" support to make a treatment decision, the representative may temporarily consent to the treatment plan on his behalf, and the person's capacity must then be reviewed at regular intervals (you will meet this rule again under admissions).

Exam trap

Advance directive (section 5) and nominated representative (section 14) both come from the Mental Healthcare Act 2017. They are not in the Mental Health Act 1987, which had neither; not in the RPwD Act 2016, whose decision-making tool is limited guardianship (its section 14); and not in the RCI Act 1992, which is about training and registering professionals. Advance directives are registered with the Mental Health Review Board, are invoked only when capacity is lost, and do not apply to emergency treatment. Section numbers to keep apart: section 5 is the advance directive, section 14 the nominated representative, section 15 the nominated representative of a minor.

Exam trap

A 2024 paper asked which statement on the treatment of minors under the MHCA is accurate, and keyed: "A minor has the right to appoint a nominated representative, and their opinion on treatment must be considered in an age-appropriate manner." Only the second half is right. Section 14 gives the right to appoint a nominated representative to "every person who is not a minor", and section 15 makes the legal guardian the minor's nominated representative. What the Act does require is that the minor's wishes be taken into account: admission must be in his best interests "taking into account the wishes of the minor if ascertainable" (section 87). Pick the keyed option as the best of those offered, because the others are wrong: admission does not need the Board's prior consent (the Board is told within 72 hours and reviews admissions lasting beyond 30 days); the option giving the nominated representative "all decisions" overstates his power, because although an admitted minor is treated with the representative's informed consent (section 87), the representative must give particular credence to the minor's views (section 17), ECT for a minor also needs the Board's prior permission (section 95), and the Board can replace an unfit guardian (section 15); and a minor certainly can be admitted against his own wishes on the guardian's application if the conditions of section 87 are met. In a written answer or an interview, say the law correctly.

The rights of persons with mental illness and the duties of government

Chapter V is the heart of the Act: eleven sections, ten of whose headings begin with the word "right" (the eleventh, section 24, restricts the release of information to the media). The first is the most far-reaching. Section 18 gives every person a right to access mental healthcare and treatment from mental health services run or funded by the government, and spells out what "access" means: services of affordable cost and good quality, in sufficient quantity, geographically accessible, provided without discrimination on the basis of gender, sex, sexual orientation, religion, culture, caste, social or political beliefs, class or disability, and acceptable to persons with mental illness and their families. The services must range from outpatient and inpatient care to halfway homes, sheltered and supported accommodation, support for families, home-based and community rehabilitation, and child and old-age mental health services. Government must integrate mental health into general health care at every level; use long-term institutional care only as a last resort; make at least minimum services available in every district (and pay for treatment elsewhere where they are not); provide basic and emergency care at all community health centres and above; treat people below the poverty line, the destitute and the homeless free of charge; and supply the medicines on an Essential Drug List free at government facilities. A right with duties attached is no longer a hope; it is a claim that can be taken to the Board or the courts.

The other rights follow quickly. The right to community living (section 19) is the right to live in, be part of and not be segregated from society, and it forbids keeping anyone in a mental health establishment merely because he has no family, is not accepted by his family, is homeless or lacks community facilities; the government must create less restrictive alternatives such as halfway homes and group homes. Chapter 9 shows what this means for rehabilitation services. The right to protection from cruel, inhuman and degrading treatment (section 20) begins with a right to live with dignity and lists what dignity means inside an institution: a safe and hygienic environment, privacy, proper clothing and the right to wear one's own clothes rather than a uniform, leisure, education and religious practice, wholesome food and sanitation including items needed during menstruation, freedom from forced work (and pay for work done), freedom from compulsory tonsuring (shaving of the head), and protection from physical, verbal, emotional and sexual abuse. The right to equality and non-discrimination (section 21) requires that persons with mental illness be treated as equal to persons with physical illness in emergency services, ambulances and living conditions; that a child under three be ordinarily kept with a mother receiving inpatient care; and, in a provision that changed the insurance industry, that every insurer provide medical insurance for mental illness "on the same basis" as for physical illness, which is called insurance parity.

The remaining rights concern information and contact. A person and his nominated representative have a right to information about the provision under which he is admitted, his right to apply to the Board, and the nature of his illness and its proposed treatment with its known side effects, in a language he understands (section 22). He has a right to confidentiality, subject to listed exceptions such as release to his nominated representative or treating professionals, to protect another person from harm or violence, to prevent a threat to life, or on the order of a Board or court (section 23); no photograph or information about him may be released to the media without his consent (section 24); and he may see his basic medical records, unless disclosure would cause serious mental harm to him or make harm to others likely (section 25). Chapter 2 teaches sections 22, 23 and 25 in the setting of assessment and record keeping, and Section 1.8 sets them beside the ethical rules on confidentiality. The list ends with rights to personal contacts and communication (section 26), to free legal services (section 27), and to complain about deficiencies in care, first to the officer in charge, then to the Board and then to the State Authority (section 28).

Rights mean little unless someone must deliver them, so Chapter VI places duties on the "appropriate Government" (the Central Government for its own establishments, the State Government for the rest): to run programmes that promote mental health, prevent mental illness and in particular reduce suicides and attempted suicides (section 29); to publicise the Act and fund anti-stigma programmes (section 30); to train all medical officers in public health facilities and prisons to give basic and emergency mental health care, and to try to meet international norms for the number of mental health professionals within ten years (section 31); and to coordinate the ministries concerned (section 32).

Exam trap

Match the right to its section: 18 access to mental healthcare (the flagship right), 19 community living, 20 protection from cruel, inhuman and degrading treatment, 21 equality and non-discrimination (including insurance parity in section 21(4)), 22 information, 23 confidentiality, 25 access to medical records, 27 legal aid, 28 complaints. "Right to information" (what my illness is and how it will be treated) is not the same as "right to access medical records" (seeing the file). Free treatment is guaranteed to people below the poverty line "whether or not in possession of a below poverty line card", and to the destitute and homeless.

Authorities, Review Boards and the registration of professionals and establishments

A rights-based law needs institutions to enforce it, and the Act creates three. The Central Mental Health Authority (Chapter VII) is chaired by the Secretary or Additional Secretary of the Department of Health and Family Welfare. Besides officials of several ministries and the directors of the central mental health institutions, its members include one clinical psychologist, one psychiatric social worker and one mental health nurse, each with at least fifteen years' experience, and representatives of persons who have had mental illness, of care-givers and of non-governmental organisations. Under section 43 it registers mental health establishments under the Central Government and keeps a national register of all establishments, sets quality norms, supervises central establishments and receives complaints about them, keeps a national register of clinical psychologists, mental health nurses and psychiatric social workers based on state registrations, trains officials and professionals in the Act, and advises the Central Government.

Each state has a State Mental Health Authority (Chapter VIII), similarly composed (it too has a clinical psychologist with fifteen years' experience among its members), which must meet at least four times a year. Under section 55 it registers all mental health establishments in the state other than central ones, sets norms, supervises them and receives complaints, and, most important for you, registers clinical psychologists, mental health nurses and psychiatric social workers in the state to work as mental health professionals. This is the registration that makes a clinical psychologist a "mental health professional" within the Act. It is separate from registration with the RCI, which the RCI Act requires before anyone may practise as a clinical psychologist at all (taught later in this section).

Section 65 then requires that no person or organisation establish or run a mental health establishment unless it is registered with the relevant Authority. The Central Government may exempt notified categories of existing establishments, but there is no blanket exemption for government hospitals. Running an unregistered establishment attracts a penalty that starts at 5,000 to 50,000 rupees and rises to five lakh rupees for repeated contraventions, and a professional who knowingly works in one can be fined up to 25,000 rupees (section 107).

The third institution is the one you will meet in practice. The Mental Health Review Board (Chapter XI) is constituted by the State Authority for a district or a group of districts. Under section 74 each Board consists of a District Judge (or an officer qualified to be one, or a retired District Judge) as chairperson; a representative of the District Collector or District Magistrate; a psychiatrist and a medical practitioner; and two members who are persons with mental illness, care-givers, or representatives of their organisations or of non-governmental organisations working in mental health. Applications to it are free and may be made by the person, his nominated representative or a registered non-governmental organisation with his consent, in exceptional cases even orally or by telephone. Its proceedings are judicial proceedings, held in camera at the establishment where the person is admitted. The Act sets deadlines: challenges to a minor's admission or to a supported admission of up to 30 days, and applications to appoint a nominated representative, must be decided within seven days; challenges to supported admission beyond 30 days within twenty-one days; everything else within ninety days (section 80).

The Board's powers (section 82) gather together everything you have read so far: to register, review, alter, modify or cancel advance directives; to appoint nominated representatives; to hear challenges against the decisions of establishments under sections 87, 89 and 90 (the admission of minors and supported admissions); to decide on information withheld from medical records under section 25; to adjudicate complaints about deficiencies in care under section 28; and to visit and inspect prisons and question their medical officers. Where an establishment ignores its orders, the Board or Authority may fine it up to five lakh rupees and recommend cancellation of its registration. Appeals against orders of a Board or Authority go to the High Court (section 83). Table 1.24 gathers the three bodies together.

Table 1.24 The three bodies that run the Mental Healthcare Act 2017.
BodySet up byHeaded byMain jobs
Central Mental Health AuthorityCentral GovernmentSecretary or Additional Secretary, Department of Health and Family WelfareRegisters central establishments; national registers of establishments and of professionals; norms; advises; regulations on advance directives
State Mental Health AuthorityState GovernmentSecretary or Principal Secretary, state Department of HealthRegisters establishments in the state; registers clinical psychologists, mental health nurses and psychiatric social workers (section 55); constitutes Boards
Mental Health Review BoardState Authority, for a district or group of districtsDistrict Judge (serving, qualified or retired)Advance directives; nominated representatives; reviews of minors' and supported admissions; complaints; prison visits
Exam trap

The Review Board is chaired by a District Judge, not a psychiatrist, and it is constituted by the State Mental Health Authority. Its six members are the judge, the Collector's representative, a psychiatrist, a medical practitioner and two members representing service users or care-givers; a clinical psychologist sits on the Central and State Authorities but is not one of the Board's prescribed members. A question option saying that "mental health establishments are exempt from registration if government-run" is false: section 65 requires every establishment to be registered with the Central or State Authority, and only the Central Government can exempt a notified category of existing establishments.

Admission, treatment and discharge

Chapter XII answers the questions with which this section began. Its first rule sets the tone: all admissions shall, as far as possible, be independent admissions, except when conditions make a supported admission unavoidable (section 85). The Act knows four routes into hospital and one route to treatment outside it, and Figure 1.16 shows how they fit together.

Independent admission (sections 85, 86 and 88). An independent admission is the admission of a person who has the capacity to make mental health care and treatment decisions or who needs only minimal support to make them. An adult who believes he has a mental illness and wants to be admitted asks the medical officer or mental health professional in charge, who admits him if the illness is severe enough to need admission, he is likely to benefit, and he has understood the nature and purpose of admission and asked of his own free will. A person who needs support "approaching hundred per cent" cannot be admitted this way. The independent patient may not be treated without his informed consent, needs nobody else's consent or presence to be admitted, and may discharge himself. There is one brake. If a mental health professional believes that the person cannot understand his decisions and needs very high support, or has recently threatened or attempted self-harm, behaved violently or caused others to fear harm, or shown an inability to care for himself that puts him at risk, he may prevent discharge for up to 24 hours so that the person can be assessed for a supported admission (section 88).

Admission of a minor (section 87). A minor can be admitted only by this procedure. His nominated representative (the legal guardian) applies; two psychiatrists, or one psychiatrist and one mental health professional, or one psychiatrist and one medical practitioner, must examine him independently on the day of admission or in the preceding seven days and both conclude that he has a mental illness severe enough to need admission, that admission is in his best interests "taking into account the wishes of the minor if ascertainable", that his needs cannot be met without admission, and that community alternatives have failed or are unsuitable. He must be housed separately from adults, in surroundings suited to his age. The nominated representative, or an attendant he appoints, must stay with him throughout, and a female attendant must stay with a girl whose representative is male. Treatment is given with the representative's informed consent, and the minor must be discharged if the representative withdraws support. The Board must be told of every admission of a minor within 72 hours, may visit and interview him, must be told if the admission reaches 30 days, and must then review it within seven days and every 30 days after that.

Supported admission up to 30 days (section 89). A supported admission is the Act's name for admission without the person's own consent, and its conditions are tight. The nominated representative applies. One psychiatrist and one other mental health professional or medical practitioner must independently examine the person on the day of admission or in the preceding seven days, and both must conclude that his mental illness is so severe that he has recently threatened or attempted (or is threatening or attempting) to harm himself, or has recently behaved violently towards another person or caused another person to fear bodily harm from him, or has recently shown an inability to care for himself that places him at risk of harm. They must also certify, after taking account of any advance directive, that admission is the least restrictive option possible, and the person must be unable to make treatment decisions independently, needing very high support from his representative. Treatment then takes account of his advance directive, or of his informed consent given with his representative's support; only when he needs "nearly hundred per cent" support may the representative temporarily consent on his behalf, and his capacity must then be reviewed every seven days. The establishment must report the admission to the Board within three days for a woman or a minor and within seven days for anyone else; the person, his representative or an NGO acting with his consent may ask the Board to review it, and the Board must give a binding decision within seven days. The admission ends after 30 days, or earlier if the criteria cease to apply, when the person may stay on as an independent patient; a person discharged from a supported admission cannot be readmitted under the same section within seven days.

Supported admission beyond 30 days (section 90). If longer inpatient care is needed, two psychiatrists must examine the person and find that the danger to self or others, or the inability to care for himself, has been present "consistently over time"; that admission remains the least restrictive option; and that he still needs very high support. The admission must be reported to the Board within seven days, and the Board must permit it or order discharge within twenty-one days, after asking whether institutional care is really needed and whether care in a less restrictive community setting is possible. The Board may demand a plan for community-based treatment, and nobody may be kept in hospital merely because community services do not exist where he lives. Such admissions run for up to 90 days at first, then up to 120 days, and then up to 180 days at a time, each extension requiring the same procedure. Where the representative is consenting on the person's behalf, capacity is reviewed every fortnight.

Emergency treatment (section 94). Outside these admissions, any registered medical practitioner may give emergency treatment, at a health establishment or in the community, with the informed consent of the nominated representative where one is available, when it is immediately necessary to prevent death or irreversible harm to the person's health, serious harm by the person to himself or others, or serious damage to property flowing directly from his mental illness. Emergency treatment includes taking the person to the nearest mental health establishment for assessment. It is limited to 72 hours or until the person has been assessed at an establishment, whichever is earlier (up to seven days during a declared disaster), it may not include treatment unrelated to the emergency, and it may never include electroconvulsive therapy.

Now take Bharat through the Act. He refuses admission, so the independent route is closed unless he changes his mind. His father, as a relative, is his nominated representative by default (Bharat has appointed nobody and has no advance directive) and applies for a supported admission. A psychiatrist and a clinical psychologist registered with the State Authority examine Bharat separately that day. Both find a severe mental illness; both note that he threatened his father with a knife, which meets the criterion of having "recently behaved violently towards another person"; both find that he cannot follow an explanation of the proposed treatment and needs very high support to decide; and both certify that admission is the least restrictive option, since home treatment has failed. Bharat is admitted under section 89. The hospital tells him, and his father, under what provision he is held and that he may apply to the Board (section 22), and it reports the admission to the Board within seven days. For the first few days Bharat cannot weigh any information at all, so his father temporarily consents to the treatment plan, and the team review Bharat's capacity every seven days. He may not be chained or secluded, and if he must be physically restrained to prevent immediate harm, a psychiatrist must authorise it, it must last no longer than necessary, it must be recorded in his notes, and his father must be told within 24 hours (section 97). By the third week Bharat can discuss his treatment; the supported admission ends, and he chooses to stay on as an independent patient. Before he goes home, the psychiatrist meets him, his father and the psychiatrist who will see him at the district clinic to agree a discharge plan (section 98).

Restricted and prohibited treatments. Section 95, headed "Prohibited procedures", is short and absolute. Notwithstanding anything else in the Act, four things "shall not be performed on any person with mental illness": electroconvulsive therapy without the use of muscle relaxants and anaesthesia; electroconvulsive therapy for minors; sterilisation of men or women when it is intended as a treatment for mental illness; and chaining "in any manner or form whatsoever". One exception is written in: if the psychiatrist in charge of a minor's treatment thinks ECT is required, it may be given with the informed consent of the guardian and the prior permission of the Board. There is no such exception for ECT without anaesthesia. The treatment in question, electroconvulsive therapy (ECT), was first used by the Italian psychiatrists Ugo Cerletti (1877 to 1963) and Lucio Bini (1908 to 1964) in Rome in 1938; it induces a brief generalised seizure by passing a current across the head. Given without anaesthesia and muscle relaxants, as was common for decades and was still done in parts of India before 2017, it is called unmodified ECT (also "direct" ECT): the patient is awake as the seizure begins and the muscular convulsion can fracture bones. Given under a short general anaesthetic with a muscle relaxant, it is modified ECT, and that is the only kind Indian law now allows.

The other restrictions follow the same logic. Psychosurgery may be performed as a treatment for mental illness only with the informed consent of the person and the approval of the Board (section 96). A person with mental illness may not be subjected to seclusion or solitary confinement; physical restraint is allowed only when it is the only way to prevent imminent and immediate harm and is authorised by the psychiatrist in charge, may never be used as punishment or because staff are short, must be reported to the nominated representative within 24 hours, and must be included in a monthly report to the Board (section 97). Research involving interviews or any psychological, physical, chemical or medicinal intervention needs the free and informed consent of the participant; research on a person who cannot consent but does not resist needs the permission of the State Authority, the consent of the nominated representative and the approval of an institutional ethics committee, and only when the research cannot be done on people who can consent and serves the mental health needs of the group the person belongs to (section 99). Table 1.25 brings the admission rules together.

Table 1.25 Admission and treatment routes under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017.

Minor (87)Nominated representative (legal guardian)Two: psychiatrist plus psychiatrist, mental health professional or doctorReviewed at 30 days and every 30 days afterTold within 72 hours; decides challenges in 7 days

Exam trap

JEMAS 2025 asked when ECT without anaesthesia is legally allowed, offering "in emergencies, with consent and under strict guidelines", "whenever requested by a psychiatrist", "for research only" and "never allowed under law". The answer is never. Section 95(1)(a) lists "electro-convulsive therapy without the use of muscle relaxants and anaesthesia" among procedures that "shall not be performed on any person with mental illness", with no exception at all; consent, emergency or research make no difference. Do not be misled by summaries saying that the Act "restricts ECT to emergencies": section 94(3) says the opposite, that emergency treatment may never include ECT. For minors the ban is not absolute: modified ECT is possible with the guardian's informed consent and the Board's prior permission (section 95(2)). Sterilisation as a treatment and chaining are also prohibited; psychosurgery is allowed only with the person's consent and the Board's approval.

Police, magistrates and prisons; section 115; the three Acts compared

Under the 1912 Act the police and the magistrate were the gatekeepers of the asylum. The 2017 Act keeps them but changes their job from confinement to protection. The officer in charge of a police station must take under protection a person found wandering whom he believes to have a mental illness and to be unable to care for himself, or who is a risk to himself or others because of mental illness, and take him to the nearest public health establishment for assessment within 24 hours; such a person may never be held in a police lock-up or prison, and if he does not need admission the police must take him home or to a government shelter and try to trace his family (section 100). Anyone who believes that a person with mental illness is being ill-treated or neglected must report it to the police, who must report it to a magistrate (section 101), and a magistrate may send a person for assessment or authorise his admission for up to ten days for that purpose (section 102). Prisoners with mental illness are to be treated in a psychiatric ward in the prison's medical wing or transferred to an establishment, and every state must set up a mental health establishment in at least one prison (section 103); residents of beggars' homes, orphanages, women's protection homes and children's homes who appear mentally ill must be taken for assessment (section 104); and when proof of mental illness is challenged in court, the court refers the question to the Board (section 105). One short section speaks to every profession: no mental health professional or medical practitioner may "specify or recommend any medicine or treatment not authorised by the field of his profession" (section 106). For a clinical psychologist this is the legal form of an ethical rule: you do not prescribe medication.

The best-known section of the Act sits in its miscellaneous chapter. For more than 150 years, attempting suicide was an offence under section 309 of the Indian Penal Code (1860), punishable with imprisonment. Section 115(1) of the Mental Healthcare Act provides that, notwithstanding section 309, "any person who attempts to commit suicide shall be presumed, unless proved otherwise, to have severe stress and shall not be tried and punished under the said Code". Section 115(2) adds a duty: the government must provide care, treatment and rehabilitation to a person who has severe stress and has attempted suicide, "to reduce the risk of recurrence of attempt to commit suicide". This presumption of severe stress is why textbooks say the Act decriminalised attempted suicide in effect, although it did not repeal section 309 itself. When the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita replaced the Indian Penal Code on 1 July 2024, it did not carry over a general offence of attempting suicide; what remains is a narrower offence of attempting suicide in order to compel or restrain a public servant in the discharge of his duty. Chapter 2 shows how this change helps a clinician take an honest history after an attempt, and Section 9.7 places it within suicide prevention.

Two final points complete the Act. Contravening its provisions is punishable with imprisonment of up to six months or a fine of up to 10,000 rupees or both for a first offence, and up to two years or a fine of 50,000 rupees to five lakh rupees or both thereafter (section 108); and the Act overrides any inconsistent law (section 120).

Step back now and compare the three laws. Each one was written for a different idea of what a person with mental illness is: a danger to be confined, a patient to be treated, or a citizen with rights who sometimes needs support. Table 1.26 sets them side by side; it is one of the most useful tables in this book for the Indian papers.

Table 1.26 India's three mental health Acts compared.
FeatureIndian Lunacy Act 1912Mental Health Act 1987Mental Healthcare Act 2017
Act number; in forceAct IV of 1912Act 14 of 1987; in force 1 April 1993Act 10 of 2017; in force 29 May 2018
Basic outlookCustodial: protect societyTreatment-oriented, still proceduralRights-based, aligned with the UNCRPD
The person is calledLunatic ("an idiot or a person of unsound mind")Mentally ill person (mental retardation excluded)Person with mental illness (mental retardation excluded; alcohol and drug conditions included)
The place of careAsylumLicensed psychiatric hospital or nursing homeRegistered mental health establishment, including general hospitals

Exam trap

IHBAS 2025 asked for "a key legal provision under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017". The answer is that suicide attempts are decriminalised in effect, under section 115, which presumes severe stress. Each wrong option is a law you now know is false: ECT cannot be given "without consent in all cases" (independent patients are treated only with informed consent, supported patients with their advance directive or supported consent, and minors with the guardian's consent and the Board's permission); psychologists are not excluded (a registered clinical psychologist is a mental health professional); and government-run establishments are not exempt from registration (section 65). Note the precise wording of section 115: "presumed, unless proved otherwise, to have severe stress".

The disability laws: the PwD Act 1995, the National Trust Act 1999 and the RPwD Act 2016

Mental health law decides how a person is treated when he is ill. Disability law decides what he is entitled to when an impairment, in the world as it is, keeps him from taking part on equal terms: a reserved job, a seat in college, a pension, a guardian, protection from abuse. For a clinical psychologist the two meet constantly, because intellectual disability, autism, specific learning disabilities and long-standing mental illness are all disabilities in Indian law, and their assessment is the psychologist's daily work. Section 9.9 of this book (its subsection on disability certification) explains how each of them is certified; here you need the laws themselves.

The Persons with Disabilities Act 1995. India's first comprehensive disability law was the Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities, Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act 1995, passed to give effect to the Proclamation on the Full Participation and Equality of People with Disabilities in the Asian and Pacific Region, adopted at Beijing in December 1992, to which India was a signatory. Because it received assent early in 1996 it is numbered Act 1 of 1996, but it is always called the PwD Act 1995. It recognised seven disabilities: blindness, low vision, leprosy-cured, hearing impairment, locomotor disability, mental retardation and mental illness, the last defined simply as "any mental disorder other than mental retardation". It reserved not less than 3 percent of vacancies in government establishments for persons with disabilities, one percent each for blindness or low vision, for hearing impairment, and for locomotor disability or cerebral palsy (section 33), so that people with mental illness or mental retardation had no reserved posts at all. Autism, cerebral palsy as a condition in its own right, and multiple disabilities had no separate place in it, and the families of people with these conditions pressed for a law of their own.

The National Trust Act 1999. The result was the National Trust for Welfare of Persons with Autism, Cerebral Palsy, Mental Retardation and Multiple Disabilities Act 1999 (Act 44 of 1999), usually called the National Trust Act 1999. Its long title names its four conditions, and its definitions describe them: autism as "a condition of uneven skill development primarily affecting the communication and social abilities of a person"; cerebral palsy as "a group of non-progressive conditions of a person characterised by abnormal motor control and posture"; mental retardation as "a condition of arrested or incomplete development of mind of a person", marked especially by subnormal intelligence; and multiple disabilities as a combination of two or more of the disabilities defined in the 1995 Act. The Act set up a statutory body, the National Trust, managed by a Board and working under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. Its objects (section 10) include enabling persons with these disabilities to live as independently and as fully as possible within and close to the community to which they belong, strengthening support for them to live with their own families, supporting registered organisations that help in a family crisis, caring for those without family support, and evolving procedures for the appointment of guardians and trustees.

That last object answers the question every parent of an adult with severe intellectual disability eventually asks: "What happens to my child after us?" Once a person turns 18, his parents are no longer his natural guardians in law, yet he may be unable to sign a bank form or a property document. The National Trust Act provides for legal guardianship through a local level committee, which the Trust's Board constitutes for a district or other area (section 13). Each committee has three members: an officer of the civil service not below the rank of District Magistrate or District Commissioner, a representative of a registered organisation, and a person with disability. A parent or relative of a person with one of the four disabilities may apply to the committee to appoint a guardian of the parent's choice, and a registered organisation may also apply, with the consent of the existing guardian (section 14). The guardian has legal duties of care, must keep an inventory of the person's property and file annual accounts, and can be removed if he abuses or neglects his ward (sections 15 to 17). The Act does not cover mental illness. A person with mental illness is supported instead by a nominated representative under the Mental Healthcare Act, and, if a guardian is ever needed, by the limited guardianship of the RPwD Act described below.

Exam trap

The National Trust covers exactly four conditions: autism, cerebral palsy, mental retardation (now called intellectual disability) and multiple disabilities. It does not cover mental illness, specific learning disabilities, blindness or deafness on their own. Guardianship under it is granted by a local level committee headed by a District Magistrate-rank officer, not by a court and not by the Review Board. The 1995 Act had seven disabilities and a 3 percent job reservation; the 2016 Act has 21 and 4 percent.

The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016. Like the Mental Healthcare Act, the new disability law was written to carry out the UNCRPD; its long title says it is "An Act to give effect to the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities". The Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 (RPwD Act; Act 49 of 2016) received the President's assent on 27 December 2016 and came into force on 19 April 2017, and section 102 repealed the 1995 Act. Its definitions move from impairment to participation. A person with disability is "a person with long term physical, mental, intellectual or sensory impairment which, in interaction with barriers, hinders his full and effective participation in society equally with others" (section 2(s)), which is the Convention's social model written into Indian law. A person with benchmark disability is a person with not less than 40 percent of a specified disability, as certified by the certifying authority (section 2(r)), and most of the Act's special entitlements are reserved for this group. A person with disability having high support needs is a person with benchmark disability who is certified as needing high support (section 2(t)). The specified disabilities (section 2(zc)) are those listed in the Schedule to the Act, and the Central Government may add more by notification. Table 1.27 lists all twenty-one in the order the government uses when it counts them.

Table 1.27 The 21 specified disabilities of the RPwD Act 2016, with their place in the Schedule.
No.Specified disabilityGroup in the Schedule
1BlindnessPhysical: visual impairment
2Low visionPhysical: visual impairment
3Leprosy cured personsPhysical: locomotor disability
4Hearing impairment (deaf and hard of hearing)Physical: hearing impairment
5Locomotor disabilityPhysical: locomotor disability
6Dwarfism (adult height 147 cm or less)Physical: locomotor disability
7Intellectual disabilityIntellectual disability
8Mental illnessMental behaviour
9Autism spectrum disorderIntellectual disability
10Cerebral palsyPhysical: locomotor disability
11Muscular dystrophyPhysical: locomotor disability
12Chronic neurological conditionsChronic neurological conditions

14Multiple sclerosisChronic neurological conditions
15Speech and language disabilityPhysical

17HaemophiliaBlood disorder
18Sickle cell diseaseBlood disorder
19Multiple disabilities, including deaf-blindnessMultiple disabilities
