Look at how the Schedule groups the conditions a psychologist assesses. Under "Intellectual disability", defined as "a condition characterised by significant limitation both in intellectual functioning (reasoning, learning, problem solving) and in adaptive behaviour which covers a range of every day, social and practical skills", it places two further conditions. Specific learning disabilities are "a heterogeneous group of conditions wherein there is a deficit in processing language, spoken or written, that may manifest itself as a difficulty to comprehend, speak, read, write, spell, or to do mathematical calculations", including perceptual disabilities, dyslexia, dysgraphia, dyscalculia, dyspraxia and developmental aphasia. Autism spectrum disorder is "a neuro-developmental condition typically appearing in the first three years of life that significantly affects a person's ability to communicate, understand relationships and relate to others", frequently with unusual or stereotyped rituals or behaviours. Under "Mental behaviour" the Schedule defines mental illness in almost the same words as the Mental Healthcare Act, "a substantial disorder of thinking, mood, perception, orientation or memory that grossly impairs judgment, behaviour, capacity to recognise reality or ability to meet the ordinary demands of life", again excluding mental retardation; unlike the 2017 Act's definition, it does not mention alcohol and drug conditions. Notice also what the new list added: speech and language disability, specific learning disabilities, acid attack victims and the three blood disorders, among others, were included for the first time.

The Act turns these definitions into rights. Persons with disabilities enjoy legal capacity on an equal basis with others, including the right to own property and control their financial affairs (section 13). Where a person who has been given adequate support still cannot take a legally binding decision, a district court or designated authority may provide a limited guardian to take that decision with him (section 14). The Act defines limited guardianship as "a system of joint decision which operates on mutual understanding and trust between the guardian and the person with disability", limited to a specific period, decision and situation and operating in accordance with the person's will; every guardian appointed under any other law is deemed to be a limited guardian. Every child with benchmark disability between six and eighteen years has the right to free education in a neighbourhood school or a special school of his choice (section 31). Government and government-aided institutions of higher education must reserve not less than 5 percent of seats for persons with benchmark disabilities, with five years' upper age relaxation (section 32). Every government establishment must reserve not less than 4 percent of vacancies for persons with benchmark disabilities: one percent each for blindness and low vision, for deaf and hard of hearing, and for locomotor disability (including cerebral palsy, leprosy cured, dwarfism, acid attack victims and muscular dystrophy), and one percent for autism, intellectual disability, specific learning disability and mental illness together with multiple disabilities (section 34). The Act is enforced by a Chief Commissioner for Persons with Disabilities (section 74) and State Commissioners (section 79), and a Court of Session in each district is designated a Special Court for its offences (section 84). Atrocities such as humiliating a person with disability in public view, sexually exploiting a woman or child with disability, denying food to a dependent person, or terminating a woman's pregnancy without her consent are punishable with six months' to five years' imprisonment and a fine (section 92).

Exam trap

The RPwD Act was passed in 2016 and recognises 21 disabilities. The distractors in the recall question are built from real numbers: 1995 and 7 belong to the old PwD Act; 19 was the number of conditions in the Bill introduced in the Rajya Sabha in 2014, before the list grew to 21 in the Act Parliament passed in December 2016; and 2017 is the year the 2016 Act came into force (19 April 2017), not the year it was passed. "Benchmark disability" means not less than 40 percent. Reservation is 4 percent in government jobs and 5 percent in higher education. Free education runs from 6 to 18 years.

Exam trap

"Which of the following is NOT a specified disability?" Check the option against Table 1.27. Mental illness, autism spectrum disorder, specific learning disabilities, intellectual disability, cerebral palsy, multiple sclerosis, Parkinson's disease, thalassemia, haemophilia, sickle cell disease, dwarfism, acid attack and speech and language disability are all in. Conditions that are not named in the Schedule, such as attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, HIV infection, diabetes or obesity, are not specified disabilities unless the Central Government notifies them under the Schedule's final item. (The Schedule's "chronic neurological conditions" are introduced with the words "such as", followed by multiple sclerosis and Parkinson's disease, so that heading is not closed; a question asking you to rule out a neurological condition such as epilepsy is on weaker ground.) Note too that the Schedule says "leprosy cured person", not leprosy, and that autism and specific learning disabilities sit under the heading intellectual disability, while mental illness has its own heading, "mental behaviour".

The RCI Act 1992 and the NDPS Act 1985

Who may call himself a clinical psychologist in India, and what happens to someone who does without the right to? The answer is in a law that, at first sight, has nothing to do with mental health. The Rehabilitation Council of India began as a registered society in 1986 and became a statutory body under the Rehabilitation Council of India Act 1992 (Act 34 of 1992), which received the President's assent on 1 September 1992 and came into force in 1993; Section 1.5 tells that history and describes the Council's place in the profession. Here the concern is what the Act says. Its long title describes it as "An Act to provide for the constitution of the Rehabilitation Council of India for regulating the training of rehabilitation professionals" and for maintaining a register of them. The Council is constituted under section 3. The Act's definition of rehabilitation professionals (section 2) is a list that includes audiologists and speech therapists, clinical psychologists, hearing aid and ear mould technicians, rehabilitation engineers and technicians, special teachers, vocational counsellors, employment officers and placement officers, multipurpose rehabilitation therapists, and any other category the Central Government notifies in consultation with the Council. That single word in a list of definitions is why the training and registration of clinical psychologists in India sit with a council under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment, not with the health ministry.

The Act's working parts are few. Qualifications granted by universities and institutions in India that are included in the Schedule to the Act are "recognised rehabilitation qualifications" (section 11), and any such qualification is sufficient for enrolment on the Register (section 13(1)). The Register is the Central Rehabilitation Register, which the Council's Member-Secretary must keep and maintain and which is a public document within the meaning of the Indian Evidence Act (section 23); professionals are entered in it on application, once the Member-Secretary is satisfied that they hold a recognised qualification (section 19). Then comes the clause that matters most. Section 13(2) provides that no person, other than a rehabilitation professional who possesses a recognised rehabilitation qualification and is enrolled on the Register, shall practise as a rehabilitation professional anywhere in India, and section 13(3) makes contravention punishable with imprisonment for a term which may extend to one year, or with a fine which may extend to one thousand rupees, or with both. A psychologist who sees clients as a "clinical psychologist" without RCI registration is therefore not merely unethical but committing an offence.

The Rehabilitation Council of India (Amendment) Act 2000 (Act 38 of 2000) widened the Council's mandate, adding the monitoring of training and "promoting research in rehabilitation and special education" to its purposes, linking its definitions to the PwD Act 1995, and extending its reach from rehabilitation professionals to rehabilitation personnel as well.

Exam trap

"Which Act mandates that rehabilitation professionals, including clinical psychologists, be registered with the RCI?" The Rehabilitation Council of India Act 1992, section 13. Not the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (which defines a clinical psychologist by an RCI-recognised qualification and adds a separate registration with the State Mental Health Authority to work as a mental health professional), not the PwD Act 1995, and not the "Mental Healthcare Act 1987", which does not exist. The RCI was made statutory by the RCI Act 1992 (society 1986; Act in force, and the Council a statutory body, from 1993; amended 2000) and sits under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. Practising without registration: up to one year or up to 1,000 rupees or both.

One more law touches clinical work with addictions. The Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances Act 1985 (NDPS Act; Act 61 of 1985), administered by the Department of Revenue in the Ministry of Finance, controls the production, sale, possession and use of narcotic drugs and psychotropic substances. Its punishments for trafficking are severe, but two of its provisions concern the person who uses drugs. Section 27 makes consumption itself an offence, punishable with imprisonment of up to one year for some drugs and up to six months for the rest. Section 64A offers a way out: an addict charged with consumption under section 27, or with an offence involving a small quantity of a drug, who voluntarily seeks treatment for de-addiction in a hospital or institution maintained or recognised by the government, is immune from prosecution, although the immunity may be withdrawn if he does not complete the treatment. The Act also empowers the government to set up centres for identifying, treating and rehabilitating addicts. For a psychologist in a de-addiction centre, section 64A is a clinical tool as well as a legal one: it lets a frightened young man come forward for treatment without first going to court. And because the Mental Healthcare Act's definition of mental illness includes conditions associated with the abuse of alcohol and drugs, the same young man, once admitted, has every right that the 2017 Act gives. The practice of addiction treatment and relapse prevention is taught in Section 9.9.

National programmes, the 2014 policy, the national survey and Tele-MANAS

Laws give people rights; programmes decide whether there is anyone in the district to honour them. In the early 1980s most psychiatric care in India was given in large mental hospitals in a few cities, and there were very few psychiatrists, clinical psychologists, psychiatric social workers and psychiatric nurses for a population of about 700 million. Pilot projects in the 1970s (Section 9.6 describes those at Raipur Rani in Haryana and Sakalwara near Bengaluru) had shown that doctors and health workers in primary health centres could be trained to recognise and treat mental disorders. Following the recommendations of the World Health Organization, the government built a national programme on that finding.

The National Mental Health Programme (1982). The National Mental Health Programme (NMHP), launched in 1982, set out three objectives that are still quoted almost word for word: "to ensure the availability and accessibility of minimum mental healthcare for all in the foreseeable future", particularly to the most vulnerable and underprivileged sections of the population; to encourage the application of mental health knowledge in general health care and in social development; and to promote community participation in the development of mental health services and stimulate self-help in the community. (The health ministry's current statement of the programme adds a fourth: to enhance human resources in the mental health sub-specialties.) Its central strategy was integration: mental health was to be delivered through the general health system, above all through primary health care, rather than only through specialist hospitals.

The District Mental Health Programme (1996) and the Bellary model. To find out how integration could work across a whole district, NIMHANS ran a pilot project in Bellary district of Karnataka from 1985 to 1990. Doctors and health workers at district, taluk and primary health centre level were trained to deliver basic mental health care, with a small specialist team at the district hospital supporting them. This Bellary model became the template for the District Mental Health Programme (DMHP), launched under the NMHP in 1996 in four districts and expanded to 27 districts by the end of the Ninth Five-Year Plan. A DMHP team (typically a psychiatrist, a clinical psychologist, a psychiatric social worker and a psychiatric nurse at the district hospital) is responsible for four components: early detection and treatment, training of general health staff, public awareness (information, education and communication), and simple record keeping for monitoring. Over time it added outpatient and inpatient services at the district hospital, outreach through satellite clinics at community and primary health centres, and targeted interventions such as life-skills training and counselling in schools and colleges, workplace stress management and suicide prevention. The programme now runs within the National Health Mission, and by 2024 the health ministry was reporting 767 districts sanctioned under it, nearly every district in the country.

After an evaluation in 2003 the NMHP was restructured. Two schemes were added that year, one to modernise the state mental hospitals and one to upgrade the psychiatric wings of medical colleges and general hospitals, and in 2009 two manpower development schemes followed: Scheme A, which funds Centres of Excellence by upgrading existing institutions to train more psychiatrists, clinical psychologists, psychiatric social workers and psychiatric nurses, and Scheme B, which supports postgraduate departments in these four disciplines. The shortage of trained people that these schemes tried to fill is the background to the task-shifting described in Section 1.5 and Chapter 9.

Exam trap

NMHP 1982; Bellary pilot 1985 to 1990 (run by NIMHANS, in Karnataka); DMHP 1996, starting in 4 districts. Do not swap 1982 and 1996, and do not place Bellary in Andhra Pradesh or Tamil Nadu. The NMHP's first objective begins "to ensure the availability and accessibility of minimum mental healthcare for all", which an option may paraphrase as "basic mental health care for all". Raipur Rani (Haryana, run from PGIMER Chandigarh) and Sakalwara (near Bengaluru) were the earlier pilot projects, not the model for the DMHP.

The National Mental Health Policy (2014). In April 2011 the government set up a Mental Health Policy Group to recommend a policy for the country. Its work became India's first National Mental Health Policy, released by the Union Health Minister, Harsh Vardhan, on World Mental Health Day, 10 October 2014, under the title New Pathways, New Hope, together with a one-year action plan. Its vision is "to promote mental health, prevent mental illness, enable recovery from mental illness, promote de-stigmatization and desegregation, and ensure socio-economic inclusion of persons affected by mental illness by providing accessible, affordable and quality health and social care to all persons through their life-span, within a rights-based framework". Its values and principles are equity, justice, integrated care, evidence-based care, quality, a participatory and rights-based approach, governance and effective delivery, a value base in all training and teaching, and a holistic approach that recognises the relationship between mind, body and soul. Its goals are to reduce distress, disability, exclusion, morbidity and premature mortality associated with mental health problems across the life span; to enhance understanding of mental health in the country; and to strengthen leadership in mental health at national, state and district levels. Its first objective is "to provide universal access to mental health care". A policy is not a law: it binds nobody in court. But the 2014 policy set out the direction that the 2017 Act then turned into enforceable rights, and you can hear its language (access, rights, community, recovery) in the Act's sections.

The National Mental Health Survey (2015 to 2016). A programme cannot be planned without knowing how many people need it, and until 2016 India had only scattered local studies. The national survey filled that gap, and it is the study you are most likely to be asked about.

Key study: the National Mental Health Survey of India (2015 to 2016)

The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare commissioned NIMHANS, Bengaluru, to find out how common mental disorders are in India, how many affected people receive treatment, and how well each state's mental health system works. The survey team visited households chosen by multistage, stratified random sampling in 12 states (three districts in each, chosen by poverty level, with rural, urban non-metro and urban metro clusters) and interviewed every adult member: 34,802 adults in all, plus about 1,191 adolescents aged 13 to 17 in four of the states. Diagnoses were made with the Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview (MINI 6.0), which gives ICD-10 diagnoses in a single interview. The result: excluding tobacco use disorder, about 10.6 percent of adults had a current mental disorder and 13.7 percent had had one in their lifetime, which, scaled to the population, meant that nearly 150 million Indians needed active intervention, while fewer than 30 million were seeking care. The treatment gap was above 60 percent for every disorder except epilepsy, and the burden was heaviest in the urban metros. The conclusion was that India's mental health systems were "weak, fragmented and uncoordinated". The criticisms are those of every household survey: people who are homeless or in institutions are missed, alcohol and drug problems are under-reported to interviewers, and the MINI captures only the more severe end of alcohol use, so the true figures are probably higher.

Table 1.28 collects the figures examiners use, and Figure 1.17 shows the treatment gap disorder by disorder. Look at the shape of the bars: even for the most visible severe illnesses, bipolar disorder and psychosis, seven in every ten people were not being treated, and for depression, the neurotic and stress-related disorders and alcohol use disorder the gap was well over eight in ten.

Exam trap

The survey is the National Mental Health Survey 2015 to 2016, carried out by NIMHANS for the health ministry in 12 states. Current prevalence 10.6 percent; lifetime 13.7 percent; about 150 million in need. The treatment gap is usually quoted as "70 to 92 percent", and that is the survey's own figure: its report gives gaps from 70.4 percent for bipolar disorder to 91.8 percent for tobacco use, with 85.0 percent for common mental disorders, 73.6 percent for severe mental disorders and 86.3 percent for alcohol use disorder. The upper end, 92, is tobacco; among the mental disorders in Figure 1.17 the gap runs from about 70 to 86 percent. The survey's shorter Summary words the same finding differently (more than 60 percent for every mental disorder except epilepsy, and 86 percent for alcohol use disorders), so do not be thrown by a different range. Choose the option that says most affected people receive no treatment. Do not confuse the 10.6 percent figure for adults with the 7.3 percent figure for adolescents.

Helplines and tele-mental health. The newest layer of the national response uses the telephone. In September 2020, during the COVID-19 pandemic, the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment launched KIRAN (1800-599-0019), a 24-hour toll-free mental health rehabilitation helpline in 13 languages, coordinated by its national institutes in Chennai and Sehore, offering screening, psychological first aid, support and referral. Then, after an announcement in the Union Budget of 2022, the health ministry launched Tele-MANAS (Tele Mental Health Assistance and Networking Across States) on World Mental Health Day, 10 October 2022. It is a free, round-the-clock service, reached at the toll-free number 14416 or 1-800-891-4416, in which callers choose their language. It has two tiers: state Tele-MANAS cells staffed by trained counsellors and mental health specialists form the first tier, and specialists at District Mental Health Programme and medical college centres, seen in person or by video through the government's e-Sanjeevani telemedicine service, form the second. NIMHANS is the nodal centre, with technical support from the International Institute of Information Technology, Bangalore. Section 9.7 places Tele-MANAS and India's first National Suicide Prevention Strategy (2022) within crisis work, and Table 1.29 lists the whole sequence of programmes and services in order.

Clinical link

Put the pieces of this section together and you have a description of a clinical psychologist's legal position in India. You are trained on an RCI-recognised course and must be on the Central Rehabilitation Register before you practise (RCI Act, section 13). You are a "clinical psychologist" under section 2(g) of the Mental Healthcare Act and, once registered with the State Mental Health Authority, a "mental health professional" who can examine patients for admission and be in charge of an establishment, but who may never prescribe (section 106). Your clients have rights to information, confidentiality and records that override habit. When you assess a child for intellectual disability, autism or a learning disability, your report may open the door to the RPwD Act's entitlements, and for some families to a National Trust guardian. And in a district clinic or on a Tele-MANAS line you are part of the programme that turns all these rights into care.

The case of Charles Gilman, revisited

Go back to the University of Pennsylvania in March 1896, where Margaret Maguire brought Lightner Witmer a fourteen-year-old who could not spell. You can now see that the whole profession is already in the case. By the criteria of Section 1.6 Charles was abnormal in a narrow and useful sense: he deviated sharply from his classmates, having lost three years in eight, and his difficulty was a dysfunction that blocked his schooling, though he was serious, industrious and plainly not dull. Witmer's handling of him was the scientist-practitioner model half a century before the Boulder conference named it: he took a single case, examined it with the methods of science, formed a hypothesis about the cause, called in a medical colleague, designed a treatment delivered through a teacher, and followed the boy up for years. Sending Charles to Dr Posey was the multidisciplinary team in miniature, and a lesson in the boundaries of competence: when a problem may be physical, the psychologist refers. And no single model of Section 1.7 explains the case. Its causes were biological (the two eyes could not work together on one point), psychological (he could not hold a written word in mind as a whole) and educational, and it needed all three kinds of help, which is the biopsychosocial model eighty years before Engel gave it a name.

Now imagine Charles brought to a clinic in India today. The psychologist who sees him, like the one who sees Aditi in Section 1.1, must hold an RCI-recognised qualification and be enrolled on the Central Rehabilitation Register, which the RCI Act 1992 makes compulsory, and she would begin, as Witmer did, by having his vision checked. Because he is a minor she would need his parent's consent and his own assent (Section 1.8), explain at the first session what stays private and what is shared, and treat a request from his school for his results as a third-party request (Section 1.9). If her assessment pointed to a specific learning disability, the RPwD Act 2016 would count it among its 21 specified disabilities, and her report would support his disability certificate. Even Witmer's decision to publish the boy under the name "Charles Gilman" anticipates what Standard 4.07 still asks of anyone who writes about a client: disguise the client thoroughly, or obtain written consent. Witmer wrote that "clinical" implies a method, not a locality. This chapter has shown what that method has become, an applied science, a regulated profession and a set of legal and ethical duties, and the boy who could not spell is where it began.

Chapter summary

1.1 What Clinical Psychology Is. Lightner Witmer founded the first psychological clinic (Pennsylvania, 1896), put the term clinical psychology into print (1907) and insisted that "clinical" implies a method, not a locality. APA Division 12 defines the field as integrating science, theory and practice to understand, predict and alleviate maladjustment, disability and discomfort and to promote adaptation and personal development. In India the title is regulated: a clinical psychologist needs an RCI-recognised qualification and entry on the Central Rehabilitation Register. In the multidisciplinary team only the psychiatrist is a medical doctor who prescribes; the psychiatric social worker works with the family and social world (casework, group work in Trecker's principles, community organisation) and the psychiatric nurse gives continuous care. Counselling psychology (Division 17) serves normal-range problems. The work spans assessment, diagnosis and formulation, psychotherapy (the largest share of time in Norcross's surveys), research, teaching, consultation, administration and expert testimony.

1.2 From Demons to Asylums. Supernatural, biological and psychological explanations of madness take turns through history. Stone Age trephining is usually read as releasing an evil spirit; demonology blames possession and exorcism treats it. Hippocrates gave the naturalistic view, the four humours and the classes mania, melancholia and phrenitis, and the word hysteria; Galen added the four temperaments. The bimaristans and Avicenna kept humane medicine alive, and Charaka described five types of unmada. The Middle Ages mixed demons with natural explanations (Charles VI, Oresme, family care at Geel) and saw dancing manias, now called mass psychogenic illness. Kramer and Sprenger's Malleus Maleficarum (1486) armed the witch hunts; Weyer (1563) argued that the accused were ill. Then came asylums: Valencia (1409), Bethlem ("Bedlam"), San Hipólito in Mexico City, the great confinement of 1656 that Foucault described, and India's first asylum at Bombay (1745).

1.3 Reform, Moral Treatment and Modern Psychiatry. In the 1790s reform made kindness a treatment. Chiarugi banned chains in Florence; Pinel at the Bicêtre (1793) gave the reform its authority, though Pussin removed the chains (1797). The Quaker William Tuke opened the York Retreat (1796), and moral treatment, "moral" meaning psychological, offered small institutions, routine, work and reward. Dorothea Dix's crusade (her Memorial, 1843) filled state hospitals too large for it, and custodial care followed. Clifford Beers, a former patient, wrote A Mind That Found Itself (1908) and launched the mental hygiene movement (National Committee, 1909). The somatogenic perspective triumphed with general paresis: Krafft-Ebing's inoculation experiment (1897), Noguchi and Moore's spirochaete (1913), Wagner-Jauregg's fever therapy. Kraepelin separated dementia praecox from manic-depressive insanity by course and outcome, and Bleuler renamed the first schizophrenia (1908). The psychogenic perspective ran from Mesmer through Charcot, Janet and Breuer to Freud.

1.4 The Rise of Clinical Psychology in the West. Witmer's clinic studied the individual case, began with assessment, called in physicians and treated through teachers. The testing era ran from Galton's laboratory (1884) and Cattell's "mental test" (1890) through the Binet-Simon scale (1905) and Terman's Stanford-Binet (1916) to Yerkes's Army Alpha (verbal) and Beta (non-verbal) in 1917 and Wechsler's scale (1939). Healy's Chicago institute (1909) began child guidance; the APA (Hall, 1892) gained a Clinical Section in 1919 and Division 12 in 1945. After the Second World War the Veterans Administration funded training, the Shakow report (1947) set the plan and the Boulder conference (1949) adopted the scientist-practitioner model and the PhD. Vail (1973) endorsed the practitioner-scholar model and the PsyD, first offered at Illinois (1968), and McFall's manifesto (1991) launched the clinical science model. Certification protects the title, licensure the practice; New Mexico (2002) first gave psychologists prescription privileges.

1.5 Clinical Psychology in India. Sen Gupta founded India's first psychology department at Calcutta (1916), and Girindrasekhar Bose, author of the theory of opposite wishes, founded the Indian Psychoanalytic Society (1922). The Ranchi European Lunatic Asylum (1918), reformed by Berkeley-Hill, became the Central Institute of Psychiatry; the All India Institute of Mental Health, Bangalore (1954), began clinical psychology training in 1955 and became NIMHANS in 1974. The Indian Association of Clinical Psychologists formed in 1968. The RCI, a society from 1986, became statutory under the RCI Act 1992, sits under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment and keeps the Central Rehabilitation Register. After NEP 2020 the M.Phil is giving way to the RCI's two-year master's (from 2025-26, renamed M.Clin.Psy), while the one-year PDCP leads to Associate registration. Indian practice means few clinicians, the family in the room, other healers first, stigma, and task-shifting to lay counsellors.

1.6 What Counts as Abnormal. No single criterion defines abnormality. The statistical criterion would call genius abnormal and miss common disorders; the social-norm criterion suffers from cultural relativism and has been abused (drapetomania, Soviet dissidents, homosexuality, removed from the DSM in 1973). Maladaptiveness leaves open whose goals count, and personal distress misses mania while catching normal grief. Jahoda's ideal mental health (1958) has six criteria but sets the bar too high. Comer's four Ds are deviance, distress, dysfunction and danger, and Wakefield (1992) defined disorder as harmful dysfunction, the logic behind the DSM-5-TR definition. Szasz called mental illness a myth (1961); Rosenhan's pseudopatients (1973), Scheff's labelling theory and Goffman's stigma and total institution showed what labels do. Indian law forbids diagnosing anyone for nonconformity, and since DSM-5 culture-bound syndromes have become cultural concepts of distress, such as dhat syndrome.

1.7 Models of Psychopathology and Treatment. Each model names a cause and a treatment. The supernatural model blames spirits and treats by ritual. The biological (medical) model points to genes, brain chemistry and structure, treats physically, and is charged with reductionism. The psychodynamic model rests on Freud's id (pleasure principle), ego (reality principle) and superego (conscience and ego ideal), with Vaillant's four levels of defence. The behavioural model sees disorder as learned and eliminates unwanted behaviour; the cognitive model blames irrational beliefs and Beck's cognitive triad; the humanistic-existential model blames blocked growth and evaded existential anxiety; the sociocultural model weighs social causation against social drift; the family-systems model studies family rules and expressed emotion. Engel's biopsychosocial model (1977) says the levels always interact, and the diathesis-stress model (Meehl, 1962; Zubin and Spring, 1977) says disorder appears when stress exceeds a threshold set by vulnerability, now with protective factors and resilience.

1.8 Ethics I: Principles, Competence, Consent and Confidentiality. Codes exist because clients are vulnerable to the clinician's power. Deontology (Kant) judges acts by duty and utilitarianism (Bentham, Mill) by consequences; virtue ethics and Gilligan's ethics of care add character and relationships. Beauchamp and Childress (1979) gave four principles: respect for autonomy, nonmaleficence, beneficence and justice; Kitchener added fidelity. The APA code (2002, amended 2010 and 2016) has five aspirational General Principles (Beneficence and Nonmaleficence, Fidelity and Responsibility, Integrity, Justice, Respect for People's Rights and Dignity) and ten sections of enforceable Standards. Competence has boundaries. Informed consent needs capacity, voluntariness, information and comprehension; children give assent. Research rules grew from Nuremberg, Tuskegee, Milgram, Helsinki and Belmont, and deception requires debriefing. Confidentiality is the professional's duty, privilege the client's legal right; confidentiality has limits, and Tarasoff (1974, 1976) turned a duty to warn into a duty to protect.

1.9 Ethics II: Boundaries, Records, Assessment and New Settings. A multiple (dual) relationship adds another role to the professional one; the APA code forbids only those likely to impair objectivity or risk exploitation, and small communities make some unavoidable. Gutheil and Gabbard (1993) separated harmless boundary crossings from harmful violations, usually reached by a slippery slope. Sexual intimacy with a current client is always forbidden; with a former client, never within two years and afterwards only in the most unusual circumstances. Records serve five purposes and are kept secure. Test data go to the client on his release; test materials stay secure. Fabrication, falsification and plagiarism are research misconduct. Teletherapy needs location checks and secure platforms; trainees must disclose supervision. The RCI code binds Indian clinicians, and a seven-step model (recognise, get the facts, check the code, consult, weigh options, decide and document, review) guides dilemmas.

1.10 Mental Health Law and Policy in India. Indian law moved from custody to rights. The Indian Lunacy Act 1912 (reception orders) gave way to the Mental Health Act 1987 (in force 1993) and then the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (in force 29 May 2018), aligned with the UNCRPD. It presumes capacity, defines the clinical psychologist by an RCI-recognised qualification, and grants advance directives (section 5), nominated representatives (section 14) and the right to access mental healthcare (section 18). Authorities and District Judge-led Review Boards enforce it; section 95 bans unmodified ECT, sterilisation as treatment and chaining, and section 115 presumes severe stress in attempted suicide. The RPwD Act 2016 lists 21 disabilities (benchmark 40 percent), the National Trust Act 1999 provides guardianship, and the RCI Act 1992 makes registration compulsory. The NMHP (1982), the Bellary-model DMHP (1996), the NMHS (10.6 percent current prevalence) and Tele-MANAS (2022) complete the picture.

From the past papers

  1. Which one of the following is NOT a principle of social group work given by H.B. Trecker? (RML 2020)

    (a) The principle of democratic self determination (b) The principle of flexible functional organisation (c) The principle of progressive programme experience (d) The principle of focusing on change and possibilities

    Answer: (d). Trecker's ten principles include democratic group self-determination, flexible functional organisation and progressive programme experiences, together with planned group formation, specific objectives, a purposeful worker-group relationship, continuous individualisation, guided group interaction, resource utilisation and evaluation. "Focusing on change and possibilities" sounds like social work language, which is why it tempts, but it is not on the list.

  2. In the context of psychopathology, a "formulation" differs from a "diagnosis" in that a formulation primarily aims to: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Classify the client's symptoms according to a standardized nosological system like DSM-5. (b) Provide a hypothesis about the cause, precipitants, and maintaining factors of an individual's difficulties. (c) Determine the prognosis and likely course of the disorder over time. (d) Rule out organic causes for the presenting psychological symptoms.

    Answer: (b). A diagnosis places a person's problems in a category of a system such as ICD-11 or DSM-5-TR, which is option (a); a formulation explains why this person has this problem now, as an individual hypothesis about its causes, triggers and maintaining factors (Section 2.9 teaches how to build one). Forecasting the course and ruling out a physical cause belong to a full assessment, but neither is what distinguishes a formulation from a diagnosis.

  3. The first person to use the term clinical psychology in print was (RML 2020)

    (a) Erik Erikson (b) Sigmund Freud (c) Lightner Witmer (d) Cattell

    Answer: (c) Lightner Witmer. His article "Clinical psychology" opened the first issue of his journal The Psychological Clinic in 1907, the printed source that histories cite; he had founded the first psychological clinic in 1896. Cattell, Witmer's teacher, coined "mental test" (1890), Freud founded psychoanalysis and Erikson is the psychosocial stages.

  4. The Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI), the body that licenses clinical psychologists, was first set up as a registered society in 1986 and became a STATUTORY body through the RCI Act of: (Amity recall)

    (a) 1986 (b) 1992 (enforced 1993) (c) 1947 (d) 2016

    Answer: (b) 1992 (enforced 1993). The RCI began as a registered society in 1986 and became a statutory body, one created by an act of Parliament, under the RCI Act 1992, which came into force in 1993 and was amended in 2000. 1986 is the society, not the statute; 2016 is the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, and 1947 is a distractor year.

  5. To describe a predisposition towards and risk triggers for a mental disorder, modern theorists use a (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Diathesis-Stress model (b) Neurodevelopmental model (c) Clinical-Reductionist model (d) Syndrome-Sign model

    Answer: (a) Diathesis-stress model. The diathesis is the predisposition and the stress is the trigger, and the model (Meehl, 1962; Zubin and Spring, 1977) says disorder comes from their interaction. The neurodevelopmental model (Weinberger; Murray and Lewis, 1987) is a narrower theory of early brain disturbance, while "clinical-reductionist" and "syndrome-sign" are not established models at all: reductionism is a criticism, and symptoms, signs and syndromes describe a disorder without explaining it.

  6. The diathesis-stress model of psychopathology suggests that mental disorders result from : (RML 2023)

    (a) Genetic factors only (b) Environmental factors only (c) A combination of genetic predisposition and environmental stressors (d) Neurobiological abnormalities only

    Answer: (c). The model's whole point is the combination: a diathesis alone usually produces no disorder, stress alone usually produces none either, and the greater the vulnerability, the less stress it takes. Every option with "only" names a single factor and is wrong for that reason, and the same logic disposes of "purely biological causes", "learned experiences alone" and "supernatural influences" when RML 2025 asked the question again.

  7. Biopsychosocial model of illness was proposed by (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Engel, G.L. (b) Freud, S. (c) Cannon, W.B. (d) Lazarus, R.S.

    Answer: (a) Engel, G.L. George Engel's paper "The need for a new medical model: A challenge for biomedicine" (Science, 1977) argued that biological, psychological and social factors always act together, against the reductionist biomedical model. Freud is the psychodynamic model, Walter Cannon the fight-or-flight response and homeostasis, and Richard Lazarus the transactional model of stress and coping.

  8. According to Freud, the id is to the ___ principle as the ego is to the ____ principle. (RML 2020)

    (a) aggressive, sexual (b) sexual, aggressive (c) pleasure, reality (d) reality, pleasure

    Answer: (c) pleasure, reality. In the structural model (The Ego and the Id, 1923) the id demands immediate gratification on the pleasure principle, and the ego delays gratification until a realistic and safe way is found, on the reality principle. There is no "aggressive" or "sexual" principle (these are the id's drives), and (d) reverses the pair.

  9. Which of the following is considered a "Neurotic" level defense mechanism? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Splitting (b) Intellectualization (c) Sublimation (d) Projection

    Answer: (b) Intellectualization. In Vaillant's 1977 hierarchy intellectualisation is neurotic, with repression, displacement, reaction formation and dissociation. Sublimation is mature and projection immature; splitting is not in Vaillant's 1977 list, and later schemes place it below the neurotic level, so it is never the neurotic answer.

  10. Which group contains only immature defences in Vaillant's (1977) scheme? (NIMHANS 2026 recall)

    (a) Projection, acting out, passive aggression, hypochondriasis (b) Repression, displacement, reaction formation, intellectualization (c) Altruism, humour, sublimation, anticipation (d) Delusional projection, psychotic denial, distortion

    Answer: (a). Vaillant's immature level is projection, schizoid fantasy, hypochondriasis, passive aggression and acting out, normal in adolescence and typical in adults of personality disorders and substance misuse. Each distractor is a correct level, just the wrong one: (b) is neurotic, (c) mature and (d) psychotic.

  11. behavioural therapies applying learning principles to: (RML 2020)

    (a) identify and treat the underlying causes of dysfunction (b) improve learning and insight (c) eliminate the unwanted behaviour (d) improve communication and social sensitivity

    Answer: (c) eliminate the unwanted behaviour. On the behavioural model abnormal behaviour is learned and can be unlearned, so behaviour therapy aims at the behaviour itself, eliminating what is unwanted and building desirable behaviour in its place. Treating underlying causes is the psychodynamic aim, insight belongs to insight-oriented therapy, and communication and social sensitivity are the aims of encounter and sensitivity groups.

  12. Functional analysis is a therapy based on: (RML 2022)

    (a) Classical conditioning (b) Humanistic principles (c) Operant conditioning (d) Psychodynamic principles

    Answer: (c) Operant conditioning. Functional analysis, like behaviour analysis (asked in the same paper), rests on operant conditioning, in which behaviour that is reinforced is repeated: a child whose tantrums reliably win attention has more tantrums. Classical conditioning explains fears acquired by pairing, and humanistic and psychodynamic principles are not learning theories; the technique itself is taught in Chapter 4.

  13. Carol Gilligan departs from Kohlberg's theory on moral development by highlighting which of these differences? (RML 2022)

    (a) Males have ethics of care (b) Males have ethics of justice (c) Females have ethics care (d) Females have ethics of justice

    Answer: (c) Females have an ethic of care. In In a Different Voice (1982) Gilligan argued that Kohlberg's stages rewarded an abstract ethic of justice and rights and undervalued an ethic of care, rooted in relationships and responsibility to particular people, which she found more often in women's reasoning. The ethic of justice is what Kohlberg's scheme already rewarded, so it cannot be her departure, and (a) and (d) reverse her claim.

  14. The principle of nonmaleficence in ethical practice is BEST described as the obligation to: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Act in the best interest of the client and promote their welfare. (b) Respect the client's right to self-determination and make their own choices. (c) Be fair and impartial in the distribution of services and resources. (d) Refrain from causing harm or injury to the client.

    Answer: (d). Nonmaleficence, from the Latin for "not doing evil", is the obligation not to inflict harm, summed up as primum non nocere. The other options define the other three of Beauchamp and Childress's principles: (a) beneficence, (b) respect for autonomy and (c) justice.

  15. 'The ethical principle that research participants should be told enough about a piece of research to be able to make a decision about whether to participate in it.' Which research principle is this a glossary definition of? (RML 2021)

    (a) Informed consent. (b) Debriefing (c) Expectation Management. (d) De-concealment.

    Answer: (a) Informed consent. Informed consent comes before the research: participants are told its purpose, duration, procedures and risks and their right to decline or withdraw (Standard 8.02), and they agree voluntarily. Debriefing comes after the study is over, and "expectation management" and "de-concealment" are invented distractors.

  16. The principle of ethical research under which the subjects should be given full information about all aspects of the research study after it is done is known as: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Debriefing (b) Description (c) Explanation (d) Interpretation

    Answer: (a) Debriefing. After participation, participants are given full information about the nature, results and conclusions of the research and any misconceptions are corrected (Standard 8.08); after deception the debriefing must also dehoax and desensitise (Holmes, 1976). "Description", "explanation" and "interpretation" are ordinary words, not technical terms of research ethics.

  17. Which of the following best describes research ethics? (RML 2023)

    (a) Following legal regulations only (b) Ensuring confidentiality of data (c) Manipulating data to support hypotheses (d) Ignoring informed consent from participants

    Answer: (b) Ensuring confidentiality of data. Confidentiality protects participants' data throughout a study, and it is the only option that names an ethical duty. Law is only the floor (the Nuremberg Code, Helsinki, Belmont, the APA code and the ICMR guidelines all ask for more), manipulating data is falsification, one of the three forms of research misconduct, and ignoring informed consent breaks the first rule of research ethics.

  18. All of the following statements are TRUE about exceptions to the rule of confidentiality EXCEPT: (RML 2020)

    (a) a court order requires the release of information (b) the information is essential to safeguard the health of others (duty to protect; warn) (c) the information is probative of guilt or innocence in a criminal matter (d) there are never exceptions to the rule of confidentiality

    Answer: (d). Confidentiality has never been absolute: the APA code (Standard 4.05) and section 23 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 both allow disclosure without consent in defined situations. A court order, the duty to warn and protect others, and information that a court treats as probative of guilt or innocence are genuine exceptions, so the only false statement is that there are none.

  19. A dual relationship occurs when (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) a psychologist has both professional and personal relationships with a client. (b) two psychologists treat the same client collaboratively. (c) a client sees two therapists simultaneously. (d) a psychologist practices in two different clinic.

    Answer: (a). A dual or multiple relationship (Standard 3.05) exists when a psychologist in a professional role with a person also has another relationship with that person or with someone close to him. Two psychologists working together is co-therapy or team care, a client seeing two therapists at once is a separate issue under Standard 10.04, and practising in two clinics creates no second relationship with anyone.

  20. Which of the following is an example of a boundary violation? (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Maintaining professional rapport (b) Engaging in a romantic relationship with a current client (c) Providing therapy at the clients' workplace (d) Scheduling appointments at convenient times

    Answer: (b). In Gutheil and Gabbard's distinction (1993) a violation harms or exploits the client and serves the therapist's needs, and a romantic or sexual relationship with a current client is the clearest case; the APA code forbids it absolutely (Standard 10.05). A session at the client's workplace can be a legitimate boundary crossing, as in exposure work, and rapport and convenient appointments are simply good practice.

  21. The right to make an advance directive about mental health treatment is given by the: (Recall set)

    (a) Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 (b) Mental Health Act, 1987 (c) Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 (d) Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1992

    Answer: (a) Mental Healthcare Act, 2017. Section 5 lets every person who is not a minor write down how he wishes and does not wish to be treated and whom he appoints as nominated representative; the directive is registered with the Review Board and is invoked only when he loses capacity. The 1987 Act had no advance directives, the RPwD Act's tool is limited guardianship, and the RCI Act concerns the training and registration of professionals.

  22. According to the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 (MHCA 2017), which of the following statements accurately reflects the provision for the treatment of minors (persons under the age of 18)? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) A minor can be admitted to a mental health establishment only with the consent of the Mental Health Review Board. (b) The nominated representative of the minor has the right to make all decisions regarding the minor's treatment, irrespective of the minor's capacity. (c) A minor has the right to appoint a nominated representative, and their opinion on treatment must be considered in an age-appropriate manner. (d) A minor can never be admitted to a psychiatric hospital against their will, regardless of the parents' wishes.

    Answer: (c) as keyed, but the key is only half right. Section 14 gives the right to appoint a nominated representative only to "every person who is not a minor", and section 15 makes the legal guardian a minor's nominated representative; the true half is that the minor's wishes count, since admission must be in his best interests "taking into account the wishes of the minor if ascertainable" (section 87). Pick (c) as the best of the four, because the others are plainly wrong: the Board is informed within 72 hours rather than asked first, the representative must give particular credence to the minor's views and needs the Board's permission for ECT, and a minor can be admitted against his wishes on the guardian's application.

  23. Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT) without anesthesia is legally allowed only (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) In emergencies, with consent and under strict guidelines (b) Whenever requested by psychiatrist (c) For research purpose only (d) Never allowed under law

    Answer: (d) Never allowed under law. Section 95(1)(a) of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 lists "electro-convulsive therapy without the use of muscle relaxants and anaesthesia" among procedures that "shall not be performed on any person with mental illness", with no exception for consent, emergency or research. Option (a) is the trap: section 94(3) says that emergency treatment may never include ECT at all.

  24. Which of the following is a key legal provision under the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017? (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Suicide attempts are decriminalized, recognizing mental illness as a contributing factor (b) Electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) can be administered without consent in all cases (c) Only psychiatrists can provide therapy; psychologists are excluded (d) Mental health establishments are exempt from registration if government-run

    Answer: (a). Section 115 presumes, unless proved otherwise, that a person who attempts suicide has severe stress (the Act's word is stress, not mental illness) and shall not be tried and punished under section 309 of the Penal Code, which decriminalises attempted suicide in effect. ECT is never given "without consent in all cases", a registered clinical psychologist is a mental health professional, and section 65 requires every establishment, government-run or not, to be registered.

  25. The Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPwD) Act was passed in ____ and recognises ____ disabilities. (Recall set)

    (a) 1995; 7 (b) 2016; 19 (c) 2016; 21 (d) 2017; 21

    Answer: (c) 2016; 21. The RPwD Act received assent on 27 December 2016 and lists 21 specified disabilities, among them mental illness, autism spectrum disorder and specific learning disabilities. The distractors are real numbers misplaced: 1995 and 7 belong to the old PwD Act, 19 was the count in the Bill of 2014, and 2017 is when the Act came into force (19 April 2017), not when it was passed.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. The diathesis-stress model: disorder arises from the interaction of a diathesis (a biological, psychological or social predisposition) and a stress that triggers it; neither alone usually produces disorder, and the greater the vulnerability, the less stress it takes. Meehl (1962) gave the logic, Zubin and Spring (1977) the threshold of vulnerability. Options with "only", "alone" or "supernatural" are wrong. asked 5 times
  2. In Freud's structural model (The Ego and the Id, 1923) the id runs on the pleasure principle, the ego on the reality principle, and the superego has two parts, the conscience and the ego ideal. There is no aggressive or sexual principle; conscious and unconscious are regions of the topographic model. asked 4 times
  3. The Rehabilitation Council of India was a registered society from 1986 and became a statutory body under the RCI Act 1992 (in force 1993; amended 2000), under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. Section 13 of the RCI Act makes enrolment on the Central Rehabilitation Register compulsory for clinical psychologists; neither mental health Act creates the Register. asked 4 times
  4. Informed consent in research comes before participation: participants are told enough about the purpose, procedures, risks and their right to withdraw to decide, and agree voluntarily (Standard 8.02). The consent form is only its record; random assignment is a design procedure. asked 3 times
  5. Behaviour analysis and functional analysis rest on operant conditioning (reinforced behaviour is repeated), and token economies use secondary (conditioned) reinforcers. asked 3 times
  6. Lightner Witmer founded the first psychological clinic (University of Pennsylvania, 1896) and first used the term clinical psychology in print (1907, in his journal The Psychological Clinic). Wundt is the first laboratory (Leipzig, 1879), Hall the APA (1892), Cattell the "mental test" (1890). asked 3 times
  7. Vaillant's levels of defence (1977): psychotic (delusional projection, psychotic denial, distortion); immature (projection, schizoid fantasy, hypochondriasis, passive aggression, acting out); neurotic (intellectualisation, repression, displacement, reaction formation, dissociation); mature (sublimation, altruism, suppression, anticipation, humour). Splitting is never the neurotic answer. asked 2 times
  8. The APA Ethics Code (2002, amended 2010 and 2016) has five aspirational General Principles: A Beneficence and Nonmaleficence, B Fidelity and Responsibility, C Integrity (accuracy, honesty and truthfulness), D Justice, E Respect for People's Rights and Dignity. Equity is not one; the ten sections of Ethical Standards are the enforceable part. asked 3 times
  9. The advance directive (section 5) and the nominated representative (section 14) come from the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, not the Mental Health Act 1987, the RPwD Act 2016 or the RCI Act 1992. asked 2 times
  10. The biopsychosocial model was proposed by George L. Engel in Science in 1977 as an alternative to the biomedical model. Kraepelin, Bleuler, Beck, Freud, Cannon and Lazarus are the distractors. asked 2 times
  11. The four principles of Beauchamp and Childress (1979) are respect for autonomy, nonmaleficence (refrain from causing harm), beneficence (act for the client's welfare) and justice (fair distribution of services). Integrity is not one of them; Kitchener (1984) added fidelity. asked 2 times
  12. Trecker's principles of social group work include democratic group self-determination, flexible functional organisation and progressive programme experiences; "focusing on change and possibilities" is not one. asked once
  13. A diagnosis classifies a person's problems in a system such as ICD-11 or DSM-5-TR; a formulation is an individual hypothesis about their causes, triggers and maintaining factors. asked once
  14. Behavioural therapies apply learning principles to eliminate unwanted behaviour and build desirable behaviour; treating underlying causes is the psychodynamic aim. asked once
  15. Carol Gilligan (In a Different Voice, 1982) argued that Kohlberg's stages rewarded an ethic of justice and undervalued the ethic of care, which she found more often in women's reasoning. asked once
  16. Debriefing comes after participation: full information about the nature, results and conclusions of the research, with misconceptions corrected (Standard 8.08); after deception it must dehoax and desensitise. asked once
  17. Research ethics includes ensuring the confidentiality of data; manipulating data to fit hypotheses is falsification, and following the law alone is not enough. asked once
  18. Confidentiality is not absolute: a court order, the duty to warn and protect others, and information probative of guilt or innocence are exceptions, so "there are never exceptions" is false. asked once
  19. A dual (multiple) relationship is a professional role plus another relationship (personal, social, financial) with the same client or someone close to him (Standard 3.05); only those likely to impair objectivity or risk exploitation are forbidden. asked once
  20. A boundary violation harms or exploits the client to serve the therapist (a romantic relationship with a current client); a boundary crossing is benign, such as therapy at the client's workplace (Gutheil and Gabbard, 1993). asked once
  21. Under the MHCA 2017 only an adult may appoint a nominated representative (section 14); a minor's nominated representative is the legal guardian (section 15), and the minor's wishes must be taken into account (section 87). A 2024 key that gives minors the right to appoint is only half right. asked once
  22. ECT without muscle relaxants and anaesthesia is never allowed (MHCA 2017, section 95), emergency treatment may never include ECT (section 94), and ECT for a minor needs the guardian's consent and the Board's prior permission. asked once
  23. Section 115 of the MHCA 2017 presumes that a person who attempts suicide has severe stress and is not to be tried or punished, so attempted suicide is decriminalised in effect. asked once
  24. The RPwD Act 2016 lists 21 specified disabilities (benchmark disability 40 percent; in force 19 April 2017); the PwD Act 1995 had 7, and the 2014 Bill had 19. asked once
  25. The humanistic perspective rests on belief in human potential (self-actualisation), the availability of positive regard and a respectful therapist; availability of reinforcement is a behavioural idea. asked once
  26. The Indian Association of Clinical Psychologists was formed in 1968; NIMHANS was formed in 1974 and became an Institute of National Importance in 2012, and the first PsyD (Illinois, 1968) was American. asked once
  27. The Boulder conference (1949) adopted the scientist-practitioner model (PhD); Vail (1973) endorsed the practitioner-scholar model (PsyD). asked once
  28. The duty to warn and protect an identifiable third party comes from Tarasoff v. Regents of the University of California (1974 duty to warn; 1976 duty to protect); M'Naghten, Durham and Daubert are forensic rules. asked once
  29. Deontological ethics (Kant) judges an action by its intrinsic rightness and conformity to duty; utilitarianism (Bentham, Mill) judges it by its consequences, in a cost-benefit way. asked once
  30. Ethical competence is all of these: knowing and understanding the codes, recognising, analysing and resolving ethical situations, and taking responsibility for one's own actions. asked once
